Chapter 61: Naked Under Fluorescent Light, Which Is Somehow Worse



Monday, November 3, 1997: Sunnydale MHMR, Topanga Street, Sunnydale, Evening 7:12pm —

Okay. So. Here's where we're at.

One paper gown that ties in the back, which — whoever designed this thing, I hope you step on a Lego, every day, for the rest of your natural life, because this is not clothing, this is a dare. One gray blanket that smells like bleach and somebody's worst week. Zero shoes, because apparently the geniuses running this facility decided the real threat to national security was Cordelia Chase's cute flats, and okay, fine, credit where it's due, that's actually not the dumbest security call anyone's made this month, but I am going to be doing this barefoot on linoleum that has personally insulted my feet forty different times since Thursday and I want that on the record.

Camera on the ceiling. Fourteen-second cycle. I know this the way I used to know which lunch table not to sit at. Some skills you don't ask for, you just wake up one day fluent.

I'm cold. I'm going to be honest about that for exactly one sentence and then I'm never bringing it up again, because if I start listing everything that's wrong with tonight we will be here till 2004.

Randall Chase's daughter. That's who's lying on this bed being a good quiet non-threatening little psych patient. Not the girl who cried in the bathroom because Harmony said something bitchy about her hair sophomore year. My father walks into a room and in thirty seconds he has the exits mapped, the weak points flagged, and the names of the three people who matter. He taught me that before I taught myself literally anything else useful. Never once said "I love you" like a normal dad. Said "always know where the door is." Same thing, apparently, in Chase family.

I am going to have so many things to say to that man someday —

No. Not someday. There's no someday for that conversation, there hasn't been one since I was fourteen, and my brain just tried to schedule an argument with a dead man like he's going to walk through that door and take a number.

Great. Excellent brain. Really pulling its weight tonight.

Fine. Not tonight. Tonight I've got a bracket. Lower left crossbar. Somebody repaired it at some point with a fastener that is not up to code for a facility that specializes in, oh, I don't know, keeping people who might want to leave from leaving. Behind it, three inches of spring steel that isn't a knife and isn't a gun and is absolutely going to be something before this is over, because that's the whole plan — you use what you've got, not what you wish you had, which is a sentence I got from a JROTC assembly I slept through sophomore year and did not expect to become my personal religion.

Camera left.

I hold still. I have gotten disgustingly good at holding still. If someone handed me a medal for Olympic-level Not Moving I would wear it to prom.

Two rotations left before the fastener clears. I've been counting in my head like some kind of unhinged metronome since Thursday, one- Mississippi, two-Mississippi, and if you'd told me in September that by November I'd have a security camera's sweep pattern memorized better than my class schedule I would've had you committed, ha, very funny, very timely, save that one, somebody's going to appreciate the psych-ward joke from inside the actual psych ward eventually. Probably not tonight. Tonight the audience is me and a camera and God, and God's not saying much.

Walsh's people have been in twice since the escape attempt. Not for "sessions." For verification. Is the subject still horizontal. Is the subject still non-threatening. The subject has gotten extremely good at non-threatening in under two days, which either means I have a future in community theater or it means Walsh's people are not paying close enough attention, and I am rooting hard for door number two.

Pooka's late.

She's not late. She's exactly on schedule and I know that, I built the schedule, and I am still lying here with my heart trying to relocate to my throat every single time that vent doesn't light up violet on the first possible second it could.

She's a seven-inch Marine with two swords and more actual discipline than half the adults I've met this week. She's fine. Say it again. She's fine.

Camera left. Right on time. Story of my life, the camera's more reliable than everybody I used to trust.

Vent's dark.

Okay, don't. Don't do the thing where you fast-forward straight to the worst version. You don't have the seconds for it. You've got a bracket and a girl the size of my thumb somewhere inside a building full of guys with guns and a boss who's already gotten at least one person killed by a Terminator this month, so — no. Not doing it. Counting instead. Left. Right. Left.

Violet light.

I nearly cried from relief and I would like that struck from the record too, right next to the cold thing.

"Hi," she whispers. The careful whisper. The not-hysterical-anymore whisper of someone who found out exactly what hysterical costs you on Halloween and has decided never to pay that bill again if she can help it.

"Hi," I breathe back, eyes at half-mast, face doing its best sedated-and-boring impression. "You okay?"

"Uh huh. You okay?"

"Working on it." Camera's sweeping toward the door. Six seconds. "Xander—"

"He's okay." Fast. Like she already knew that was going to be question one, because of course it was, because it's always question one. "Hurt but okay. Captain Chief Mickey says Harris doesn't know how to be anything else."

God. Of course he doesn't. Nobody ever taught that boy the concept of lying down and staying down, and if I get him alone for ten consecutive minutes when this is over I am going to tell him exactly what I think of that, at length, possibly while checking his pulse with my own two hands just to be sure.

Camera's on the door. Eight seconds.

"Tell them I have a second plan," I say, fast, low, spending every word like it costs money I don't have. "First plan got me information. Tell them don't move before oh-six-hundred. I need till then. You got all that?"

"Duh," Pooka says, with the offended dignity of a decorated combat veteran being asked whether she can remember a sentence, and honestly? Fair. I deserved that.

I almost laugh. Actually laugh, out loud, in a psych ward, because apparently I am fiercely, stupidly fond of a pixie with two swords and zero patience for my nonsense, and if that's the last normal feeling I have room for tonight, fine, I'll take it.

"Tell them Walsh has a backup archive," I say instead of laughing. "Not Lowell House. Here. I've got the code." Camera's sweeping back. Four seconds. "That comes out with me. Tell Michaela specifically."

"What else."

Camera's on the bed. Eyes closed the rest of the way. Sedated. Resting. Excellent, boring, uninteresting data point, nothing to see here.

"Tell Xander," I say, in the half-second the mic's only picking up breathing, "I'll be home by morning."

The pause that comes back is longer than the others.

"He's gonna worry," Pooka says, and there's nothing tactical in those four words at all, just a very small person who watched a boy try to run on a bullet wound to get to this building, telling me the truth about what that means.

"Yeah," I say. "Tell him anyway."

The light goes out.

And I lie there in my twelve-by-nine box, in my paper gown, freezing, furious, doing math on a bolt instead of falling apart, thinking about a boy who apparently does hardware checks with his left hand and doesn't even know he's doing it anymore, and I think: I have a list. Seventeen things. I'm going to say every single one of them to his stupid, brave, currently-shot-twice face, and he is going to sit there and take it, because he owes me that much for making me plan an escape in a paper gown.

Camera left. Two rotations. One.

I close my hand slow around the edge of the blanket, where no camera on earth can see what my fingers are doing, and I start working that last bolt free.

Somewhere over my head, through eleven feet of ceiling and forty-eight hours of planning, a girl the size of my thumb is flying back through a vent to blow up a meeting that hasn't even started yet.

I don't know that part. I just know the bolt's almost loose, morning's still ten hours and forty-three minutes off, and I intend to spend every single one of them getting ready to walk out that door on my own two bare, ungrateful, freezing feet.

Camera right.

I smile in the dark, where that can't see it either.

---



Chapter 62: Seven and a Half Inches of Pure Attitude



Monday, November 3, 1997: Sunnydale MHMR, Topanga Street, Sunnydale, Evening 7:19pm —

Ventilation systems, Pooka Bell had decided somewhere around Wednesday, were the single greatest human invention nobody appreciated properly. Better than pizza. Almost. She'd have to think about that ranking more later, when she wasn't currently flying flat-out through eleven feet of galvanized steel duct at a speed that would've made her old drill instructor either extremely proud or extremely concerned, possibly both, he'd had a face that did both at once a lot.

The vent grille behind her was still humming from where she'd popped through it, which meant she had, generously, six seconds before the next camera sweep hit that section of hallway ceiling, and she intended to use every single one of those seconds being somewhere else entirely.

Left at the junction. Then down. Then the stretch that ran over the nurses' station, which was the fun part, because it was also the part where if she went too fast her wings buzzed loud enough that Nurse Denny — the one with the perpetually offended eyebrows, like the whole building was constantly letting her down personally — might glance up.

Pooka slowed down for that part. Barely. Just enough.

*She's fine,* she thought, meaning Cordelia, meaning the whole reason any of this mattered tonight. *She's fine and she's got a plan and plans are good, plans are the best, Sergeant Michaela is gonna eat that plan up like it's got sprinkles on it.*

She cleared the nurses' station stretch. Buzzed a hard right into the service shaft, which dropped four feet before it leveled out again, and enjoyed exactly one half-second of freefall the way she'd enjoy a good sneeze, sharp and satisfying and over too fast.

There was a hawk that lived somewhere near the parking structure. She knew this because it had tried to eat her on Thursday, which, rude, extremely rude, she was a Marine on a mission, not a snack, and she'd had Words about it at the time — mostly hand gestures, since talking takes longer than gestures and also the hawk didn't speak English — and every time she cut past that section of duct now she checked, out of what she considered professional vigilance and what a less generous observer might call a grudge.

No hawk tonight. Good. She had places to be.

The thing about the mission — the actual mission, not the fun parts, though the fun parts were also real, because both things were just true at the same time and always had been — was that Lady Cordelia had looked so still back there. Not scared-still. Not exactly. Working-still. The kind of still Pooka herself did right before a breach, where your whole body goes quiet because everything that matters is happening somewhere else, in your head, where nobody can see it.

She'd looked cold, too. Pooka had noticed that and hadn't said anything, because there wasn't a version of *I noticed you're cold* that helped anybody get out of that room faster, and helping was the whole job.

She thought about Xander lying in that hospital bed doing his own version of working-still, and she thought, not for the first time, that these two absolutely deserved each other, in the way that meant *good, actually,* the way you meant it about two people who were both going to drive each other completely up the wall for the rest of their natural lives and be delighted about it the entire time.

The main duct opened up ahead — the big one, the one that ran the length of the east wing and let her actually get some speed going instead of squeezing through joints built for airflow and not for seven-and-a-half-inch Marines with somewhere urgent to be.

She hit it at full throttle. Wings a blur. Two tiny swords riding quiet against her back where she'd strapped them, because you didn't fly a covert extraction with unsecured gear banging around, that was Basic, Chapter One, everybody knew that.

*Don't move before oh-six-hundred,* she recited to herself, locking it down the way she'd locked down every briefing she'd ever gotten, word for word, no wiggle room. *She's got a second plan. First plan got her information. Access code. Backup archive. Not Lowell House — here. Tell Michaela specifically.*

And the other part. The part that wasn't tactical at all, that she was going to carry the whole way back like something breakable held carefully in both hands.

*Tell Xander I'll be home by morning.*

Pooka had said *he's gonna worry* before she could stop herself, and Lady Cordelia had said *tell him anyway,* and something about the way she'd said it — steady, sure, like she'd already made her peace with the worrying and just needed him to have the information anyway — had made Pooka's chest do something complicated that she didn't have a tactical term for yet.

She cleared the last junction and dropped through the return vent into the stairwell shaft, which put her three floors and one very inconvenient elevator shaft away from the parking structure exit where Merrill was supposedly waiting with the van, assuming Merrill hadn't gotten Views about the ethics of waiting in a van outside a psychiatric facility, which, given his face lately, was a genuine possibility she was prepared to argue him out of.

*Focus. Mission. Message. Then pizza.*

Priorities. A soldier without priorities was just a person running around a duct system for no reason, and Pooka Bell was never, under any circumstances, a person running around for no reason.

She hit the exit vent going fast enough that the grille rattled, popped it with her shoulder, and dropped out into the cool November dark over the parking structure in a controlled little tumble that ended, as these things generally did lately, with both feet planted and her chin up like she'd meant to do that the whole time.

Somewhere above, a shape moved against the streetlight glow. Wings. Not hers.

Pooka looked up, found the hawk sitting smug on the light pole like it owned the whole parking structure, and gave it both hands, very deliberately, very seriously, the exact gesture she'd have gotten written up for back home and did not currently care even slightly about.

The hawk did not appear impressed.

"Yeah, well," Pooka muttered, already airborne again, angling toward the van, "you started it."

She had a message to deliver, a meeting to interrupt, and, God willing and if Joyce Summers had made any extra, a pizza waiting on the other end of all of it.

Mission first. But she was absolutely thinking about the pizza.

---



Chapter 63: Nine Hundred Years Is a Long Time to Not Know What a Shell Company Is



"Money has no memory, and no particular respect for how many centuries you've accumulated." — Mark Twain, attributed

Monday, November 3, 1997: Crawford Street, Sunnydale, Evening 7:24pm —

The ballroom ceiling had once been something. Frescoes, probably. California damp had been unkind to whatever it used to be, and what was left now was mostly the ghost of composition — shapes suggesting themselves in the mold the way a body suggests itself under a sheet.

Count Savros Hieronymus Blahdivastri stood in the middle of the ruined floor with his hands folded behind his back, looking up at it, and thought, not for the first time since Tuesday, that he had genuinely seen better ceilings.

He'd had a castle once. Actual castle. Actual frescoes, done by a man who understood proportion, back when proportion had been the entire point of a fresco and not something people apologized for wanting.

That castle had also, admittedly, had significantly less indoor plumbing, so. Trades.

"The plaster's coming down," Veronique said, from the doorway. She had her hands behind her back too, in the exact mirror of his own posture, which he'd have found irritating in anyone else and found, in her, oddly steadying. Eight hundred and forty-four years of practice, standing in ruins and deciding what came next. She was good at it. She'd always been good at it.

"Obviously," he said. "What's beneath it may be salvageable."

"It won't be." She walked further into the room, her footsteps exactly as loud as she'd chosen to make them, which tonight was almost silent. "I checked Tuesday night. Water damage goes down to the lath in most of the east half. We're not restoring a fresco, Savros. We're painting a new ceiling and telling people it's authentic, and in four hundred years somebody will believe us, because that is, historically, how this works."

He allowed himself the sound that passed, in this body, for a laugh. It came out smaller than it should have. He was still getting used to that — the insult of an eight-hundred-and-something-year-old sense of humor filtered through a sixteen-year-old's diaphragm.

"You've done this before," he said. It wasn't a question. He knew she had. He'd heard the stories, the ones she told and the ones other things told about her.

"I have restored six residences across four centuries," Veronique said. "None of them were Californian. All of them required convincing mortal authorities that a very old thing was, on paper, a very new thing." She turned in place, reading the room the way she read everything — assessing, filing, already three moves ahead. "The principle transfers. The specific instruments do not."

"Meaning?"

"Meaning," she said, "in 1887 I established residence in Prague using a marriage contract, a forged baptismal record, and the cooperation of a notary who owed my sire a debt he did not survive long enough to regret. None of those tools exist here. What exists here instead is something called a limited liability company, which Sunnydale's public library was extremely willing to explain to me in considerable and tedious detail this afternoon."

Blahd looked at her. "You went to a library."

"I went to a library," she confirmed, "and sat at a small table reading a pamphlet titled *Starting Your Small Business: A Guide,* while a librarian named Mrs. Castellan asked if I needed help finding anything, and I said no, thank you, I believe I have it, and she smiled at me the way adults smile at children who are pretending to understand something advanced, and I let her, because it was easier than the alternative conversation."

"The alternative conversation being."

"'No, madam, I do not require assistance, I have simply not needed to know what a limited liability company is because the last time I established a residence the primary legal instrument was a wax seal and a threat.'" She said it entirely flat, dry, and something in Blahd's chest that had gone quiet a very long time ago did the thing it did increasingly often around her lately, which he had also not fully worked out how to categorize.

"We require capital," he said, because that was the actual problem and the ceiling was not going to solve it by being looked at.

"We require capital," she agreed. "My accounts are, as of Tuesday, inaccessible. Two centuries of holdings, and I cannot produce so much as a document proving Veronica Daley has ever opened a bank account, because Veronica Daley was sixteen and had, as far as I can determine, an allowance and a library card."

"Mine are worse," Blahd said. "Freddy Iverson's proxy documentation named me — named Freddy — as executor of certain instruments that no longer exist in any accessible form, because the attorney who arranged them died in 1974 and his firm dissolved with him."

They stood in the ruined ballroom for a moment, two people who between them had personally witnessed the fall of at least one empire and the rise of several others, stymied by the concept of opening a checking account.

"There is," Veronique said slowly, "a sufficiency of old money in this city that does not answer to banks at all."

"Squisher's."

"Squisher's," she agreed. "And several individuals who owe debts of the sort that predate currency as we currently understand it. I spent Thursday evening there. A sufficiency of the patrons recognized what we are despite these—" a small, precise gesture at her own body, the sixteen-year-old shape of it, the school uniform she'd taken to wearing because it was, infuriatingly, the correct camouflage for this era — "despite this."

"And?"

"And several were extremely interested in the idea of old blood taking an interest in this town's affairs again," she said. "There has not been an established house here in decades. Just — appetite. Wandering appetite, with no structure, which is why this town is, forgive the observation, an embarrassment by any civilized vampiric standard."

Blahd looked at the ceiling again. At the ghost of the frescoes.

"A nightclub," he said, "requires capital before it produces capital. We require the former to obtain the latter."

"Squisher's would extend credit," Veronique said. "At interest. At very old interest, calculated the way interest used to be calculated, before usury laws made everyone soft about it."

"And in exchange for the credit?"

"Cooperation," she said. "Information sharing. A seat, eventually, at whatever passes in this era for the table where Sunnydale's supernatural economy actually gets discussed. Which brings us," she said, turning to look at him directly, "to the meeting."

"We have discussed the meeting."

"We have discussed *attending* the meeting. We have not discussed what we intend to say when we arrive." She folded her arms. "The Watcher will recognize us. He is old enough in his training, if not his years, to know precisely what we are the moment we walk through the door."

"And the American Captain."

"Michaela Reeves," Veronique said, "runs Black Company field operations with a ruthlessness I have observed and found impressive, which I do not say lightly. She will not be charmed. She will assess."

"So we give her something to assess."

"We give her everything," Veronique said. "Trask. The safe houses. The floor plan of Squisher's basement. All of it, freely, before she asks — because the alternative is being treated as a threat to be managed rather than a resource to be used, and I did not survive eight hundred years by choosing to be managed."

Blahd considered this. Considered her. The sixteen-year-old face with the nine-century weight sitting behind it, at ease with a decision most things twice her apparent age would have hedged on for a month.

"You want to see whether they're the kind of people who can be told the truth and use it correctly," he said.

"I have not frequently encountered such people," she said. "But I have encountered them. And when I have—"

"You remained."

"I remained." She looked at the door, at the ruined foyer beyond it, at the city outside doing the thing cities did after dark, ordinary and humming and entirely unaware of what was standing in this house deciding its future. "The meeting is at eight. We should not be late. First impressions, Savros."

"We were not invited."

"We were not *not* invited," Veronique said, already moving toward the door. "The distinction is going to matter a great deal, I suspect, before this century is out."

He looked at the ceiling one last time — at nine hundred years of better ones, filed away somewhere he no longer had access to — and followed her out into the dark.

---



Chapter 64: The Second Star to the Right, Rezoned



"All children, except one, grow up." — J.M. Barrie, Peter Pan

Monday, November 3, 1997: Sunnydale Amusement Park (abandoned), Evening 7:35pm —

The Ferris wheel didn't turn anymore, hadn't turned in the eleven years since the park closed, but forty feet up in its rusted gondolas somebody had strung Christmas lights that ran off a car battery, and from a distance on a clear night the whole skeletal wheel glowed faintly orange-gold, like something that had decided to keep being beautiful long after anyone had asked it to.

Peter Pan sat on the highest strut he could reach without the wheel groaning under him — he'd learned exactly how much groan meant something versus how much groan was just old metal complaining about the cold — and looked down at the midway, where approximately forty Lost Boys and several things that weren't boys at all were doing what they did every night, which was whatever they wanted, loudly.

He liked this park considerably more than he'd expected to like anything on this side of the portal. Neverland had never had a carousel. Neverland had crocodiles and a pirate with a hook where his hand should be and a very kind of forever that had started, somewhere in the last three days, to feel less like forever and more like a very long Tuesday.

"You're doing the thinking face," Wendy said, from the strut below him. She'd climbed up without being asked, the way she did most things now — she went where the interesting conversation was, and lately the interesting conversation kept happening to be wherever Peter had gone quiet and started looking at the horizon.

"I don't have a thinking face."

"You have several. This is the one where you're deciding whether something's a good idea before you've finished having the idea, which is backwards, and also very you." She settled against the strut, boots dangling over forty feet of nothing with the unconcern of somebody who used to fly and hadn't fully adjusted to not being able to anymore, except when the moon was doing something specific and the whole crew went up together for an hour like it was a scheduled thing, which, lately, it basically was.

"I want to talk to them," Peter said. "The Board."

"The ones who beat us."

"The ones who beat us," he agreed, without any rancor. Losing was a new experience for him and he'd discovered, to his own surprise, that he didn't hate it the way he'd expected to. Losing to something good was a different flavor of thing than losing to something merely stronger. "They didn't have to be careful with us. They were careful with us."

"The boy with the eyepatch broke Slightly's arm."

"Slightly bit him first."

"Slightly bites everyone first, that's not evidence of anything."

Below them, a Lost Boy who'd gone by Curly for approximately ninety years and by Kevin Marsh for one bewildering Halloween night before reverting was attempting to teach a fox — an actual fox, not a boy who'd been a fox, a genuine local fox that had wandered into camp three nights ago and simply never left — to fetch a stick, with a success rate of exactly zero and an enthusiasm level that suggested the failure rate was irrelevant to his enjoyment.

Near the dead carousel, two of the boys who weren't precisely boys — one of them had been, before October 31st, something with more teeth than the situation currently called for — were having an argument about whether the abandoned Tilt-A-Whirl could be made to spin using bicycle parts, an argument that had been ongoing for two days and showed no signs of theoretical or practical resolution.

It was, Peter reflected, a good camp. A good crew. Everyone fed, mostly, on what could be scavenged or occasionally what Wendy negotiated from a sympathetic grocer three blocks over who'd decided not to ask questions about the pack of feral children who paid in surprisingly good manners and exact change. Nobody dying. Nobody growing up, technically, though several of the boys had started doing things — reading books, asking about school, one of them had expressed a genuine interest in learning to drive — that Peter recognized, with a specific unease he didn't examine too closely, as symptoms.

"You want to ally with them," Wendy said. Not a question.

"I want to know what they're building." He looked out at the town beyond the park fence, at the lights of Sunnydale doing its ordinary evening thing, oblivious to how much had almost ended in a school library three days ago. "They're not soldiers. Not really. The tall one — Xander — fights like someone who learned it because he had to, not because he wanted to. And the girl. Cordelia." He said the name carefully, still getting used to names that belonged to this world instead of his. "She looked at all of us — at me — like we were a problem to be solved kindly rather than an enemy to be destroyed. Nobody's looked at me like that since—"

He stopped.

"Since Neverland," Wendy finished, quietly. "Since it was actually somewhere, instead of a thing you remember being."

Peter didn't answer that directly, because there wasn't a version of answering it that didn't hurt in a way he'd decided, three days in, not to make into a whole event.

"I want to offer them something," he said instead. "Not fealty. I'm not doing fealty again, I did enough of that as somebody's idea of a story." A beat. "But — cooperation. We know this town's underside better than they do already, after four days. We know where the things that come up out of the dark actually go. We're fast, we're small, half of us can go places their soldiers can't fit."

"And in exchange."

"In exchange we get to stop being a rumor the adults in this town are frightened of," Peter said. "We get to be something instead. A crew that matters, instead of forty runaways living in a dead carnival waiting for CPS or something worse to notice."

Wendy was quiet for a moment, looking down at Curly, who had, against all evidence and expectation, gotten the fox to sit, and was celebrating with the unrestrained joy of somebody who'd stopped counting his own age in the normal way a long time ago.

"You're scared," she said. Gently. Not an accusation.

"I'm reassessing," Peter said, with dignity.

"That's the thinking face for scared. I told you, you've got several."

He looked at her. At the steadiness she'd carried since the day she'd stopped being Wendy Halloway-for-one-night and started being just Wendy, permanently, the way he'd become just Peter. Two people who'd been handed the exact shape of who they'd always secretly wanted to be, and were now finding out that wanting a thing and living inside it forever were different weights entirely.

"Tomorrow," he said. "I'll go tomorrow. Alone — no, not alone, that's stupid, that's the kind of stupid that gets you shot at by people who've already had a very long week. I'll send word first. Ask for a meeting on their terms, not mine."

"Who do you send."

Below, on the far side of the midway, a very small light was moving through the dark at a speed and trajectory that had nothing to do with any of the local wildlife and everything to do with somebody who flew for a living.

Peter and Wendy both went still, tracking it.

It wasn't one of theirs.

"Huh," Peter said, watching the light bank hard around the dead Ferris wheel's base and angle straight up toward them with the unbothered confidence of somebody who had absolutely clocked exactly how high up they were and did not consider it a problem. "That's convenient."

"That's not one of our fairies," Wendy said.

"No," Peter agreed, standing, one hand finding the strut above him out of old flying-adjacent habit even though he couldn't fly anymore, not really, not without the whole crew and the moon cooperating. "That is somebody else's fairy entirely."

The light resolved, forty feet up, into seven and a half inches of extremely focused pixie, hovering at eye level with an expression that suggested she'd already fully briefed herself on exactly who she was talking to before she'd arrived, and had opinions.

"Peter Pan," Pooka Bell said. Crisp. Official. "Private First Scout Bell. I'm gonna need five minutes of your time, and also, does anybody down there have any food, because I've had a very long night and I am extremely hungry."

Peter looked at Wendy.

Wendy looked at Peter.

"Well," Wendy said. "That answers who you send."

---



Chapter 65: The Never-After Doesn't Keep Time the Way You Think It Does



"Time is what keeps everything from happening at once." — attributed to Ray Cummings

Somewhere in the Never-After, aboard the Windover, No time that translates cleanly —

The Windover didn't creak the way a ship built of ordinary wood was supposed to creak. It groaned lower, longer, in a register that lived somewhere under the water instead of above it, and Elise Nakamura had stopped flinching at the sound around day four, or what she'd decided to call day four, because there wasn't a sun in the Never-After to measure days by, only the ship's bell and the watches and the quality of exhaustion that told your body it was time to sleep whether or not anything outside your body agreed.

She was on deck-swab duty, which was the good duty, the duty you wanted, because deck-swab duty happened in the open air instead of belowdecks, and belowdecks was where the things that weren't duty happened.

Captain Ezekiel Hook — Chad Everette, before, though nobody on this ship used the before-name, including, increasingly, Chad Everette himself — walked the quarterdeck in the unhurried way he walked everything, a man who had all the time in a place that didn't measure time and had made his peace with that in a manner Elise found more frightening than any amount of shouting would have been.

He stopped near her. Didn't say anything at first. That was its own kind of pressure — the not-saying, the waiting to see what you'd fill the silence with, because filling it wrong cost you.

Elise kept swabbing.

"You're quick," Hook said, eventually. "Quicker than the Petrova girl was, this time last week."

Elise didn't ask what had happened to the Petrova girl. She'd learned that too, around day four. Some questions bought you information you couldn't afford to have.

"Admiral Landlorn's fleet took two prizes last night," Hook said, conversational, the way another man might discuss weather. "A trawler out of somewhere that doesn't exist anymore and something stranger that I'm told used to be a Spanish galleon before it wasn't anything at all for about three hundred years. Crew's being sorted." A pause, precisely timed. "Some of the sorted crew will come aboard here."

Elise kept her eyes on the deck. On the grain of wood under the brush.

"You'd know some of them, perhaps," Hook said. "This being where Sunnydale's overflow tends to wash up, one way or another. Funny thing about a town on top of a Hellmouth. Things fall through the cracks here that wouldn't fall through cracks anywhere else."

"I don't know anyone who's missing," Elise said. It came out steadier than she felt, which she counted as a small private victory.

"No," Hook agreed. "You wouldn't, would you. You've been keeping your head down very admirably." He crouched, unexpectedly, to her level, the hook that had replaced his hand catching the ship's lantern light in a way that made the shadow of it stretch long and wrong across the deck. "That's a skill. Keeping your head down. I respect it in a crew member. It's the ones who don't learn it who end up interesting to Lady Joy, and interesting to Lady Joy is not a condition I'd wish on you."

He stood. Walked on. Left the sentence sitting there the way he left most sentences — unfinished on purpose, so the finishing happened in your own head, where it did more work than anything he could have said outright.

Elise finished the section of deck she was on. Moved to the next. Did not look toward the companionway that led belowdecks, where Lady Joy kept the private quarters that everyone on the Windover knew about and nobody discussed above a whisper, because Lady Joy's interests ran to specific things that Elise had decided, around day two, she was never, under any circumstance, going to think about directly if she could help it. Thinking about it directly made it real in a way that surviving it required her not to let it be, at least not yet, not while there was still a version of this where she got out.

Because there was going to be a version where she got out.

She held onto that the way you held onto anything on a ship that groaned like something drowning — carefully, with both hands, not looking down.

---

Somewhere else in the Never-After, the flagship Retribution, Same non-time —

Admiral Landlorn did not visit the lower decks often. It wasn't beneath him, precisely — he'd been beneath worse things in worse centuries — but the lower decks of his own fleet held a specific smell of fear-sweat and salt and bilge that he found, at this stage of a very long unlife, simply tiresome rather than satisfying.

Tonight he made an exception, because tonight's haul included something his lieutenant had described, with the carefulness of a man reporting something he didn't fully understand, as *not from anywhere we've charted.*

The something was currently chained in the forward hold, and it did not look afraid, which was itself the interesting part. Landlorn had seen a great many prisoners over a great many centuries and fear was, invariably, the baseline. This one — young, human-shaped, dressed in clothes that belonged to no era Landlorn immediately recognized — was watching him with the calculating stillness of somebody doing math, the way a navigator does math, the way somebody plans a thing three moves ahead while their face stays perfectly, deliberately blank.

"You're not from the trawler," Landlorn said.

"No," the prisoner agreed. Male voice. Young. Unbothered in a way that was, Landlorn noted, either very brave or very stupid, and he'd learned the difference between those two things mattered less than people assumed. "I fell through something. About four days ago, your time, I think, though your time and my time don't seem to be the same thing at all."

"A door."

"A door," the prisoner confirmed. "One of yours. It opened somewhere it shouldn't have, and I was standing in the wrong place at the wrong moment, and here I am." He looked around the hold with the interest of somebody cataloguing an environment rather than fearing it. "This is Never-After. I've heard of it. Didn't believe in it, exactly, until about four days ago."

"You know what this place is and you're not afraid of it."

"I'm plenty afraid," the prisoner said. "I'm just also from a town where being afraid and doing the math anyway is basically the local hobby." A beat. "Sunnydale. You've probably heard of it, if doors keep opening near it."

Something in Landlorn's long-still chest did the thing that passed, in him, for genuine interest — a rare enough sensation after several centuries that he noted it specifically, filed it, decided it mattered.

"Sunnydale," he repeated.

"You've heard of it," the prisoner said. Not a question. Something that might, in a face less carefully arranged, have been the beginning of a plan settling into place.

"I have heard," Landlorn said slowly, "that there is presently a great deal of interesting activity in Sunnydale. More than usual, even for a Hellmouth." He crouched, the way Hook had crouched on the Windover in another part of the Never-After that did not know it shared this exact posture at this exact non-moment. "What's your name, boy."

"Marcus," the prisoner said. "Marcus Okafor." A pause, precisely as timed as anything Hook had ever managed. "And before you decide what I'm worth to you, Admiral — I should tell you I know things about that town's current situation that I don't think you've heard yet. And I'm the kind of person who trades information for things I want."

Landlorn looked at him for a long moment.

Then, for the first time in longer than he cared to calculate, he laughed — a low, delighted sound that hadn't come easily to him in centuries.

"Chain him somewhere with a chair," Landlorn told the guard. "And bring him something to eat. I find I'm curious."

---



Chapter 66: First Sergeant, Age Eleven, Bedtime Negotiable



"The heaviest thing a soldier carries isn't equipment. It's the moment they realize the numbers on the page were people." — Tim O'Brien, The Things They Carried, paraphrased

Monday, November 3, 1997: Sheridan Residence, Rosewood Court, Sunnydale, Evening 7:40pm —

Beverly Teresa Sheridan — First Sergeant Benjy Sheridan to the seventeen kids currently under her operational command, Bev to her parents, Benjy to everyone else since the second grade for reasons nobody fully remembered anymore — was sitting on her bedroom floor with a shoebox full of maintenance logs for the amusement park's electrical grid, cross-referencing generator output against the number of working Christmas lights on the Ferris wheel, because someone needed to and apparently that someone was her.

She was eleven.

She kept having to remind herself of that fact lately, the way you'd remind yourself of a phone number you didn't call often enough to have memorized. Eleven. Fifth grade. Two more years until middle school, assuming middle school was still a thing that applied to her, which she was increasingly unsure about.

"You've redone that math three times," Misty said, from the beanbag chair by the window, where she was field-stripping a wrist rocket with the unhurried competence of somebody who'd done it forty times this week alone and intended to be able to do it in the dark, blindfolded, with her hands half-frozen, because that was the kind of contingency Misty had started planning for without being asked.

"It keeps not coming out right."

"It's come out the same wrong three times in a row, Bev. That's not a math problem anymore." Misty still didn't look up from the wrist rocket. "That's you needing something to hold that isn't actually about the generators."

"Because it keeps not adding up." Benjy set down the shoebox. Looked at her hands, which were, she noticed with the distant clinical interest that had become a habit since Halloween, not shaking. They didn't shake anymore. They used to shake, a little, before tests, before anything that mattered. They'd stopped shaking somewhere around the point where she'd had to tell four kids to move left instead of right in a hallway full of things with too many teeth, and the four kids had lived because they'd listened, and she'd had approximately one second to be relieved about that before the next thing needed deciding.

"Bev."

"I'm fine."

"You said the math wasn't adding up like it's a personal insult." Misty set the wrist rocket parts down in the order she always set them down in — order mattered, Misty had explained once, with the seriousness of someone stating doctrine, because a rushed reassembly under pressure was how people lost fingers, and Misty Pantine, who three days ago had been the kind of girl who couldn't remember her locker combination, now thought in these terms constantly and without apparent effort. "It's a Ferris wheel generator. It's allowed to just be bad math."

"I know."

"So what's the actual thing."

Benjy looked at the shoebox. At the maintenance logs. At the eleven years of her life that had, up until October 31st, mostly consisted of soccer practice and a moderate ongoing rivalry with a girl named Cassidy over who got picked first in gym class, and which now consisted of this — logistics, casualty math she didn't want to be doing, a very specific and very permanent weight sitting somewhere behind her sternum that she'd started thinking of, privately, as the thing.

"Halloween," she said.

Misty went still. Not scared-still. Careful-still. The stillness of somebody who'd learned, fast, that when Benjy said the word Halloween in that specific flat voice, the next few minutes required actual attention rather than the reflexive kind.

"What about it."

"I keep running it back," Benjy said. "The hallway. The four of them. I told them left." She looked at her hands again. Still not shaking. "What if I'd told them right."

"You didn't."

"That's not the point—"

"It's exactly the point," Misty said, setting the wrist rocket aside now, giving this her full attention in a way that would have been completely uncharacteristic of the girl she'd been two weeks ago, and was now simply how she operated. "You told them left because left was correct. You'd mapped the hallway. You knew where the thing behind us was faster than any of us did, because you'd been watching it the whole fight instead of just fighting it, which is a thing exactly none of us knew how to do before Wednesday."

"I got lucky."

"You got right," Misty said. "Those aren't the same word, and you know they're not the same word, and I think you know it and you're choosing the scarier word anyway because the scarier word means you get to keep worrying, and worrying feels like doing something, even when it's not."

Benjy looked at her.

Eleven years old. Both of them. Misty Pantine, who six months ago had been unable to find her way to the front office without a hall pass and a friend to walk with, now sitting cross-legged on a beanbag chair explaining the psychology of command anxiety with the calm precision of somebody twenty years older, because that was apparently also a thing Halloween had done to her, quieter than the wrist rockets, easy to miss if you weren't looking for it.

"When did you get smart," Benjy said. It came out more raw than she meant it to.

"I was always smart," Misty said. "I was just spending it all on figuring out how to seem like I wasn't, because seeming smart in fifth grade gets you eaten alive in a different way than the literal kind." She picked the wrist rocket back up. Started reassembling it, unhurried. "Now there's actual things trying to eat me the literal way, so. Reprioritized."

Benjy almost laughed. It came out as something closer to a real laugh than she'd managed in three days, small and surprised and genuine.

"The meeting's at eight," she said.

"I know."

"I have to give Captain Reeves the casualty numbers. The actual ones. Not the version where I say we're fine."

"You are fine," Misty said. "Fine doesn't mean nothing happened. It means you're still standing up afterward and figuring out the next thing." She finished the reassembly, checked the tension, set it in her lap with the satisfaction of a job done right. "You want me to come with you to the meeting."

"Yeah," Benjy said, quiet. "Yeah, I do."

Downstairs, Michael Sheridan called up that dinner was ready, in the careful voice both her parents had started using since Halloween — not scared exactly, but working very hard not to look directly at the thing that had happened to their daughter, the way you didn't look directly at the sun, because looking at it fully might mean having to say something neither of them had found the words for yet.

Benjy stood. Picked up the shoebox.

Her father was at the table when she came down, and something in the set of his shoulders was different tonight — less careful, more direct, the shift she'd learned to read over eleven years as the difference between Dad-who-worries and the other one, the one who used to come back from things he never fully talked about and would, for a day or two afterward, move through the house checking the locks twice without seeming to notice he was doing it.

"Casualty numbers," he said, without preamble, once Michelle had gone to get the bread. "You're taking them to the meeting tonight."

"Yeah."

"Real ones. Not the soft version."

"Yeah."

Michael Sheridan nodded once, and stood up from the table instead of sitting back down to it, which wasn't what Benjy had expected at all.

"Come here a second," he said. "Bring the shoebox."

He cleared a space on the kitchen counter and had her lay the maintenance logs out flat, the way she'd have laid out a map, and started going through them with her — not talking about fear, not talking about the meeting at all, just working the actual numbers alongside her, checking her math the way he'd have checked a rigger's inspection before a jump, silent and thorough.

"You know what they told us, first jump school," he said, eventually, still checking a column of figures. "Nobody in that plane is less scared than you are. The guy who looks calm just learned to keep his hands busy while he's scared. That's the whole trick. Not being unscared. Just not letting the fear be the one doing your job for you." He tapped the shoebox. "This is you keeping your hands busy, Bev. Numbers, logs, a job to do instead of just standing at the door waiting to jump. Nothing wrong with that. I did the same thing my whole career and so did every good jumpmaster I ever served under."

"Did it get easier?"

"No," he said, flat, honest, the way she'd learned this year that flat honest was worth more than careful. "You just get more practice doing it scared. Static line doesn't care how you feel about the door. You clip in, you check the man in front of you, and when the light goes green, you go. That's the whole job, every time, forever. Feeling ready's got nothing to do with it."

He closed the last log, satisfied the numbers actually held, and set the shoebox back in her hands.

"Numbers are good," he said. "Go eat something before you present them. Command decisions land better on a full stomach — learned that one the hard way, more than once."

"Thanks, Dad."

"Anytime." He didn't hug her, didn't make it a moment — just clapped her once on the shoulder, the same brief, solid contact he'd have given a soldier heading out the door, and went back to his coffee like the whole exchange had been nothing more than checking a chute.

"Hey," Misty said, at the door. "For what it's worth. Left was right."

Benjy looked at her for a second.

"I know," she said, and found, a little to her own surprise, that this time she actually believed it.

---



Chapter 67: Wardrobe Consultation With a Non-Human Party



"Clothes make the man. Naked people have little or no influence on society." — Mark Twain

Monday, November 3, 1997: 1630 Revello Drive, Sunnydale, Evening 7:45pm —

Buffy Summers had changed shirts four times, which was three more times than any shirt decision in the history of shirt decisions had ever required, and she knew this, and she kept doing it anyway, because the alternative was sitting still with the actual thing she didn't want to sit still with.

"Not that one either," 'Kat said, from the bed, where she'd arranged herself in the boneless way that should have been anatomically impossible and clearly wasn't, one October-blue eye and one October-green eye tracking Buffy's every move with the patient, faintly amused attention of somebody who had clearly done this before, in some other body, some other century, and found it perpetually entertaining.

"What's wrong with this one?"

"Nothing's wrong with it. It's a lovely shirt. It says 'I am here to have a fun, low-stakes evening,' which is not the message you're going for." 'Kat's tail flicked once, thoughtfully. "You're meeting military people tonight. You want the shirt that says 'I could end you and I have somewhere to be after.'"

"That's a very specific message for a shirt to send."

"Clothes are just information you put on your body," 'Kat said, with the flat certainty of a fact so obvious it barely needed stating. "You've been sending information your whole life. You just weren't doing it on purpose."

Buffy looked at the closet. At the chaos of a wardrobe built for a girl whose biggest wardrobe crisis, one year ago, had been whether a skirt read as trying-too-hard for a first date. That girl felt very far away right now, further away than one year should reasonably make anything feel.

"The dark blue," 'Kat said. "The one in the back you never wear because you think it's too plain."

"How do you even know which one I never wear?"

"I sleep in this room sometimes," 'Kat said, unbothered by how that sentence sounded. "You'd be amazed what you learn by paying attention to a closet."

Buffy pulled out the dark blue. Held it up. It was, annoyingly, exactly right — not flashy, not trying, just clean and dark and the kind of thing that made you look like you'd already decided you belonged in whatever room you were about to walk into.

"You've changed four times," 'Kat said, watching her put it on. "Chessie's timing you. She says this is a record."

"Chessie needs a hobby that isn't timing me."

"Chessie's hobby is timing things. It's very fulfilling for her. Don't take it from her." 'Kat rolled onto her back, all four paws in the air, the universal posture of a creature completely at ease in a house that had, three days ago, been a stranger's house to her. "You're worried about the meeting."

"I'm not worried."

"You've changed four times."

"I'm being thorough."

'Kat looked at her — actually looked, the kind of look that came with the weight of something older than the small body it was currently operating out of. "You're worried because there's about to be a room full of very competent adults who know things you don't, and you're used to being the most competent person in any room you walk into. The Slayer doesn't usually have to share the floor."

Buffy went still, halfway through buttoning the shirt.

"That's not—" she started, and then stopped, because it was, actually, exactly that, and there wasn't a version of arguing the point that wasn't just her being defensive about something true.

"It's a reasonable thing to feel," 'Kat said, gently, for her. "You've spent two years being the person who handles it. Whatever it is. And now there's a whole crew of people who handle things too, and some of them handle things you don't even have a category for, and that's — it's an adjustment. It's allowed to be an adjustment."

Buffy finished the button. Sat down on the edge of the bed, next to the cat girl who fit into this room and this house like she'd always been meant to be here, the way none of them had actually planned for but all of them had somehow ended up building space for anyway.

"I keep thinking," Buffy said, quiet, "that I should've been more useful. On Halloween. Everybody else got — I don't know. Upgraded. Complicated. Interesting. I got picked up by a fifteen-foot gorilla and carried to the top of a water tower, where he spent two hours grooming me for fleas while a news helicopter got the whole thing on tape. That's it. That's my Halloween. I didn't fight, I didn't save anyone, I just sat up there next to King Kong and waved at Channel Nine like an idiot because what else are you supposed to do." She gestured, vaguely, at the general concept of Seven of Nine and Stephanie Rogers and a former Terminator sitting quietly in a school library thinking new thoughts. "And everyone else came out the other side with this whole — thing. And I'm just still me. Still me, and apparently a five-second clip on the local news that Cordelia is never, ever going to let me forget."

'Kat considered this with the seriousness it deserved.

"You weren't even there for it," she said, quietly. "Not the way everyone else was there for theirs. Xander still has Hicks. Seven still has everything the Borg ever gave her. Jonathan's carrying a whole dead man's war around behind his own eyes, every day, whether he wants to or not. But you — you went all the way under. You were Cinderella, completely, the whole night. You don't even get to claim the part where you didn't panic, because that wasn't you making a choice. That was a fairy tale believing it lived in an enchanted kingdom, patting a gorilla's hand like it was the most natural thing in the world."

"That's not making me feel better."

"I'm not finished." 'Kat's tail moved, slow, thoughtful. "Everyone else got handed something extra and had to learn how to carry it. You got erased for one night, completely, and came back the next morning exactly yourself again — not augmented, not haunted, not carrying anyone else's memories around. Just you. Which means whatever's actually driving you through every fight since Halloween isn't leftover magic from a costume. It's just you. Underneath, unassisted, the whole time." She looked at Buffy steadily. "I think that might scare you more than being ordinary would. Because ordinary would be an excuse." "Most competence looks like having no other options, right up until you watch what somebody does with them," 'Kat said. "You had one option. You picked it well. Some people get handed a lifetime of borrowed memory and it still takes them days to stop flinching at doors. You got picked up by a gorilla and you were composed enough to wave at a news camera within the hour. I know which of those I'd rather have covering my back."

"That sounds like something you're supposed to say."

"I'm not supposed to say anything," 'Kat said. "I say what's true because saying what's true is more interesting than saying what's polite, and I've had a very long existence of people saying polite things at me and I find I have no patience left for it." She stretched, extravagantly, and hopped off the bed. "Blue was correct, by the way. You look like someone who could end a small war and still make it home for dinner."

"That's oddly specific."

"I contain multitudes," 'Kat said, already halfway to the door, tail doing something complicated and satisfied. "Also Chessie says you beat her record by ninety seconds, and now she's going to be insufferable about the stopwatch for a week, so, you're welcome for that too."

She was gone before Buffy could figure out how to respond to any of that.

Downstairs, something in the kitchen smelled like Joyce was solving several problems simultaneously through the medium of roasted chicken, and Devila's cartoon was still producing enthusiastic small explosions from the den, and the house held all of it — the ordinary and the impossible, side by side, without apparent strain — the way it had been holding everything since October 31st, because that was, Buffy was starting to understand, simply what this house did now.

She looked at herself in the mirror. Dark blue. Steady.

Not the most competent person in the room, maybe. Not anymore, not automatically, not by default.

But still exactly the person who'd looked a fifteen-foot gorilla in the eye, gotten carried off against her will, and come out the other side of it steady enough to wave at a news helicopter instead of falling apart.

That was going to have to be enough for tonight, and — she thought, straightening, heading for the door — it probably actually was.

---



Chapter 68: The Gap Column



"Intelligence work is ninety percent noticing what's ordinary and ten percent noticing what isn't. The ratio never changes. Only the stakes do." — Murphy's Laws of Combat

Monday, November 3, 1997: Levinson Residence, Paden Street, Sunnydale, Evening 7:50pm —

The thing nobody warned you about, when a war hero's entire combat memory got installed in your head via a Halloween costume malfunction, was the quality of quiet that came afterward. Not peace. Jonathan Levinson had stopped expecting peace somewhere around day two. Just quiet — the kind where you sat very still and watched the exits without deciding to, because some part of you that wasn't entirely you anymore had decided the exits mattered more than whatever conversation was happening in the room.

"You're doing it again," Tamara said, not looking up from the legal pad.

"Exits," Jonathan said, which had become shorthand between them for the whole phenomenon, three syllables standing in for *sorry, that wasn't me, or it was me, I can't always tell anymore.*

"Specifically the window this time, or the door?"

"Both. Window's got a fire escape landing. Door's got a straight shot to the stairs." He heard himself say it in the flat cataloguing voice that wasn't his voice, not originally, and made himself stop. Made his shoulders drop half an inch, which Tamara had taught him was the tell — when his shoulders came up, the residue was driving, and when he noticed and dropped them, he was back.

Tamara had learned all his tells in about four days flat, which said something about her that Jonathan hadn't fully worked out how to categorize yet, except that it was good, whatever it was, and he was grateful for it in a way that embarrassed him slightly every time he thought about saying so.

"Gap column," she said, tapping the legal pad. Three columns: TX BEHAVIOR. BEHAVIORAL BASELINE (Harmony). GAP. The gap column was the interesting one. The gap column was where Jonathan's actual brain — the part that was just his, unaugmented, Sunnydale-High-social-nothing Jonathan Levinson — did work that the borrowed combat instincts couldn't touch.

"The TX is running Harmony's social architecture," he said. "Competently. Better than competently — it's had three days and full access to everything Harmony ever knew, every calibration, every reflex." He tapped the second column. "But there's a lag. A processing lag before it executes anything socially calibrated. Harmony didn't have that lag. Harmony was mean the way some people are cold — it wasn't a decision, it just happened before she'd thought about it."

"And the TX thinks first."

"Every time." Jonathan looked at the gap column, at the pattern he'd been building for three days now, cross-referenced against Giles's nightly notes. "It's not faking human. It's approximating human, competently, from the outside in. That's not the same thing as being human, and I don't think it thinks it's the same thing either."

Tamara wrote that down. Her handwriting was better than his — looped, confident, the handwriting of somebody who'd never once doubted a thought was worth committing to paper, which Jonathan found, honestly, kind of remarkable, given that six months ago she'd have said the same thing about him and been catastrophically wrong.

"You found something else today," she said. Not a question. She'd learned to read him almost as well as she'd learned to read the TX reports.

"Sightlines." He flipped back two pages in his own notebook, found the sketch he'd made that afternoon, the rough floor plan of Sunnydale High with three positions marked in red. "I walked the building at lunch. There are exactly three spots where you can see both the library entrance and the main hallway doors at once. I checked, and Harmony never used any of them — she didn't think in sightlines, she thought in social geography, who's-watching-me-and-do-they-matter."

"And the TX has been standing in them."

"According to Giles's notes, yes. Repeatedly." Jonathan looked at the third column. GAP. "That's not social behavior. That's tactical behavior. Which means whatever's running behind Harmony's face has been doing threat assessment on its own containment environment for three days straight, which either means—"

"It's planning an escape," Tamara said.

"Or it's protecting something," Jonathan said. "And I don't know which, and I think that not-knowing is the actual thing I need to bring to Giles tonight, because everybody keeps asking me for a conclusion and the honest answer is I've got a gap and I don't have enough data to close it yet."

Tamara was quiet for a moment, looking at the sketch, at the three red marks, at the accumulated evidence of a Terminator quietly, patiently, thinking about a school library for three days straight.

"Audie Murphy never got an after," she said, carefully, like she was testing the shape of the thought before committing to saying the rest of it. "Whatever's in your head stops on a burning tank destroyer with half his platoon down and Germans still coming. No homecoming. No years afterward learning to stop flinching at doors, because that memory doesn't include ever making it out the other side." She looked up. "What if that's what you're actually looking at, in the TX. Not a machine building an escape route. Something that also doesn't have an 'after' to reference — that ran a mission, and the mission stopped, and it has no template at all for what happens once you're just supposed to be safe. So it keeps watching exits. Not because it's planning to use one. Because it doesn't know what standing down is even supposed to feel like."

Jonathan sat with that for a long moment.

The residue in him — the part that was Audie Murphy's, the part that had come home decorated and broken and had spent thirty years trying to be a person again in a body that kept flinching at doors — recognized something in what she'd just said with a clarity that had nothing to do with tactics at all.

"You're better at this than me," he said, quiet, meaning it entirely. "The reading-people part. I've got the tactical read down cold. You've got the actual person underneath it, every time, and I don't know how you do it."

"Practice," Tamara said, and something in her expression shifted, warmer, less about the gap column now. "Four days of learning your tells, remember. Turns out once you've figured out somebody's shoulders, the rest of them gets considerably easier to read too."

"Is that a compliment or a warning."

"Depends how you behave the rest of the evening," Tamara said, mild, not looking up from the legal pad, though the corner of her mouth was doing something that suggested she knew exactly what she'd just done. Jonathan felt his own ears go warm and elected, wisely, not to comment on it.

"That's not in the reports," he said, quiet.

"No," Tamara agreed. "It's not data. It's just — a guess. About what it's like to be a thing that used to only know how to be dangerous, and is trying to figure out how to be something else, in a room everyone's watching."

Jonathan looked at the gap column. At the space between what the TX did and what Harmony would have done, which had felt, until thirty seconds ago, like a tactical puzzle and now felt like something considerably more like a mirror.

"I need to write that down," he said. "Not for the report. For — I don't know. For me."

"Write it down for the report too," Tamara said, gently. "Giles should hear it. Michaela's people should hear it. Everybody keeps treating the TX like a math problem, and maybe it is one, but maybe it's also just—" she shrugged, a small, careful gesture, "—somebody who came back from something, the way you did, and is trying to figure out who that makes them now."

Jonathan looked at her.

"When did you get good at this," he said.

"I've always been good at this," Tamara said, echoing something she couldn't have known Misty had said in almost the same words three blocks away, in the way that this town's kids had all apparently gotten quietly, terrifyingly competent in the same three-day window, each in their own direction. "I just didn't have anything worth being good at it for, before."

Jonathan closed the sketch notebook. Checked his watch. Forty minutes to the meeting.

"Eight o'clock," he said.

"Eight o'clock," Tamara agreed, already gathering the legal pad, the sketches, the whole careful architecture of the gap column, ready to carry it into a room full of adults who were, Jonathan had started to suspect, going to need this specific insight a great deal more than they currently understood.

---



Chapter 69: Patents



"Any sufficiently advanced technology is indistinguishable from magic." — Arthur C. Clarke

Monday, November 3, 1997: Mears Residence, Guava Drive, Sunnydale, Evening 7:55pm —

"The meeting's at eight," Warren said, for the third time in as many minutes, which he was aware made him sound like a nervous parent and not like a seventeen-year-old boy who two weeks ago had been mostly known for a moderately successful robotics project and an unmoderately unsuccessful attempt to ask Buffy Summers to homecoming.

"I am aware of the time," Seven said, not turning from the terminal. The secondary monitor she'd requisitioned — commandeered, really, though she preferred the word requisitioned, which she'd explained was more accurate to the transaction even if less accurate to how Warren had actually experienced losing his good monitor — threw pale blue light across the side of her face, across the faint silver line at her temple that had been, before Halloween, just a scar from a bike accident in second grade, and was now something more complicated.

"You said that four minutes ago."

"The statement remains accurate. Repetition does not diminish accuracy." She adjusted something on the schematic, small, precise, the kind of correction Warren had learned to recognize as *satisfied* even though her face didn't do anything a normal person would call satisfied. "The regeneration module design is at seventy-eight percent completion. I would prefer to reach a stable checkpoint before we leave."

Warren looked at the back of her head. At the architecture of her — not costume anymore, hadn't been costume in three days, just her, the way his own borrowed genius had already stopped feeling like somebody else's clothes and started just feeling like the shape his own thinking now took.

This should have been the strangest relationship of his entire life.

It was, objectively, the strangest relationship of his entire life.

It was also, and this was the part he still hadn't found anyone he could say out loud to, the first time in seventeen years that Warren Mears had ever felt like the things he built actually mattered to someone in the room while he was building them, instead of mattering only after, in the abstract, to a science fair judge or a college application.

"Seven."

"Yes."

"Are you okay? Actually okay. Not the readout version." He'd learned to ask it that specific way, because the first six times he'd just said *are you okay* she'd given him a diagnostic-accurate answer about implant integrity percentages that told him everything about her hardware and nothing about her.

She paused. The pause itself was data, he'd learned — Seven didn't pause for effect, she paused when a question required processing that didn't map cleanly onto anything she already had a template for.

"I am uncertain how to answer that question in the terms you're asking it," she said, finally. "I have been assimilated. I have been de-assimilated, incompletely, into a body that is not the one I was assimilated in. I am currently regenerating implant function using nineteen-nineties consumer electronics and Dr. Soong's positronic theory, which is, on any rational assessment, an absurd combination of tools for the task." She turned, finally, the full weight of her attention landing on him, both the optical implant and the plain brown human eye beside it tracking him with the same intensity. "And I find that I do not experience this as absurd. I experience it as — functional. Correct. I do not have a framework for why a situation this objectively strange feels correct."

Warren sat with that for a second.

"I think that's just what it feels like when something's actually working," he said. "Even if it doesn't look like it should, from outside."

Something in her expression did the very slight adjustment that was, he'd learned, her version of considering a thing seriously.

"The Collective did not have this quality," she said. "Efficiency, in the Collective, felt correct because it was optimal by design. This—" she gestured, a small precise motion that took in the room, the terminal, him, "—is not optimal by any design I originally understood. And it still feels correct. I have been attempting to reconcile those two data points for several days."

"Maybe it's not a design thing," Warren said. "Maybe it's just — you picked it. Nobody assigned you this. You picked the regeneration module, you picked the workaround, you picked—" he stopped himself right before the sentence finished walking somewhere he hadn't fully decided he was ready to say out loud yet.

Seven looked at him for a long moment.

"You may finish that sentence," she said. "I am aware of what the end of it likely was. I would prefer accurate data to omitted data."

"You picked me," Warren said, and felt his ears do the thing they did, the thing that had nothing to do with Dr. Soong's borrowed composure and everything to do with being seventeen and saying a true thing out loud for possibly the first time in his life.

"Yes," Seven said, without hesitation, in the flat factual register she used for things that were simply, uncomplicatedly true. "I did. The decision was not efficient by Collective standards. It has proven, empirically, to be correct by standards I am still developing a framework for." A pause. "I would make the same selection again, with full knowledge of the variables involved."

Warren looked at her.

"That's — really nice, actually. In a very Seven way."

"I am pleased it registered as such," Seven said, and turned back to the terminal, apparently considering the conversation's data fully exchanged, though the corner of her mouth did the very slight thing that Warren had learned, patiently, over three days, to recognize as the closest thing to a smile her particular architecture currently permitted.

"The relay bypass," she said, all business again, sliding the pad across to him. "Assess the junction at position four."

Warren looked at the diagram. Felt the thing that wasn't quite his knowledge and wasn't quite not his knowledge surface, the way it always did, quiet and certain. "Move the T-connector fourteen millimeters left. You'll get feedback resonance at sustained load otherwise."

"I calculated the same correction," Seven said. "I wanted independent confirmation."

"You always want independent confirmation."

"Independent confirmation is how errors are caught before they become failures," Seven said, already making the adjustment without looking at it, muscle memory from implants that predated this body by decades. "It is not inefficiency. It is due diligence."

"Sure," Warren said, smiling slightly, in a way he was getting steadily less embarrassed about doing in front of her. "Due diligence."

She glanced at him sideways. "You are amused."

"A little."

"That is acceptable," Seven said, and shut down the secondary monitor with the finality of somebody closing exactly one chapter of a much longer document. "We should go. Ninety seconds early, as previously calculated."

"Ninety seconds early," Warren agreed, and grabbed his jacket, and followed her out into the cold November evening toward a meeting that neither of them fully knew yet was about to get stranger, faster, than either of them currently had a framework for.

---



Chapter 70: All the King's Men



"All the king's horses and all the king's men couldn't put Humpty together again — but they could, at least, all show up to try." — traditional nursery rhyme, adapted

Monday, November 3, 1997: South Marion Drive, Sunnydale Medical Complex, Conference Room B, Sunnydale, Evening 7:58pm —

The conference room smelled like industrial solvent and fluorescent futility, and no amount of Joyce Summers's garlic bread was going to fully fix that, though it was, Michaela had to admit, putting up a genuinely heroic fight.

She'd counted heads twice already, out of habit more than necessity. Giles and Zabuto, arrived nine minutes ago, Zabuto going around the room in one slow professional sweep the moment he walked in, cataloguing exits and threats with a Watcher's trained thoroughness that Michaela recognized because she did the same thing herself, just faster and with better posture. Joyce, setting the garlic bread down next to Aura's covered dish with the calm of a woman who'd stopped being surprised by anything three days ago and had decided the only sane response left was to keep feeding people. Dawn, one step behind, notebook already open, pen already moving.

Brockhurst had found coffee somewhere in a building that had no business producing coffee this good, and handed her a cup without ceremony. "Don't get used to it, Army," he said, mild. "Zoomie hospitality's a limited-time offer."

"Careful, Colonel," Michaela said, taking it anyway. "Keep being useful and people might start mistaking you for competent."

"Wound me," Brockhurst said, unwounded, and went back to his tablet.

Cheng had the far corner, laptop open, running comms checks on the extraction team's frequencies with the economy of a man who'd done this in worse rooms than a hospital conference space, against stranger things than whatever tonight turned out to require, and had stopped, some years back, seeing much daylight between the two categories.

"Boss," Buckley's voice came in low over her earpiece, flat, no wasted syllables, from the mobile command post two blocks out. "Sitrep."

"Full house, minus the two currently extracting," Michaela said, thumb on the mic. "Cheng's got comms."

*"Copy. We clear on the vampire and the exchange student who isn't a vampire."*

"We're clear."

*"And the eleven-year-old running her own casualty reports like a company clerk."*

"Also clear."

A pause, exactly as long as it took a man who'd personally walked out of two things that shouldn't have been survivable to decide whether a joke was worth the airtime. *"Copy. Wouldn't have made it into the brief if I hadn't seen weirder. Buckley out."*

Cheng, without looking up from the laptop, allowed himself the smallest possible smile — the rationed amusement of a man who'd learned, a long time ago, exactly how much humor a comms channel could afford before it started costing you something.

"Disposition," she said, to the room.

"Moseby's in the corridor. Merrill's doing a final sweep of the loading dock." Brockhurst checked his tablet. "Bell's with the MPs, coordinating something she's being cagey about."

"Cagey how."

"Cagey in the way that means she's already done the thing and is waiting for the right moment to mention it," Brockhurst said, mildly, and Michaela decided she didn't have the bandwidth tonight to chase that thread down before the meeting started.

The door opened. Buffy came in first, dark blue, steadier than Michaela had seen her look in the two brief encounters they'd had so far, and behind her Jonathan Levinson and a tall girl with a legal pad Michaela didn't yet have a name for, both of them moving with the focused urgency of people who'd found something and needed it heard before anyone else's agenda ate the room's attention.

"Levinson," Michaela said. "You look like you've got something."

"I might," Jonathan said, and there was a flatness in it that Michaela had learned, over three days, to take seriously — the flatness of somebody quoting a much older, much more tired voice than his own. "It's not conclusive. I want to say that up front so nobody treats it like conclusions."

"Noted. Sit."

He sat. The tall girl sat next to him, notebook already angled so they could both read it, and Michaela filed that too — whoever she was, she mattered to how Jonathan Levinson processed information now, which meant she mattered to the room.

Warren Mears and a girl Michaela's briefing materials identified as Kimberly Williams — no. Not Williams. Seven. Just Seven, apparently, the briefing had been very specific that using anything else got you a correction, delivered politely but without deviation — came in ninety seconds later, exactly as advertised by nobody except themselves, and took seats with the economy of two people who'd already had every conversation they needed to have before walking in.

"Captain Reeves," Seven said, by way of greeting, precise, formal, without small talk. Michaela decided she appreciated that more than she'd expected to.

Giles cleared his throat. "Before we begin properly — Jonathan, you said you had something on the TX's behavior."
