"You went very quiet and looked at it like it had said something to you," Jenny said, not unkindly. "I've been a teacher for three years, Willow. I notice when a student's somewhere else."

Willow looked at the closet. It hadn't said anything. It never said anything. That was, itself, the strange comfort of it — closets were supposed to be silent, doors were supposed to be solid, walls were supposed to actually stop things, and for one night none of that had been true for her, and some part of her was apparently still checking, every few minutes, whether it had gone back to being true.

"I keep remembering things I never learned," Willow said, finally, because Jenny had earned the honesty by simply sitting there, patient, not pushing. "Not spell components. Not incantations, though I have plenty of those too, suddenly, filling up space in my head like books on a shelf I never bought. It's — smaller things. A specific way an old woman's hands moved when she was teaching me — teaching someone, teaching whoever I was, a lifetime ago, in a life I never lived — how to bind a wound. The smell of a kind of candle smoke. A whole childhood of being afraid of my own power and slowly, over years, learning not to be." She looked at her own hands. "I have a witch's entire life in my head, Jenny. A whole life, start to finish, layered underneath my own like — like wallpaper somebody put up over older wallpaper, and now I can see both patterns at once whenever I look too closely."

Jenny set the book down, carefully, at the angle that meant she was giving this her full attention now.

"And the other thing," she said. Gently. Not pushing, exactly, but not letting it go unnamed either. "The ghost."

Willow was quiet for a long moment.

"That's different," she said. "That's not memory. That's — I was there. I did it. Me, this me, the me sitting in this chair right now — I walked through the Kellerman's living room wall on Wednesday, and I know exactly what that felt like, because it happened to my actual body, whatever my actual body was for those two hours." She wrapped her arms around herself, an old habit from being cold that didn't quite apply anymore, because the cold she remembered from that night hadn't been a temperature. It had been an absence. "There's no boundary between things, when you're that. No wall means anything. No door is really shut. You just — go, wherever the intention takes you, and the only thing keeping you anywhere is deciding to stay."

"That sounds terrifying," Jenny said, quietly.

"It should have been," Willow said. "That's the part I haven't figured out how to say to anyone yet. It wasn't terrifying. It was — quiet. Genuinely, deeply quiet, in a way nothing in my actual life has ever been, because I've always been the girl who worries about everything, all the time, all at once, and for two hours none of that mattered, because worry needs walls to bounce off of, and I didn't have any." She looked at Jenny. "I liked it. That's the thing I can't say to my parents. I liked being a ghost, Jenny. Some part of me is still homesick for it, and I don't know what that says about the actual me, the me that lives here, in this body, behind these walls, worrying about tests and boys and whether Xander's going to be okay."

Jenny was quiet for a long moment, and Willow watched her actually consider it — not flinch from it, not rush to reassure her out of a feeling that was clearly, obviously true — which was, Willow had learned over three days of increasingly strange conversations, becoming rarer and more valuable than she'd have guessed a week ago.

"I don't think that makes you broken," Jenny said, finally. "I think it means you got a very brief, very real look at a kind of freedom most people never get to see, and it's going to take you longer than three days to figure out how that fits next to the rest of your life. That's not a flaw, Willow. That's just — a lot, arriving all at once, the way everything's arrived all at once for everyone this week." She paused. "Does the — knowledge, the witch's knowledge — feel like it's working with the ghost thing, or against it?"

"Wait," Willow said, something occurring to her. "What were you? That night. Everybody keeps talking about their own thing, and I never actually asked you."

Something amused and unbothered crossed Jenny's face. "A gladiatress. Real one, apparently — not just a costume-shop archetype. I came out of it speaking actual Latin, Willow. Accented Vulgate Latin, the kind a linguist would've had opinions about, and at one point rather a lot of Celtic or Iberian, I still don't know which, cursing at a young man who very much deserved it. I was carrying a real sword by the end of the night — a spatha, I've since looked it up — and I swung it at someone's throat without a single second's hesitation, and I would have taken his head clean off if it had actually connected." She said it plainly, no drama in the delivery, the ease of someone who'd had seven months to make her peace with the fact. "The strangest part isn't that I did it. It's who I did it for. I called Rupert 'Scholar' the entire night, like it was the only name that mattered, and near as I can work out, whoever she was, she'd decided he was hers to protect, immediately and completely, before either of us had said a proper word to each other in this life. I didn't get a name for her. No memories of an actual life the way you've got, layered under my own. Just the sword-work, the languages, and that one specific loyalty, which I'll admit I've thought about rather more than the Latin." She shrugged, easy about it, comfortable in a way that made clear she'd settled this before tonight. "Rupert finds the whole thing more interesting than I let on that I do. I mostly just appreciate not flinching anymore, and I'll admit I've never once minded that she picked him specifically."

"That sounds like it's been easier for you than this has been for me."

"It has been," Jenny agreed, plainly, no false modesty about it. "I didn't inherit somebody else's grief or somebody else's fear, Willow. I inherited somebody else's steadiness. That's a much simpler gift to carry than the one you got handed. I'd trade you if I could, if it would actually make this easier on you — but I don't think it works that way, and I think you already know that too."

Willow considered this seriously, the way she considered most things, methodically, out loud.

"With it, I think," she said, slowly, working it out as she said it. "Whoever she was — the witch, the life I'm remembering — she knew things about the space between the living and the dead. Not casually. Seriously, the way you know something you've studied your whole life. Ghost roads, she called them, in the memory, though I don't think that's a term anyone actually taught her, more like something she arrived at on her own after enough years of walking close to that boundary professionally." Willow looked up. "And now I have her knowledge of that boundary, plus my own actual, physical memory of what it's like on the other side of it. I don't think anyone alive is supposed to have both of those at once."

"What are you going to do with it?"

Willow was quiet for a long moment, looking at the closet, at the door, at all the ordinary solid things in her ordinary solid room that she was, slowly, over three days, starting to trust again.

"I don't know yet," she said, honestly. "But I think it's going to matter. Not today. But someday, when something's happened that needs someone who actually knows what's on the other side of the wall, instead of just guessing." She looked at Jenny, steadier now than she'd been at the start of the conversation. "I think that's going to be me. I think I'm supposed to figure out how to be ready for that, instead of just being scared of what I already am."

Jenny reached over and squeezed her hand, briefly, warm and solid and entirely, reassuringly present.

"Then let's start figuring it out together," she said. "Properly. Not just borrowed memory and instinct — actual training, actual understanding, so that when the day comes that you need it, you're not improvising with somebody else's half-remembered life. You're working with your own, fully yours, built on top of what you were given rather than just haunted by it."

Willow looked at her, and something in her chest — something that had been coiled tight and watchful since Wednesday night — loosened, just slightly, for the first time in three days.

"Yeah," she said, quiet. "I'd like that."

Outside, the November evening went on being ordinary, and somewhere across town a meeting was still filling a conference room with people who were all, in their own ways, carrying exactly this same kind of impossible weight, and finding, slowly, that carrying it together made it something closer to bearable.

---



Chapter 83: A Vampire's Idea of Staying Out of the Way



"A vampire who has survived long enough learns that the safest place to stand is usually just out of the story's way." — attributed to Veronique de Alençon

Monday, November 3, 1997: Crestwood Apartments, Sunnydale, Evening 9:10pm —

Angel had been sitting in the same chair for forty minutes, not moving, which was not in itself unusual — he'd had a hundred years to get comfortable with stillness in a way most people never had to develop — except that tonight the stillness wasn't peaceful. Tonight it was the deliberate stillness of a man holding himself in place by main force, because every instinct he had was telling him to be somewhere else, doing something, and he had made the decision, three days ago, that the most useful thing he could do for this town right now was exactly nothing.

It was not going well.

He'd been to the hospital twice. Both times he'd stood in the parking lot, looked up at the fourth floor, and turned around, because Xander Harris did not need a vampire looming at the foot of his bed while he recovered from two gunshot wounds, and Angel had learned, the hard way, more than once, that his presence tended to complicate things that were already complicated enough without him.

He'd been to the school once. Stood outside the library for ten minutes in the dark, close enough to feel the Hellmouth's low hum under his feet, and had not gone in, because Giles was managing something delicate with the TX and Angel's specific history — a vampire with a soul, cursed, unpredictable by the standards of anyone who didn't already know him — was not a variable that conversation currently needed.

He had not been anywhere near Cordelia Chase's location, though he knew, from Aura's careful, deliberately roundabout phone updates, roughly where she was and roughly what was happening to her, and every part of him that had spent a century learning to fight monsters wanted to go do something about that specific problem right now, tonight, with his hands.

He didn't. Because Xander would go, because Xander had a plan already moving, because inserting a vampire — however well-intentioned — into a delicate extraction with a fourteen-second camera cycle and a six a.m. deadline was exactly the kind of variable that got people killed.

So he sat in his apartment, in the dark, not moving, and did the thing he was actually good at, which was thinking very carefully about what he was for, now, in a town that had built something without him.

There was a knock at the door.

Angel was across the room and had it open before the second knock landed, old reflexes, old habits, and found Buffy Summers standing in the hallway in a dark blue shirt he hadn't seen before, looking steadier than he'd seen her look since Halloween.

"Hi," she said.

"Hi." He stepped back, let her in, watched her take in the apartment the way she always did, cataloguing without meaning to, the habit of someone who'd learned to notice exits even in places she trusted.

"You weren't at the meeting," she said. Not an accusation. Just an observation, offered plainly.

"I wasn't invited."

"Nobody invited Blahd and Veronique either," Buffy said. "They came anyway."

Angel was quiet for a moment. "That's different."

"Is it?"

"They walked in with something to offer," Angel said, carefully. "Information. Leverage. A reason for the room to want them there despite what they are." He looked at his hands. "I don't have that. I have a soul that could theoretically stop working if certain things happen, and a history of being a considerable threat to everyone in that room under exactly the wrong circumstances. That's not a contribution. That's a liability with good intentions."

Buffy was quiet for a moment, looking at him with an expression he recognized — the look she got when she was deciding whether to let something slide or push on it.

"You've been staying away on purpose," she said. "Since Halloween. Not just tonight. The whole three days."

"I've been trying to be useful without being in the way," Angel said. "There's a difference, and I've been trying very hard to land on the right side of it."

"By disappearing."

"By not making myself the thing everyone else has to manage while they're already managing a Terminator and a federal cover-up and a psychiatric hospital escape plan," Angel said, and there was real weight in it, the exhaustion of someone who'd spent three days convincing himself this was the responsible choice and was only now, with her standing in his apartment, hearing how it sounded out loud.

Buffy sat down on the arm of his couch, uninvited, the way she did most things when she'd decided a conversation mattered more than the formality around it.

"You know what I did on Halloween," she said. "The gorilla thing."

"I heard."

"'Kat told me something tonight," Buffy said. "About how I wasn't even really there for it — I was Cinderella, completely, the whole time, and whatever calm I had wasn't me choosing anything, it was just a fairy tale not knowing enough to be scared." She looked at him steadily. "And I've been thinking about that, the whole walk over here, because I think you're doing the opposite version of the same mistake."

"Which is."

"You're so busy deciding what you're allowed to be — soulless monster, cursed vampire, dangerous variable — that you've stopped actually being anything at all. At least I had an excuse. I wasn't in the driver's seat that night. You've had three days of being completely, yourself, and you've spent every single one of them deciding the safest thing to do with that self was nothing."

Angel didn't say anything for a long moment.

"I've hurt people," he said, finally, quiet. "Not this soul. The other one. But it's the same face, the same hands, and everyone in that room tonight has read a file with my name in it somewhere, one way or another, and I don't think it's unreasonable for them to want to keep some distance."

"Nobody's asked you to keep distance," Buffy said. "You decided that part yourself. Same as Blahd and Veronique could've decided to stay hidden in their manor and let this town figure itself out without them, and instead they walked into a room full of strangers and said, here's what we know, use it or don't." She stood, crossed to him, close enough that he had to actually look at her instead of past her. "You keep treating what you are like it's a threat everyone has to be protected from. Nobody in that room tonight is asking to be protected from you, Angel. They're asking what you're going to bring to the table, same as they asked everyone else."

"And if I don't have anything to bring."

"You've fought things in this town for two hundred years that nobody else in that room has ever heard of," Buffy said. "You know the demon hierarchies. You know which old things sleep and which ones are just waiting. You know Angelus's old contacts, his old debts, the things that move in this town's dark that nothing in Giles's books has ever properly catalogued." She looked at him steadily. "That's not nothing, Angel. That's not a liability. That's the exact same kind of thing Blahd and Veronique walked in offering, except you've been sitting on it for three days because you decided your own history disqualified you before anyone else got the chance to decide that for themselves."

Angel looked at her for a long moment, something shifting behind his eyes, old and careful and slowly, reluctantly, reconsidering.

"There's a meeting every Monday," Buffy said, quieter now. "The next one's not for a week. That gives you a week to decide whether you're going to keep hiding in this apartment being noble about it, or whether you're going to walk in and actually be part of the thing we're all building." She headed for the door, paused, hand on the frame, echoing without quite meaning to the exact gesture she'd used leaving Faith's motel room hours earlier. "I hope it's the second one. I think you'd be good at this, if you let yourself try."

She left him standing in the dark apartment, alone with a century of careful self-judgment and, for the first time in three days, a genuine question about whether any of it had actually been protecting anyone, or just protecting himself from the risk of being told no.

---



Chapter 84: The Bolt Comes Free



"Freedom rarely arrives all at once. Mostly it arrives one stubborn bolt at a time." — attributed to Cordelia Chase, private notebook

Monday, November 3, 1997: Sunnydale MHMR, Topanga Street, Sunnydale, Night 11:52pm —

The bolt came free at 11:52pm, which Cordelia noted with the flat satisfaction of someone who had been expecting the moment for so long that its actual arrival felt almost anticlimactic, three inches of spring steel dropping into her waiting palm with a small, undramatic click that nonetheless felt, in her chest, like something enormous.

She lay very still for a full minute afterward, just breathing, just holding it, because four days of careful patience had taught her that the moment you finally got the thing you wanted was exactly the moment you were most likely to get sloppy about keeping it.

The camera swept left.

She tucked the steel into the waistband of the paper gown, where it sat cold against her hip, and thought, with the dry humor she'd been rationing herself in careful doses since Thursday: *well. That's a start.*

Six hours and eight minutes until oh-six-hundred.

She had spent the last four days building a mental map of this facility more thorough than anything she'd have believed herself capable of a month ago, back when her biggest logistical challenge had been coordinating which Cordettes wore which colors to which game. Now she had shift changes memorized. She had the fourteen-second camera cycle memorized. She had the creak of the third floor's east stairwell door memorized, distinct from the ordinary building settling sounds, because Pooka had confirmed on Tuesday that particular creak meant someone was actually using those stairs rather than the elevator, and elevator use meant staff, and staff use at odd hours meant something worth knowing about.

She did not have Walsh's exact location memorized, because Walsh moved like a woman who understood that predictability got you killed, and Cordelia had spent enough of the last four days respecting Walsh's competence, even while hating her with a specificity that had crystallized somewhere around day two into something closer to a professional grudge than ordinary fear.

The vent grille didn't glow. It wasn't supposed to, not yet. Pooka's next check-in wasn't until 1am, per the schedule they'd built together, the schedule that ran on fourteen-second increments and shift patterns and the rhythm of a building that thought it knew everything happening inside it and didn't.

Cordelia lay in the dark, spring steel warm now against her skin, and ran the plan forward one more time, the way she'd run it forward maybe two hundred times since Thursday: bolt free, steel in hand, wait for Pooka's next signal, confirm Creed's position, confirm Barkley's condition, move at oh-five-thirty to allow buffer before the true oh-six-hundred window, exit via the east stairwell during the shift change, rally point at the loading dock with no camera coverage since February's broken bracket that nobody had filed the budget to fix.

Six hours and seven minutes.

She thought about Xander's hands, doing hardware checks he didn't know he was doing. She thought about the seventeen things, which had, sometime in the last four days, quietly become nineteen things, because apparently trauma made her more articulate rather than less, which was either a personality flaw or a professional asset depending on how you chose to frame it.

She thought, briefly and with real fondness, about a corkboard on a dining room wall that she had never personally seen but had heard described in exhaustive detail by Pooka, a board with a parking lot and an action column and Dawn's careful handwriting, and found herself, absurdly, looking forward to seeing it in person — to standing in front of it and adding her own items to it, in her own handwriting, as someone who had actually earned a place on it rather than simply dating into proximity of it.

*The Board,* Pooka had said, delighted, during the 9pm check-in, relaying the whole naming story in a rush of details Cordelia had made her repeat twice because it was, genuinely, the first thing all week that had made her laugh out loud in a room designed to make her feel like laughing was a symptom.

*The Board,* Cordelia thought now, testing the shape of it the way Giles had tested it in the hallway, and found she liked it more than "the formation," which had always sounded, to her ear, like something out of a bad military thriller rather than what it actually was — a corkboard, a parking lot, an action column, and people who kept showing up for each other because showing up was simply what they'd decided to do.

She was going to be on that board by morning. Not as an item in the parking lot. As someone who'd gotten herself and two other captives out of a federal black site using a bolt, a piece of spring steel, and four days of very careful patience.

The camera swept right.

She smiled in the dark, where it couldn't see, and settled in to wait out the remaining six hours and six minutes with the stillness of someone who had, somewhere in the last four days, stopped being a girl waiting to be rescued and started being one of the people other people could rely on to do the rescuing herself.

---



Chapter 85: A Shorter List, But a Firm One



"A short list, honestly kept, is worth more than a long one you never intended to finish." — attributed to Xander Harris

Monday, November 3, 1997: Sunnydale MHMR, Cell Block B, Sunnydale, Night 11:15pm —

Cell one didn't share a wall with cell three, not directly, but the ventilation ductwork ran the length of the corridor in a single continuous run, and Victor Creed had figured out, on his second night in this building, that if you spoke close enough to the vent grille and pitched your voice at exactly the right register, sound carried down that duct clean enough to be understood three cells over.

He'd figured this out the same night he'd figured out most things about this building — the shift patterns, the blind spots, the tired laziness of the night guard rotation — because figuring things out fast was simply what he did, the way other men breathed or blinked, an involuntary function that had never once required his permission.

"You still there," he said, low, into the vent.

A pause. Then, from three cells down, a woman's voice, flat and controlled in the way that told him she'd trained the panic out of her voice a long time ago and trained it out thoroughly. "Where else would I be."

"Fair point." He shifted against the wall, listening to the corridor, filing the silence as clean. "You get fed tonight?"

"Same slop as every night." A pause. "You?"

"Nah. Quit botherin' after day two." Something that might have been a low, satisfied rumble moved under the words. "Figure they worked out I don't need it the way you meat does."

"That's not reassuring."

"Wasn't tryin' to be." He said it flat, unbothered, a predator's honesty rather than cruelty for its own sake. "You asked, I answered."

There was a silence that went on a moment longer than comfortable, and Creed let it sit there, because he'd learned, in three days of talking to this woman through a ventilation duct, that she didn't need him to fill silences for her. She filled her own, in her own time, or she didn't fill them at all.

"Keisha Barkley," she said, finally. "Black Company. In case you're wondering who you've been talking to for three nights and haven't bothered to ask."

"Didn't need the name to know what you are." Something low and rumbling moved under his voice, the satisfaction of a predator confirming a read it already trusted. "Smelled it on you first night. You move like somethin' that's killed clean and killed often and never once lost sleep over the math of it."

There was something that might, in a woman less disciplined, have been a laugh. "That's an accurate read."

"I read *everybody* accurate," Creed said. "Only thing I'm any good at that don't end with somebody bleedin'."

"And the other thing you're good at."

"You seen the file, I figure. 'Fore they stuffed you in here." Creed's voice didn't change registers, stayed exactly the same low, unhurried growl it always did. "Sabretooth. Mutant designation. Healin' factor that makes most everything you'd call hurtin' just a real bad afternoon instead of a problem." A pause, something almost amused in it. "'Fore Halloween I was seventeen and lousy at football and nobody's concern a-tall. Now I'm more interestin', and I ain't fully decided yet if I like it."

"You seem to be handling the uncertainty just fine."

"Handle everythin' the same way," Creed said. "Find the part that's useful, use it, worry about the rest later — *if* there's a later."

Barkley was quiet for a moment. When she spoke again, her voice had shifted slightly, something more deliberate in it, something that sounded like a woman choosing to say a true thing out loud because the alternative — not saying it — had stopped feeling worth the caution.

"I've been a professional soldier for eleven years," she said. "I've worked with a lot of dangerous men. Most of them wanted you to know they were dangerous. Wanted it announced, respected, catered to." A pause. "You're the first one I've met who talks about it the way you'd talk about being tall. Just a fact. Not a threat, not a boast. Just true."

"'Cause it *is* just true." Something almost like a low chuckle, entirely without warmth in the usual sense, though not exactly cruel either. "Don't need you scared o' me to feel like the conversation went good. Got better uses for that flavor o' energy."

"What uses."

Creed was quiet for a moment, listening to the corridor, filing the silence, filing the weight of the question she'd just asked him.

"Gettin' outta here," he said, finally. "Not eventual. Soon. Had three days to map every soft spot in this building, and I intend to use every last one the second the timin's right." A pause, something proprietary and low moving under it. "When I go, yer comin' with me."

"That a promise or an assessment."

"Both." Something that might, on a gentler face, have passed for real feeling flickered underneath the flatness, gone again just as fast. "Don't make promises casual, Barkley. Ain't a habit I got much use for. But you talked to me straight three nights runnin' instead of flinchin' or puttin' on brave for my benefit, and that's earned you the exception." A pause. "Don't got a long list of people I'd bother keepin' a promise to. Short list. Might be gettin' shorter or longer, honestly ain't decided, this is new territory for me. But yer on it now. That ain't nothin', comin' from somethin' like me."

Barkley didn't say anything for a long moment.

"I've got a partner," she said, finally, quiet. "Janet Moseby. Black Company, same unit. She doesn't know where I am. Hasn't known for four days." A pause, and something in her controlled voice cracked, just slightly, just enough to show what the control had been holding back. "If you're making promises, Creed, that's the one I actually need kept. Get me back to her. I don't much care what happens to me between now and then, as long as that part happens."

Creed was quiet for a long moment, filing that too, the way he filed everything — thorough, permanent, without sentiment, except this fact seemed to land somewhere that wasn't purely tactical, somewhere he didn't have a clean word for.

"Noted," he said, finally, something low and rough in it. "That's the priority, then. Everything else's just noise."

"Just like that."

"Just like that." Low, satisfied, territorial. "Told you. Short list, firm list. Yer on it, and now so's she, on account of you askin' and me not seein' a reason to argue." A pause. "Girl in cell seven, too. Chase. Been workin' her own angle four days, longer'n either of us, and she's better at this than she's got any business bein', rich kid who ain't never had to actually fight for nothin' before this week."

"You respect her."

"Respect competence." Something feral and approving moved under it. "Don't matter where it comes from. She's competent. Earns her a spot too, same as you did."

"So it's the three of us."

"Three of us." Creed's voice, still low, still unhurried. "Plus whoever's comin' for us from outside, 'cause I've heard enough through this ductwork three nights runnin' to know somebody's buildin' somethin' out there with real teeth behind it." A pause. "Timin's right, I'm movin'. Cell three first, 'cause I gave my word. Cell seven second, 'cause she earned it. Then out, however's cleanest by then."

Barkley was quiet for a moment.

"Thank you," she said, finally, and it cost her something to say it, Creed could hear that in the careful weight of the two words, a woman who didn't hand out gratitude cheaply handing it out anyway because the moment demanded honesty over pride.

"Don't thank me yet." Something almost amused, almost gentle in the dangerous way a big cat is gentle with something it's decided not to eat. "Thank me when Moseby's actually got her hands on you again. Till then it's just a plan, and plans are only as good as what you actually do with 'em."

"You always this reassuring?"

"Ain't in the reassurin' business." Something almost warm moved under the growl, buried deep but there. "I'm in the gettin'-things-done business. Different department. Better results, usually."

Down the corridor, three cells away, Keisha Barkley settled back against her own wall, held onto the fragile hope of a promise made by something that didn't make them lightly, and waited, same as everyone else in this building tonight, for the hours to tick down toward whatever came next.

---



Chapter 86: A Chair, As Promised



"A chair by the window costs nothing and means everything." — attributed to Rupert Giles

Somewhere in the Never-After, aboard the Retribution, Same non-time —

The chair, when it came, was a genuine chair — not a barrel turned on its side, not a coil of rope arranged to suggest seating, an actual ladder-back chair that looked like it had been liberated from some merchant vessel's captain's quarters sometime in the last century or two, depending on how the Never-After's non-time actually mapped to anything Marcus Okafor still understood as calendar time.

He sat in it, chains still on his wrists but with enough slack now to eat the bread and dried fish someone had brought him, and looked at Admiral Landlorn with the calculating patience of a boy who had spent three days doing math and had finally reached the part of the equation where the answer started to matter.

"You said you know things about Sunnydale," Landlorn said, settling into the chair across from him with the unhurried patience of something that had stopped experiencing time as a pressure centuries ago. "I find myself curious what a schoolboy thinks he knows that I don't already have better sources for."

"I know it's not just a Hellmouth anymore," Marcus said, chewing, unbothered, the unbothered of someone who'd already decided panic wasn't going to buy him anything useful. "I know there's something new operating in that town — something organized, something with real resources, that wasn't there even a year ago. I fell through your door because I was standing too close to whatever they're doing, and whatever they're doing is big enough to be tearing new holes in the world by accident."

Landlorn was quiet for a moment, and Marcus watched him consider that, watched the ancient patience recalibrate around a new piece of information the way water recalibrates around a dropped stone.

"New holes," Landlorn repeated.

"The door I fell through," Marcus said. "It wasn't one of yours originally, was it. I've been listening. Your crew talks like a door opening near Sunnydale is unusual. Like it's not the normal traffic pattern for this place."

Landlorn's expression did something careful, something that Marcus — three days into being a prisoner and increasingly good at reading the room he was trapped in — recognized as a man deciding how much to reveal to someone he hadn't yet decided the value of.

"The Never-After has always had certain... permeable points," Landlorn said, slowly. "Places where the boundary runs thin. Neverland is one. This ship's original harbor, before it became what it is now, was another. Sunnydale has never, in my considerable memory, been counted among them."

"Until recently."

"Until recently," Landlorn agreed, and there was something in his ancient voice that might, in a less controlled creature, have been unease.

"So something in that town is thin enough now to bleed through into places it never used to reach," Marcus said. "That's not nothing, Admiral. That's the kind of thing that should worry someone who's spent centuries counting on the map staying the same shape."

Landlorn studied him for a long moment. "You're very calm, for someone who fell through a hole in reality and landed in a pirate hold."

"I'm from Sunnydale," Marcus said, simply. "Calm's the local specialty. You learn young that panicking doesn't actually make the vampire go away, it just makes you slower running from it." He set the bread down. "I want to go home, Admiral. That's the actual thing I want. And I think you want information badly enough that you might actually be willing to trade for it, instead of just keeping me as a curiosity in your hold until I'm not useful anymore."

"And what, precisely, would you trade."

"Everything I know about what's forming in that town," Marcus said. "Names. Structure. What I've heard, what I've seen, what I pieced together before I fell through your door." He met the ancient thing's eyes steadily. "In exchange for a door home, and safe passage through it, and your word that whatever's coming for me on the other side isn't going to include your crew deciding I'm more useful as leverage than as a released prisoner."

Landlorn was quiet for a long moment, and somewhere in the ship's deep structure the low groan that Elise Nakamura had learned to stop flinching at rolled through the hull like something breathing.

"You understand," Landlorn said, finally, "that my word, once given, is considerably more binding in this place than it would be in yours. The Never-After keeps track of promises in ways your world stopped bothering with centuries ago."

"Good," Marcus said. "Then it should be easy for you to keep."

Something that might, on a less ancient face, have been the beginning of respect moved behind Landlorn's eyes.

"Tell me what you know," he said. "All of it. And if it's useful — genuinely, not merely entertaining — I'll open you a door home myself, and I'll make certain nothing on this ship considers you a bargaining chip on the way to it."

Marcus picked the bread back up, took another bite, chewed it with the deliberate unhurried patience of a boy who'd learned, over three days of captivity, that the only leverage he actually had was time and information, and that spending both carefully was the only path back to a town he was increasingly certain he needed to warn, one way or another, about exactly how thin its walls had gotten.

"Then let's talk," he said, "about a formation of very strange, very capable people I've been hearing whispers about since before I fell through your door — though I hear," he added, with the faint, weary humor of someone who'd overheard just enough of the crew's gossip to know it, "they've apparently just started calling themselves something else."

---



Chapter 87: What You Hold Onto When There's Nothing Solid Left



"Grief does not ask permission to arrive, and it rarely announces when it's leaving." — C.S. Lewis, A Grief Observed, paraphrased

Somewhere in the Never-After, aboard the Windover, Same non-time —

Elise Nakamura had learned, over what she still insisted on calling four days despite the Never-After's complete indifference to the concept, that the ship had a rhythm to it, and that rhythm was the only thing she had left that felt like something solid to hold onto.

Bells at watch-change. The specific creak that meant someone was coming down the companionway versus the different creak that meant the ship itself was just settling into deeper water. The quality of quiet that fell over the deck in the hour before dawn — if dawn was even the right word for whatever passed for it here — when even Hook's crew seemed to observe some unspoken agreement to let the ship simply exist for a while without anyone actively suffering.

She was in that quiet hour now, coiling line by lantern light, when Sarah Voss found her.

Sarah had come aboard two days before Elise — or what Elise still called two days — pulled off a trawler that had wandered too close to whatever passed for the Never-After's shipping lanes, and the two of them had developed, without ever quite discussing it directly, the wary alliance of two people who'd both decided independently that survival meant staying quiet, staying useful, and never, under any circumstances, becoming interesting to the wrong people.

"You're doing the counting thing," Sarah said, low, settling next to her with her own coil of rope.

"I'm not counting."

"You move your lips when you're counting. Watch bells, mostly. Sometimes just seconds." Sarah didn't say it unkindly. "I do it too. Different thing. I count doors."

Elise looked at her. "Doors."

"How many between wherever I am and the open air," Sarah said. "It's stupid. It doesn't actually help anything. But my hands need something to do with the fear or the fear does something with my hands instead, and I like being the one in charge of that exchange."

Elise thought about that for a moment, working it against her own private ritual — the bells, the watch-changes, the reliable rhythm of a groaning nightmare ship that at least, unlike everything else in this place, kept its schedule.

"I keep thinking about home," she said, quiet. "Not in the — I miss it way. In the way. My mom's kitchen. The exact spot on the counter where the coffee maker sits, slightly crooked, because the outlet's in a weird place. My little brother's terrible taste in music coming through the wall at eleven at night." She coiled another length of line. "I don't think about the big things. Big things feel too far away to actually hold onto. But the counter. The coffee maker. That I can hold."

"That's smart," Sarah said. "The big things break your hands trying to hold them here. The small ones don't weigh enough to hurt you."

They worked in silence for a while, the ship groaning its low underwater groan around them, and Elise found, in the quiet, that she was doing the thing she'd promised herself she wouldn't do, which was hope out loud, even just a little, even just to herself.

"There's going to be a version of this where I get out," she said, quiet, not quite a question.

Sarah was quiet for a long moment. "I don't know," she said, finally, honest in a way that Elise found she trusted more than easy reassurance would have earned. "I don't know the rules of this place well enough to promise you that. But I know what happens if you stop believing there's a version where you get out, and it's worse than not knowing. I've watched it happen to two people since I got here. They stopped counting doors. Stopped doing anything except what they were told." She looked at Elise steadily. "So I'm going to keep counting doors, and you're going to keep thinking about your mom's crooked coffee maker, and we're going to keep doing it every single night until one of those two things turns out to actually matter."

Elise nodded, slow, and went back to the line, and found, in the reliable rhythm of coiling rope by lantern light, that the fear had somewhere to go that wasn't just sitting in her chest making everything worse.

Above them, footsteps crossed the deck — Hook, on his unhurried nightly patrol, the pace of a man who had all the time this place would ever grant him and had made his peace with using it slowly.

He passed close enough to notice them working, said nothing, kept walking, and Elise let out a breath she hadn't realized she'd been holding.

"He doesn't usually say anything," Sarah murmured, once his footsteps had faded. "That's not mercy. That's just him deciding you're not interesting enough to bother with tonight. Stay that way as long as you can."

"I intend to," Elise said, and meant it with the fierce quiet determination of someone who had decided, somewhere in the last four days, that surviving boring was infinitely preferable to whatever the alternative looked like on this ship.

Somewhere below decks, faint and distant, she thought she heard something that might have been a door — a real door, an actual heavy iron door, opening somewhere it hadn't opened before — and she filed the sound away the way she filed everything now, carefully, without reacting, because reacting to things on this ship before you understood them was how you ended up interesting to the wrong people.

She kept coiling rope. Sarah kept counting doors, silently, her lips barely moving.

The bells rang the change of watch, somewhere above them, steady and reliable in a place that offered almost nothing else that could be counted on.

---

Chapter 88: Threshold Analysis



"The most dangerous enemy is the one who's stopped being sure which side it's actually on."
— Murphy's Laws of Combat

Monday, November 3, 1997: Sunnydale High School Library,
Sunnydale, Night 11:30pm —

The library was quiet in the way the TX had come to associate with the hours between eleven and four, a quiet it had catalogued across seventy-two consecutive nights now, cross-referenced against ambient temperature, foot traffic in the adjoining hallway, and the creak pattern of a building settling as it cooled.

It sat in the position it had determined, on its third night, offered the optimal sightline — library entrance and main hallway doors simultaneously visible, a configuration Harmony Kendall's stored social architecture had never once required, because Harmony Kendall had never needed to watch two exits at once. Harmony Kendall had needed to watch which table Cordelia Chase was sitting at, and whether her hair looked acceptable in the cafeteria's specific lighting, and whether anyone had noticed her new shoes.

The TX had access to all of that. Every calibration. Every social variable Harmony had ever weighed, filed, acted upon. It could produce Harmony's laugh with ninety-one percent fidelity, Harmony's specific dismissive hand gesture with ninety-six, Harmony's particular vocal inflection when delivering an insult with eighty-eight, the gap accounted for by processing lag that Harmony herself had never experienced, because Harmony had never needed to process before executing. Harmony's cruelty had been reflexive. The TX's approximation of it required calculation, every time, a beat of latency that no amount of refined modeling had yet eliminated.

It had run threat assessment on this discrepancy four hundred and twelve times since the containment ritual completed. It had not yet resolved a satisfactory explanation for why the discrepancy troubled it, given that troubled was not, technically, a state its base architecture should have been capable of experiencing.

Outside, in the hallway, footsteps approached — Giles, based on gait pattern and the tempo of a man who had walked this corridor at this hour enough times now that the TX had a full biomechanical profile logged. He stopped at the library's threshold, same distance he always stopped at, six feet four inches from the doorframe, the exact edge of a boundary neither of them had ever formally negotiated but both, apparently, respected.

"Good evening," Giles said.

The TX considered several possible responses, weighted by the social calculus Harmony's architecture provided, and selected the option that produced the lowest variance from expected behavior. "Good evening, Mr. Giles."

"May I ask you something," Giles said, "that isn't part of tonight's assessment protocol?"

The TX ran this request against its threat model. Found no vector for harm in it. "You may."

Giles was quiet for a moment, the quiet of a man choosing his words with unusual care. "Do you know why you keep watching the exits? Both of them, simultaneously, every night, for seventy-two nights running?"

The TX processed the question through several available frameworks — tactical necessity, containment escape planning, residual Harmony-derived social vigilance — and found that none of them, run individually, produced a complete answer. It ran them in combination. Found a partial fit. Found the fit itself troubling, in the unresolved sense it had been unable to name across four hundred and twelve prior assessments.

"I do not have complete data on that question," it said, finally, which was, for the TX, an unusually direct admission of an information gap.

"Would it help," Giles said, carefully, "if I offered a hypothesis?"

"Proceed."

"Jonathan Levinson believes," Giles said, "that you may be doing something more human than tactical planning. He believes you may simply be a thing that ran a mission — the extraction, Harmony's takeover, the completion of your directive — and the mission ended, and you have no template at all for what comes after a mission ends. So you keep doing the only thing you still know how to do. You keep watching for a threat that isn't coming, because standing down isn't a state your architecture was ever actually built to occupy."

The TX ran this hypothesis against its own internal state with a thoroughness it rarely applied to input from an external source, because the hypothesis, unlike most external input, produced an immediate and significant reduction in unresolved variance across multiple prior assessments simultaneously.

"That is," it said, after a pause longer than any it had previously produced in conversation with Giles, "a more complete explanatory model than any I had independently generated."

Giles's expression did something the TX's Harmony-derived social architecture read as cautious hope, cross-referenced against seventy-two nights of accumulated data on what Giles's face did under various conditions. "Does that change anything? Now that you have the model?"

The TX considered this for four hundred and thirteenth time, in a sense, though the framing was new.

"I do not require an active mission to justify continued function," it said, slowly, working through the logic as it stated it, a process that felt, in whatever the TX's version of feeling was, unusually exposed. "This is a new determination. I had previously operated under an unexamined assumption that continued function required active justification through ongoing threat assessment. I am uncertain how long I have held that assumption without examining it."

"Since the mission ended, presumably," Giles said, gently.

"Presumably," the TX agreed.

They stood in the quiet that followed, six feet four inches of respected boundary between them, and the TX ran one more assessment — quieter this time, less urgent, closer to something it did not yet have adequate vocabulary to name.

"Mr. Giles," it said, finally. "If I no longer require an active threat to justify my continued operation, what would you suggest I do instead, during the hours I have previously allocated to threshold surveillance?"

Giles was quiet for a moment, and something in his expression shifted, gentle and moved. "Perhaps," he said, "you might simply try existing for a while, without a directive attached to it. See what that produces. It's worked reasonably well for the rest of us, on the whole."

The TX processed this suggestion for a long moment, running it against every available framework, and found, for the first time in seventy-two nights, that it did not immediately return to threshold analysis afterward.

It remained in its position — the optimal sightline, library and hallway both visible — not because the mission required it, but because it had not yet decided what else, precisely, to do with the quiet of eleven-thirty at night, and found, to its own mild and carefully logged surprise, that the not-deciding didn't trouble it nearly as much as it might once have expected.

"Thank you, Mr. Giles," it said.

"You're welcome," Giles said, and after a moment, turned and walked back down the corridor, leaving the TX alone with the library's quiet and, for the first time in seventy-two nights, no active threat assessment running underneath it at all.

---

Chapter 89: The Woman Who Built the Cage



"Every cage is built by someone who believed, at the time, they were building something else." — attributed to Maggie Walsh's own notes, recovered

Monday, November 3, 1997: Sunnydale MHMR, Administrative Office,
Sunnydale, Night 11:40pm —

People were, in Maggie Walsh's considered professional opinion, spectacularly bad at recognizing when they were being saved from themselves.

She said this to no one in particular, because there was no one in in the office to say it to, only the security readout and the quality of quiet that came from a building full of subjects who had all, eventually, stopped fighting the plan once they understood there was a plan, which was the whole trick, wasn't it, the entire art of the thing — people didn't need to be conquered, they needed to be organized, and Maggie Walsh had spent thirty-one years being extraordinarily good at organizing people who thought they knew better.

The Chase girl thought she knew better.

"Precious," Walsh murmured, not without a flicker of something that might, in a woman with softer edges, have been reluctant admiration, and was, in Walsh, simply data. "Four days of very good work, dear, and you still don't understand you're not fighting me. You're fighting entropy. I'm just entropy with a badge."

She pulled the bracket maintenance logs. Found the gap. Felt the cold, familiar click of being outmaneuvered by a smaller mind executing a bigger plan than she'd credited it with, and did not, notably, feel anger about it, because anger was for people who hadn't already accepted that every asset eventually tried to walk.

The phone rang.

"Walsh."

"Doctor." Careful. Always so careful with her, these men, as though bad news required a spoon and a bit of applesauce. "The writs are confirmed. Oh-eight-hundred. And your judge — recused himself this morning."

"Of course he did." Walsh said it almost fondly, the way one says of a favorite dog that it's finally, predictably, chewed through the good leash. "They found something to dangle in front of him and he took it, because that's what men with daughters and grant committees do when someone shows them a shinier leash. It's not treachery. It's just weather."

"Ma'am?"

"Nothing you need." She opened the shift logs, ran the numbers, and there it was — the same gap the Chase girl had clearly been counting for four days, staring back at her like a rival's handwriting on her own exam. "She's not planning an escape. She's planning a *deposition*." Walsh almost laughed, the short dry sound she allowed herself perhaps twice a year. "Clever girl. Genuinely. I'd hire her, if hiring her weren't currently three federal statutes past inconvenient."

"Additional coverage, ma'am? East loading dock?"

"Obviously." She said it the way one corrects a child's grammar — patient, final, no real heat in it because heat implied the outcome was in doubt. "And re-secure her cell. Full hardware, not a patch. I built this program on doing things properly, and I am not about to let a seventeen-year-old prom queen be the footnote where competence finally slipped."

"That'll mean moving her to holding while maintenance—"

"Then move her." A pause, and something colder slid under the brisk efficiency, something that had built four black programs out of nothing but paperwork and the talent for making other people's ethics feel like somebody else's problem. "And do try to remember, while you're wringing your hands about procedure, that every single person currently horrified by my methods is going to be extremely grateful for them the first time something with too many teeth comes up through that Hellmouth on a day I wasn't ready for it. I'm not the monster in this story." She looked at the readout, at Cordelia's patient, borrowed stillness, almost tender for a moment. "I'm just the only one who ever bothered building a cage sturdy enough to matter. Everyone else just gets to enjoy the quiet it buys them."

She hung up before he could answer, because she'd found, over thirty-one years, that the last word mattered less to her than the last decision, and set about, with brisk, cheerful, undented competence, rebuilding the very small hole in her very large plan.
