Chapter 90: Everyone's Version of Not Sleeping



"Insomnia is just the mind refusing to file the day away as finished." — attributed to a Sunnydale Memorial intake form

Monday into Tuesday, November 3–4, 1997: South Marion Drive, Sunnydale Medical Complex, Conference Room B,
Sunnydale, Night 1:15am —

The meeting had technically ended hours ago, in the sense that people had stopped sitting in chairs around the table, but Michaela Reeves had learned enough about this town's specific rhythms in three days to know that a meeting like tonight's didn't actually end so much as it slowly redistributed itself into smaller, quieter versions of itself scattered across a building that never fully went dark.

She was on her fourth cup of coffee, which Brockhurst had stopped commenting on around cup three, on the theory, he'd said, that a woman running an extraction in four hours forty-five minutes had earned the right to poison herself with caffeine on her own recognizance.

"Final positions," she said, laying the annotated floor plan across the table one more time, the paper soft now at the creases from repeated folding. "Bell's confirmed the loading dock blind spot holds through oh-six-thirty. Moseby and Merrill take the perimeter, north and south. You and I take the extraction point itself."

"And the writs," Brockhurst said.

"Serve at oh-eight-hundred, exactly on schedule, whether Chase is out by then or not." Michaela traced the timeline with one finger. "We want her clear at least ninety minutes before service, so Walsh's legal scramble doesn't have any incentive to accelerate a search of her own facility before the paperwork actually lands."

"And if the second plan doesn't hold. If Chase can't move at oh-six-hundred for whatever reason."

"Then we go to the writs alone, and we get her out through the front door with federal paper instead of a vent," Michaela said. "Slower. Messier. But not nothing." She looked up. "I don't love that version. I trust her more than I trust paperwork with a recused judge's replacement I haven't vetted yet."

Brockhurst was quiet for a moment, looking at the floor plan, at the small annotated X that marked the cell block, at the four hours and forty-five minutes standing between now and the version of tonight where this either worked cleanly or didn't.

"You're pushing hard on this one," he said, not quite a question.

"I'm pushing hard on all of them." Michaela didn't look up from the plan. "But yeah. This one specifically." She was quiet a beat, deciding, apparently, that four days and four cups of coffee had earned him the honest version. "I've got two of my own, back with their father, because active duty and custody don't mix and I made my peace with that a long time ago. Doesn't mean I don't think about it, every single time one of these extractions has an actual kid's name attached to it instead of a case number. Chase is eighteen and dangerous in her own right, and I'm still going to move heaven and earth to get her out clean, because somewhere back in Colorado I've got a girl about to turn eleven who I'd want somebody moving heaven and earth for, if it were her instead."

"Didn't figure you for kids," Brockhurst said, careful, not unkind.

"Most people don't. I don't exactly advertise it." She tapped the timeline, done with the subject as abruptly as she'd opened it. "Oh-five-thirty. Let's not be late."

"You've been running this op for three days on maybe six hours of actual sleep," he said.

"I'm aware."

"I'm not telling you to stop," he said. "I'm telling you I've watched good operators lose the extra half-second that matters because they convinced themselves exhaustion was the same as discipline." He set his own cup down. "Take twenty minutes. Actual eyes-closed twenty minutes, not just sitting quietly pretending. I'll hold the board."

Michaela looked at him for a long moment — at the steady competence that had, over three days, become something she genuinely relied on rather than merely tolerated, which was, for a woman who'd built her entire career on relying on precisely no one she hadn't personally vetted twice, its own small quiet data point worth noting.

"Twenty minutes," she said. "Wake me if anything moves."

"Nothing's going to move for twenty minutes," Brockhurst said. "That's rather the point of picking this specific twenty minutes."

She let herself sit back in the chair, close her eyes, and found, to her genuine surprise, that sleep actually came, thin and fast and useful, the soldier's sleep that didn't ask permission and didn't overstay it either.

---

Monday into Tuesday, November 3–4, 1997: Chestnut Street Office,
Sunnydale, Night 1:20am —

Sheila Martini did not sleep, which was, on nights like this, a genuine professional advantage rather than the quiet loneliness it usually felt like, because it meant she was awake at 1:20am when Giles came through the door looking like a man who'd been arguing with his own conscience for several hours and had finally lost.

"You should be resting," she said.

"So should you, in whatever sense applies," Giles said, dropping into the chair across from her with the weariness of a man carrying more than tonight's immediate crisis. "I keep thinking about the TX."

"The conversation."

"Yes." He rubbed his eyes. "I told it, essentially, to simply exist without a directive attached. I'm not at all certain that was responsible advice to give something with that much raw capability, Sheila. What if 'simply existing' produces something none of us can anticipate?"

Sheila considered this, turning it over with the patience of someone who'd had a great many decades to develop patience as a discipline rather than merely a personality trait.

"I existed for over a century without a directive," she said, finally. "Nobody built me a mission. I simply survived, and did what survival required, until Halloween gave me the chance to ask what I actually wanted instead of what continued existence demanded." She looked at him steadily. "It was terrifying. It was also the first time in a hundred and thirty years I felt like a person rather than a process. I don't think you did something reckless tonight, Giles. I think you did something kinder than reckless, and kindness is always a small risk, because you can't fully control what someone does with the freedom you hand them."

Giles was quiet for a long moment.

"You're better at this than you give yourself credit for," he said. "The reassurance. The perspective."

"I've had more practice being uncertain about my own nature than most people," Sheila said, dry. "It turns out that's transferable."

Outside, the November night held its quiet, four hours and forty minutes from a deadline that mattered to nearly everyone in this town, whether they knew it yet or not.

---

Monday into Tuesday, November 3–4, 1997: Rory Harris's property, above Ojai; Night 1:00am —

Rory sat on his own porch steps with a rifle across his knees he had no present use for, cleaning it anyway, because his hands needed something to do and the alternative was sitting still with what his property currently looked like under the yard lights.

Four of Michaela's people hadn't walked away from his land tonight. He'd known exactly none of their names before this evening and would carry every one of them now regardless, because that was simply how it worked, he'd learned that lesson younger than he'd have liked and had never once managed to unlearn it since. Some of Walsh's people hadn't walked away either, and he found, turning that over, that he couldn't locate even the beginning of regret about that half of the count.

He'd fired his own weapon tonight. Actually fired it, at an actual thing that had actually needed stopping, for the first time in longer than he generally let himself think about. His hands weren't shaking now. They'd shaken plenty an hour ago, in the delayed way hands did, and he'd let them, alone on the porch, because there hadn't been an audience worth performing steadiness for.

The phone had rung twice already. Xander's voice, tight and careful, checking on him with the formality of a boy trying very hard not to sound like he needed the checking-on to go the other direction too. Rory had told him he was fine. Mostly true. True enough for tonight, anyhow.

He thought about the last time his hands had shaken like that. A jungle, a considerable number of years and a considerable number of pounds ago, a much younger man in a body that used to bounce back from things overnight instead of aching about them for a week. He'd buried that ghost decades back, mostly, the way you buried things — not gone, just quiet, filed somewhere you didn't visit on purpose.

Tonight had gone and dug the whole plot up without asking permission.

"Should've stayed retired," he said, to nobody, to the dark yard, to four strangers whose names he intended to learn properly by morning and never once forget after. "Should've just stuck to fixing up cars and complaining about my knees like a normal old man."

The yard didn't answer. Yards generally didn't.

He kept cleaning the rifle anyway, methodical, unhurried, the old discipline of a man who'd learned a very long time ago that grief and vigilance could occupy the same set of hands at the same time, and that neither one required the other's permission to be real.

Around two, headlights came up the long drive — Michaela, back for a follow-up sweep she'd insisted on doing herself rather than delegating — and Rory set the rifle aside, stood, and went to meet her, because whatever tonight had cost either of them, there was still a morning coming that both of them intended to be standing upright for.

"You should be resting," Michaela said, taking in the rifle, the cleaning kit, the state of a man who clearly hadn't slept and had no present intention of starting.

"Physician, heal thyself, Chief Warrant Officer," Rory said, dry, and something passed between them that didn't need further words attached to it — two people who'd both just watched people die on the same patch of ground, deciding, separately and simultaneously, that the way through it was simply to keep standing, and to make sure the kids who still had a morning ahead of them got to have one.

---

Monday into Tuesday, November 3–4, 1997: South Marion Drive, Sunnydale Medical Complex, Room 412,
Sunnydale, Night 1:30am —

"You should sleep," Aura said, for the fourth time in an hour, not looking up from the notebook.

"I'm resting my eyes," Xander said, eyes very much open, fixed on the water stain.

"That's not a real category of rest. I checked with Ramesh. He specifically said 'resting your eyes' is not a recognized medical phase of recovery, it's a phrase people use to avoid admitting they're not actually resting."

"Ramesh talks a lot for a man who's supposed to be off shift."

"Ramesh cares about you in a way he finds professionally inconvenient," Aura said, which was, Xander reflected, probably the single most accurate description of Dr. Ramesh's entire relationship to this patient that anyone had produced all week. "Four hours and thirty minutes."

"I know the countdown, Aura."

"I know you know it. I'm just confirming we're both still doing math on the same clock." She set the notebook down, finally, and looked at him directly, something unguarded in it that she allowed herself rarely and always, Xander had noticed, precisely when it mattered most. "She's going to be fine, Xander."

"I know."

"You keep saying that like you're trying to convince the water stain."

Xander almost laughed, and it came out closer to real than he'd have predicted an hour ago. "Maybe I am. It's a good listener. Very patient. Never interrupts."

"Low bar," Aura said, and there was real warmth in it now, unguarded, the warmth of someone who'd spent the last several days discovering that caring about people out loud cost less than she'd always assumed it would. "Four hours and twenty-nine minutes."

"I heard you the first time."

"I know," Aura said. "I'm just going to keep saying it, every few minutes, until it's zero, because apparently that's what I do now instead of sleeping. Logging. Watching. Counting down." She picked the notebook back up. "It's in the methodology."

Xander looked at the ceiling, at the water stain, at the quiet weight of four hours and twenty-nine minutes standing between tonight and whatever came next, and found, for the first time in days, that the waiting felt less like helplessness and more like the last patient stretch of a plan that was, against every reasonable expectation, actually working.

---

﻿

---



Chapter 91: Four-Fifty-Five



"The hour before dawn asks more of a person than any hour that follows it." — attributed to a night-watch tradition, origin disputed

Tuesday, November 4, 1997: Sunnydale MHMR, Cell Block B,
Sunnydale, Early Morning 4:47am —

Creed had been out of his own cell for eleven minutes before anyone in the building had a reason to notice, which was exactly as long as he'd planned for, because eleven minutes was how long the overnight guard's coffee break ran, clocked and confirmed across three separate nights of listening, and Victor Creed did not build plans around guesses when actual data was available for the taking.

He'd gone up through the ceiling panel at 4:36, same route he'd used every night to talk through the ductwork, except tonight he kept moving past cell three instead of stopping there, because tonight's first stop wasn't Barkley. Tonight's first stop was the weapons locker in the secondary security station, because a plan that ended in getting three unarmed people out of a federal facility was a plan built on hope, and Creed had exactly no professional respect for hope as a tactical resource.

The secondary station had one guard posted overnight — a man named Delgado, according to the badge Creed had read through a gap in the door three nights running, mid-thirties, competent enough by ordinary civilian security standards and unprepared for what actually shared this building with him.

Creed dropped down from the ceiling access directly behind him.

Delgado got exactly as far as turning his head before Creed had a hand across his throat and the man's own sidearm out of its holster, the whole exchange lasting under four seconds, controlled and precise and without the theatrical cruelty Creed could have brought to it and chose, deliberately, not to. Delgado was unconscious and zip-tied to his own chair inside another ten seconds, breathing, alive, out of the equation for at least the next several hours, which was all Creed actually needed from him.

He took the sidearm, a second weapon from the locker, and two spare magazines, working the combination Barkley had walked him through blind, over three nights, purely from memory of facilities she'd worked in that used the same manufacturer's standard lock pattern.

Four-fifty-five. Right on schedule.

He was back at cell three's ceiling panel in under four minutes, dropping through, and found Barkley already standing, already braced against the wall favoring her left side, ready in the way of a woman who'd spent four days assuming tonight might be the night and had refused to let herself fully relax on any of the others.

"Got you somethin'," Creed said, low, handing her the second sidearm.

Barkley checked it in the dark by feel alone, chambered a round with the economical motion of someone who'd done this ten thousand times before whatever had put her in this building, and nodded once. "Good stock. You didn't have to clear the whole locker for this."

"Wasn't gonna leave a professional soldier unarmed on my watch." Something almost prim about the phrasing, entirely undercut by the low growl underneath it. "Seemed like poor manners."

"You have manners now."

"Got priorities." Something like a low, satisfied rumble. "Manners is just what priorities look like from a distance."

Getting her through the crawlspace was the part he'd actually worried about, four nights running, because the duct system wasn't built for two people moving at once, and Barkley's left side wasn't taking weight the way the rest of her clearly wanted it to. She didn't complain. She moved the way soldiers moved through terrain that hurt them — economical, controlled, converting pain into a scheduling problem rather than an obstacle, exactly the way Creed had read her from the very first conversation through the vent.

He half-carried her the last stretch above corridor B, one arm doing the work her leg currently couldn't, and set her down gently at the junction above cell seven with the care of a man who'd decided, four days ago, that this particular cargo mattered more than his own reputation for not caring about anything.

Cordelia was already awake below them — he could hear it in the quality of her stillness through the grille, the stillness of someone listening rather than sleeping — and Creed didn't have a clean way to talk to her the way he'd talked to Barkley all week, because sound carried differently this close to the monitored section of the block, close enough to the corridor camera's audio pickup that a conversation risked exactly the kind of attention none of them needed in the next six minutes.

He eased the grille aside just far enough to catch the sliver of light from her cell, and found her already looking up at the gap, waiting.

She mouthed the words instead of speaking them, slow and deliberate, shaping each one fully so he could read it off her lips in the thin light. *Guard's gone?*

Creed answered the only way he had available — one slow, deliberate shift of the grille, tilted a fraction to the left. Yes.

*Barkley?* she mouthed.

He shifted the grille again, same slow tilt. Yes.

She held his eyes for a second, reading the rest of it off his face rather than needing more from the vent, and mouthed the next thing carefully enough that there was no mistaking it. *Walsh. First.*

Creed didn't move the grille at all this time. He just held it steady, dead still, which had become, over four days of this exact silent conversation, its own kind of answer — the stillness that meant *already decided, no argument needed.*

Cordelia's mouth curved, just slightly, in the dark.

*Then let's go,* she mouthed, and reached up to work her own loosened panel free.

She came up through the gap under her own power, steady on her bare feet despite four days of no real food and no real sleep, and the three of them stood together in the cramped crawlspace junction for one silent moment — four days of separate, patient, individually built plans finally converging into a single shared direction.

Creed handed her nothing, because she hadn't asked for a weapon, and something in the set of her jaw told him she had her own plan for Walsh that didn't especially require one.

They dropped back down into the corridor together, quiet, and moved.

---

The east stairwell door had the telltale creak Cordelia had catalogued days ago — the one that meant actual foot traffic rather than the building settling — and tonight, at four-fifty-nine in the morning, it stayed silent, because nobody was using these stairs except the three of them.

They went up, not down, past the ground floor and the loading dock and the clean, quiet exit that Pooka had confirmed twice over the last hour, because clean and quiet wasn't where any of them were actually headed first.

Walsh's private office was on the second floor, east wing, and the two staff Cordelia had catalogued as regularly posted near it at this hour — a night nurse doing rounds and one contract security guard who preferred the break room's coffee to actual patrolling — were both, per four days of careful observation, reliably absent from this specific corridor between four-fifty and five-twenty.

The door was locked. Barkley had it open in under fifteen seconds, favoring her left hand, using a pick set Creed didn't ask where she'd acquired, because some questions between professionals didn't need asking.

Walsh was at her desk.

She'd been at her desk, it was clear, for most of the night — security readouts still open on the monitor, a legal pad covered in her own tight, decisive handwriting, a cup of coffee gone cold beside it — and she looked up at the three of them coming through her door at five-oh-two in the morning without so much as a change in her breathing, the same unhurried, cataloguing attention she'd have given any other unscheduled variable.

"Well," Walsh said. "That's rather earlier than I planned for."

"We noticed you'd adjusted your own timeline too," Cordelia said, stepping fully into the room, Creed and Barkley flanking her without needing to be told where to stand. "New brackets. Extra loading dock coverage. You've been busy tonight, Doctor."

"I run a tight program." There was, extraordinarily, still something like professional pride in it, even now, even with two armed intruders and an escaped subject standing in her office at five in the morning. "You should be flattered, Miss Chase. Very few subjects have ever made me revise a security protocol mid-containment."

"I'm not here for flattery," Cordelia said. "I'm here for the backup archive. The access code. And a very specific accounting of exactly how many other people you've done this to, because I don't think Rona Williams is the only name in that file, and I intend to know every single one of them before this morning is over."

"You don't understand what I've actually built," Walsh said, slowly, without heat, as though correcting a promising but mistaken student. "The things that come up out of that Hellmouth won't wait for your formation to finish getting its footing. Someone has to be ready before the politeness runs out. I built the cage sturdy enough to matter, and every single person currently horrified by my methods is going to be extremely grateful for them the first time—"

"Save it," Cordelia said, flat and cold in a way that four days in a paper gown had built in her, brick by brick, patient hour by patient hour. "I've had four days to think about every justification you were going to give me, and none of them change what's actually in that archive."

Creed shifted his weight, almost imperceptibly, the readiness of something that had already decided, several nights ago through a ventilation duct, exactly how this conversation was likely to end — and exactly whose decision it actually was to make.

Walsh looked at him. Studied the flat, patient violence sitting behind his eyes with the same detached interest she'd have given any other specimen, and filed it, visibly, as data rather than threat.

"You brought the mutation," she observed, mild, cataloguing rather than afraid. "Sabretooth. I'd wondered whether the designation stuck, or whether it had reverted to something more ordinary once the initial adrenaline wore off. Apparently it stuck."

"Brought a professional." Something low and rumbling moved under Creed's voice. "Careful what you call things, Doctor. Words like that cost people, sometimes."

Barkley moved to the door, checking the corridor, professional and unhurried. "Clock's still running," she said, quiet, to Cordelia. "We've got maybe six minutes before the next patrol sweep."

"The access code," Cordelia said. "Now."

Walsh looked at the three of them — at a girl who'd out-planned her for four straight days, at a soldier who'd disarmed her own facility from the inside, at something with Creed's flat patience standing between her and the door — and made, with the same clinical efficiency she brought to every decision in her career, the calculation that survival tonight required cooperation rather than defiance.

She gave them the code.

Creed looked at Cordelia, waiting, the patient stillness of something that had already decided this wasn't its decision to make for her.

Cordelia looked at Walsh — at the woman who'd stripped her down to a paper gown and called four days of careful, patient survival a flattering compliment to her own methods — and felt the last of whatever hesitation might once have lived in that decision simply fail to arrive at all.

"You said it yourself," Cordelia said, quiet. "Someone has to be ready before the politeness runs out. I spent four days getting ready, Doctor."

"I see that you did." Walsh said it plainly, without fear, without any visible shift at all from the same composed, assessing register she'd used since the moment they walked in — the final courtesy of a woman who had, evidently, already run this scenario in her own head at some earlier point and found herself, at the actual end of it, more curious than afraid. "For what it's worth, Miss Chase — and I recognize you may not want it — you have been, without exception, the single most instructive four days of my career. I would have liked to see what you became, given time."

"You don't get more time," Cordelia said. "That's the actual point."

"Yes," Walsh agreed, simply. "I imagine it is."

Cordelia's hand found the spring-steel shim in her waistband, the same worked bracket she'd spent four days freeing bolt by patient bolt, and crossed the last of the distance between them without hurrying, because there was nothing left in the room worth hurrying for. Walsh did not flinch, did not raise a hand, did not so much as widen her eyes — simply watched, to the very last available second, with the same detached, cataloguing attention she'd given every hour of the preceding four days, as though even this were, to her, primarily an opportunity to observe something she hadn't previously had data on.

It took four seconds. Cordelia drove the shim in once, low and hard, beneath the sternum and up, the way four days of careful, silent rehearsal against a folded blanket had taught her hands to do it without needing her mind's permission in the moment itself, and Walsh's breath left her in a single long exhale that was not quite a word, and her hands, which had never once moved to defend herself, simply opened over the desk and went still.

There was no triumph in it. Cordelia felt none arrive, then or after, no righteous surge, nothing that resembled the ending a different kind of story might have handed her. It was simply a necessary, unpleasant task, finished efficiently because efficiency was the only mercy either of them had left available, done the way you'd clear a nest of something dangerous out of a house you had to keep living in — carefully, completely, without ceremony, and without pretending afterward that it had been anything other than exactly that.

Creed didn't move. Didn't need to. He simply watched, the way he'd told Barkley he watched everything — filing it, respecting it, offering nothing except the fact that nobody in that room was going to stop her.

Barkley didn't look away either, because she'd decided, somewhere in the last four days, that whatever this girl had earned the right to do, she'd earned the right to have it witnessed rather than done quietly in a corner where it wouldn't count.

Cordelia stood over the desk a long moment, breathing, feeling nothing she could honestly call victory, the cold coffee cup still sitting undisturbed beside the legal pad, which had, apparently, mattered to Maggie Walsh right up until the final second exactly as much as the person standing in front of her had.

"Clock's still running," Barkley said, after a moment, low, steady, the professional calm of a soldier moving everyone's attention forward before it could snag on what had just happened. "Six minutes, maybe less now."

Cordelia looked down at the woman who would not be filing any more writs, running any more programs, building any more cages out of other people's stolen lives.

"Get the archive drive," she said, quiet, and her voice held steady. "We're taking everything."

---

They were out through the loading dock at five-eleven, the blind spot holding exactly as promised, three shapes moving fast and quiet across the dark lot toward the tree line where Merrill's team was waiting with a vehicle and more relief on their faces than any of them had expected to feel this morning.

Cordelia didn't look back at the building. She'd spent four days memorizing every inch of it from the inside, and she had no further use for the view from outside, and no need to see it again in any capacity at all.

"Oh-six-hundred," Creed said, checking the sky, still dark, still nearly an hour off from the deadline they'd all been building toward. "We're early."

"We had good information," Barkley said, favoring her side, already moving toward the waiting vehicle with the relief of a woman who'd just kept a promise to herself about getting home to someone.

Cordelia stopped at the tree line for one moment, breathing the actual outside air for the first time in four days, cold and real and nothing at all like the recycled hospital air she'd been living in, and thought, with the fierce clarity of someone who'd just walked out of the worst four days of her life under her own power, about a boy in a hospital bed running hardware checks he didn't know he was running, and a corkboard on a dining room wall she'd never seen but was, finally, about to.

"Let's go home," she said, and the three of them moved together into the tree line, four days of separate patient work finally, completely, done.

---



Chapter 92: An Offer, Properly Delivered



"An offer, properly made, costs the offerer more than the one receiving it ever fully knows." — attributed to Peter Pan

Tuesday, November 4, 1997: 1630 Revello Drive, Sunnydale,
Early Morning 6:45am —

Joyce Summers had a very firm policy about who was allowed to knock on her door before seven in the morning, and that policy did not, under any previously established circumstance, include a boy in green who appeared to be somewhere between fourteen and several centuries old, standing on her porch with a girl beside him and a small, extremely serious expression that suggested he'd rehearsed this greeting more than once.

"Mrs. Summers," Peter said. "I'm sorry for the hour. I wanted to come before the day got started, when things might be — busier."

Joyce looked at him. At the girl beside him, Wendy, who had the composed patience of someone who'd talked her friend out of at least two worse opening lines on the walk over. At the general, undeniable strangeness of two Lost Boys standing politely on her porch at quarter to seven in the morning, one of them holding what appeared to be a slightly bruised gift basket of wild blackberries.

"You're the boy from the amusement park," Joyce said. Not unkindly. Simply establishing facts, the way she'd learned to do with almost everything since October 31st.

"Peter Pan," Peter said. "Formerly Danny Osbourne. Currently — this, I suppose." He held out the blackberries, slightly crushed from the walk. "I brought these. Wendy said arriving somewhere important without bringing anything was poor manners, and she's usually right about that sort of thing."

"He wanted to bring a whole pie," Wendy said, dryly. "I explained that pies don't survive a two-mile walk through brush very well."

Joyce looked at the blackberries. At the two teenagers holding them out with the earnest formality of people who'd clearly practiced this exact moment and were now discovering that practicing a thing and doing it were different exercises entirely.

"Well," she said, "you'd better come in, then. I was just about to start coffee."

---

Buffy came downstairs twelve minutes later, hair still damp, and stopped dead in the kitchen doorway at the sight of Peter Pan sitting at her mother's kitchen table, drinking orange juice out of a glass with a cartoon sun on it, while Joyce cut up the blackberries into a bowl with the unbothered efficiency of a woman who had stopped being surprised by anything that walked through her front door.

"You're up early," Buffy said, to Peter, because it was the only sentence available that didn't require immediately unpacking the fact that this was happening at all.

"I wanted to catch you before the day started," Peter said. "Before things got — busy. I understand your Board has a lot happening right now."

Buffy's eyebrows went up slightly at "the Board," which had apparently made it out of last night's conference room and into general circulation faster than she'd expected. "You've been talking to Pooka."

"She found me first, actually," Peter said. "Very direct. Asked if we had food before she asked anything else, which I found reassuring — anyone whose first priority is snacks generally turns out to be trustworthy, in my experience." He set the orange juice down. "I want to offer something. Properly, this time. Not the way I offered it after your people beat us at the park — that offer had a lot of wounded pride mixed into it, and I don't think it landed the way I meant it to."

"What's the offer," Buffy said, sitting down across from him, Wendy settling beside Peter with the watchful patience of someone ready to step in if the negotiation needed steadying.

"We know this town's underside better than anyone your Board currently has access to," Peter said. "Four days, and we've already mapped things you haven't found yet — places where the things that come up out of the dark actually go, tunnels under the old part of town nobody's used in decades, a whole network of spaces too small for adults to move through comfortably but perfectly sized for us." He paused. "We're fast. We're small. Half my crew can go places your soldiers can't fit. I'm not offering fealty — I did enough of that as somebody's idea of a story, I'm not doing it again — but I'm offering cooperation. Real cooperation, on our own terms, in exchange for something."

"In exchange for what."

"Legitimacy," Peter said, and something in his voice went quieter, more honest than the earlier rehearsed formality had allowed. "We're forty runaway kids living in a dead amusement park. I don't want fealty and I don't want charity. I want us to stop being a rumor the adults in this town are frightened of, and start being something that actually matters. A crew people can call on, instead of a story people tell each other to explain why the carnival's still fenced off."

Buffy looked at him for a long moment — at a boy who'd been handed exactly the shape of who he'd always secretly wanted to be, and was now discovering, the same as everyone else in this town since Halloween, that wanting a thing and living inside it forever were different weights entirely.

"There's a meeting every Monday," she said. "A whole board, actually, on my dining room wall, with everybody's business on it — patrol logs, an action column, all of it." She glanced at Joyce, who was still cutting blackberries with the serene patience of a woman who fed anyone who walked through her door regardless of species or century. "I think you should come to the next one. Not to ask permission. To actually be part of building it."

Peter was quiet for a moment, something moving behind his eyes that looked, for the first time since he'd walked through the door, less like careful diplomatic strategy and more like genuine hope.

"I'd like that," he said.

Wendy, beside him, allowed herself the smallest smile, the quiet satisfaction of someone who'd talked her friend into doing the brave, uncertain thing and watched it land better than either of them had dared plan for.

Joyce set the bowl of blackberries down in the middle of the table.

"Well," she said, "in the meantime, you're both welcome to breakfast. I made too much anyway. I always make too much lately." She looked at Peter with the warm, unbothered practicality that had, over the last four days, become the single most reliable constant in this entire strange new world. "You look like you could use a real meal, dear. Being fourteen and also several centuries old is probably exhausting on the appetite."

"You have no idea," Peter said, and reached for the toast.

---

﻿

---



Chapter 93: The Historian Has Feelings About This



"The historian's job isn't to make the record comfortable. It's to make it true." — attributed to Dawn Summers, private notebook

Tuesday, November 4, 1997: 1630 Revello Drive, Sunnydale,
Early Morning 6:50am —

Dawn Summers had eleven notebooks now, which was ten more than she'd started with three days ago, and she'd organized them with a cross-indexing system of her own invention that involved colored tabs, a master index in the front cover of notebook one, and a set of abbreviations that made perfect sense to her and absolutely none to anyone who tried to read them without her personal decoder key, which she had decided, privately, was probably the point.

She was awake at six-fifty in the morning, which her mother did not yet know, because Joyce was downstairs discovering that a boy named Peter Pan apparently liked his orange juice served in the cup with the cartoon sun on it, a discovery Dawn had made from the top of the stairs and filed away without comment, because not everything needed a notebook entry, and some things just needed to be enjoyed quietly from a safe eavesdropping distance.

She had a different notebook open now — not the operational one, not the one with patrol schedules and the MHMR floor plan sketch and the training rotation. This one was smaller, plainer, kept separate from the others in a way she hadn't fully admitted to herself was deliberate until just this moment, sitting cross-legged on her bedroom floor in the pale early light, pen hovering over a blank page.

*Things I actually feel about all of this,* she'd written, three days ago, at the top of the first page, and had not added anything else since, because writing the header had used up whatever courage she'd had available that particular night.

She looked at it now.

The thing nobody quite understood about being the record-keeper — and she'd started to suspect, over the last three days, that even Buffy didn't fully understand it, despite loving her, despite checking in on her constantly in the careful way of an older sister trying very hard to do right by a situation she hadn't asked for either — was that keeping the records meant she saw everything. All of it. Every casualty number Benjy reported honestly instead of rounding down. Every gap in Jonathan's column. Every quiet, private moment of fear that got mentioned once, in passing, and then filed, and then never discussed again because everyone had somewhere urgent to be.

She was eleven years old, and she had, in the last three days, transcribed more genuine terror than most adults encountered in a lifetime, and she had done it calmly, competently, with good handwriting, because that was her job, and she was good at her job, and being good at her job meant nobody worried about whether the job was costing her anything.

She picked up the pen.

*I'm scared all the time now,* she wrote, slowly, the words coming easier once the first one landed on the page. *Not the regular scared. Not homework scared or Buffy's-going-to-be-late scared. The other kind. The kind where I write down that four kids in the hallway almost died and I have to keep my handwriting neat while I do it, because if my handwriting gets messy someone might notice, and then they'll ask if I'm okay, and I don't actually know how to answer that question honestly without everyone deciding I'm too young to keep doing this, and I don't want them to decide that, because this is the one thing I'm actually good at, the one thing that's mine, and I don't want to lose it just because I'm also scared.*

She stopped. Read it back. Felt something in her chest loosen slightly, the way it sometimes did after she cried, except she hadn't cried, she'd just written the true thing down instead, which felt, unexpectedly, like almost the same kind of relief.

There was a soft knock at her door.

"It's me," Stephanie said, from the hallway — Cap, mostly, to everyone else, but Stephanie to Dawn, because Dawn had decided early on that she wasn't going to call an eleven-year-old her own age by a superhero nickname just because the rest of the household had settled into it. "Can I come in?"

"Yeah."

Stephanie came in, sat down cross-legged across from her with the careful economy of someone who'd learned, seventy years ago in a body she hadn't yet grown into, to always check a room before fully entering it. Her eyes went, briefly, to the notebook, to the private one, and then, deliberately, away from it, respecting the boundary without needing to be asked.

"You're up early," Stephanie said.

"So are you."

"I don't really sleep the normal amount," Stephanie said. "Never have, since. It's a whole thing." She looked at Dawn steadily. "You've been carrying a lot of other people's hard days in that notebook system of yours. I've been meaning to ask if you've got anywhere to put your own."

Dawn looked at the small plain notebook in her lap, the one with exactly one honest paragraph in it.

"I'm starting one," she said, quiet. "It's not very good yet. I don't really know how to write about myself the way I write about everyone else. I know how to write down what Benjy did in the hallway. I don't really know how to write down what it's like being the person who has to hear about it calmly."

"That's allowed to take practice," Stephanie said. "I've had forty years' worth of memory about how to hold a line under fire. I did not come with any matching memory about how to be an eleven-year-old girl adopted into a house full of magic. Nobody hands you a manual for the second kind." She reached over, gently, and tapped the edge of the small notebook. "For what it's worth — the fact that you're scared and still doing the job perfectly doesn't mean the job isn't costing you something. It just means you're handling the cost quietly, the way people who are actually good at hard things tend to. That's not nothing, Dawn. That's not something to hide so people don't worry."

"But if I tell people, they might—"

"They might worry," Stephanie agreed. "And then they'll probably still let you keep doing the job, because you're the only person in this house who can do it the way you do it, and everyone here has finally figured out, this week especially, that treating people like they're too fragile for the truth doesn't actually protect them. It just leaves them carrying it alone." She looked at Dawn steadily. "You don't have to carry this one alone either. Even the historian's allowed a witness."

Dawn looked at her notebook — at the one true paragraph, at the blank page waiting underneath it — and picked the pen back up.

*Stephanie says even the historian's allowed a witness,* she wrote, carefully, her handwriting steady again. *I think I believe her. I'm going to try believing her, anyway. That feels like a start.*

Downstairs, faintly, she could hear Peter Pan asking Joyce whether it would be terribly rude to have a second helping of blackberries, and her mother's warm, unbothered laugh answering him, and Dawn sat with her small private notebook and her one true page and felt, for the first time in three days, like the fear and the job might actually be able to sit in the same room together without either one having to lose.

---

﻿

Chapter 94: Turning the Mirror Back Around



"Turn the mirror around occasionally, or you'll forget what it's actually reflecting." — attributed to Angel

Tuesday, November 4, 1997: The Sunnydale Arms, Room 8,
Sunnydale, Early Morning 6:55am —

Faith Lehane hadn't slept, not really, not the kind of sleep that actually rested you, but she'd lain still on the bed for several hours with her eyes closed doing something that at least resembled it, running the same conversation back through her head on a loop she hadn't quite managed to turn off since Buffy Summers left her room the night before.

*I don't think that's the same as it being good for you.*

She sat up around six, gave up on the pretense of sleep entirely, and looked at the closet door across the room, the mirror on it still turned to face the wall, same as it had been every night since she'd checked into this room.

She got up. Crossed the small space. Stood in front of the turned mirror for a long moment, hand on the frame, not quite committing yet.

*Diana Dormer,* Buffy had said, gentle, not performing sympathy, just offering the actual name like it deserved to be spoken out loud by someone besides Faith herself. *That's not a small thing to be carrying.*

Faith turned the mirror around.

Her own reflection looked back at her — tired, a little rough around the edges, but steady, in the way she'd worked hard to look steady even when she didn't feel it, because looking steady had kept her alive plenty of times when actual steadiness hadn't been available.

She looked at herself for a long moment, the way she hadn't let herself look in weeks, and thought about Diana Dormer's specific dry patience, her exact way of saying *again, Faith, and this time actually commit to the follow-through* during training, the last real conversation they'd had before the thing that took her out had taken her out, six weeks and a lifetime ago now.

She thought about six weeks of operating alone since, telling herself alone was a preference rather than a wound she hadn't let herself name.

She thought about a girl who'd knocked on her door with her hands visible and empty, who hadn't tried to corner her, who'd just offered a thing and let it sit there without pushing, the way you offer something to someone you've decided is worth being patient with.

Faith reached for the room's ancient rotary phone, found the number Buffy had left with the front desk clerk two hours after that visit — Faith had watched her leave it, through the window, hadn't said anything about noticing — and dialed it before she could talk herself out of it, muttering something under her breath about this being the single most pathetic thing she'd ever done, dialing a number like a nervous kid asking someone to the dance, and doing it anyway.

It rang twice.

"Hello?" Joyce Summers's voice, warm, a little distracted, the tone of a woman who was clearly also managing something else happening in her kitchen at seven in the morning.

"Is, uh." Faith cleared her throat. "Is Buffy around? This is Faith."

"Oh, Faith, hello!" Joyce's voice brightened in a way that caught Faith off guard, warm and immediate and without the wary calculation Faith had half-expected from a Slayer's mother evaluating a stranger. "She just stepped out for a run, but she should be back any minute — can I take a message, or would you like to hold?"

"I can hold," Faith said, surprising herself with how easily it came out.

"Wonderful. And Faith—" Joyce paused, and something in her voice went gentler, more deliberate. "There's a meeting Monday. I don't know if Buffy's told you the details yet, but you're welcome, whenever you're ready. No pressure on the timing. I just wanted you to hear it from me too, in case it helps to know it's not just her asking."

Faith sat with the phone against her ear, in the small quiet room with the mirror finally facing the right direction, and felt something in her chest that had been clenched tight for six weeks loosen, just slightly, just enough to notice.

"Yeah," she said, quiet. "Yeah, that — that actually helps a lot, Mrs. Summers. Thank you."

"Joyce," Joyce said, warm. "Please. And here she is now — hold on, dear, I've got Faith on the phone for you."

There was a rustle, a brief muffled exchange, and then Buffy's voice, slightly breathless from the run, surprised and, underneath the surprise, clearly pleased.

"Faith? Hey — everything okay?"

Faith looked at her own steady reflection in the turned-around mirror, and found, for the first time in six weeks, that she actually believed what she was about to say.

"Yeah," she said. "Everything's okay. I've been thinking about what you said. About not doing this alone." A pause, the brave pause of someone committing to a thing out loud before they could talk themselves back out of it. "I think I'd like to come to that meeting. If the offer's still good."

"It's still good," Buffy said, and there was real warmth in it, no performance, no relief-tinged-with-management, just genuine gladness. "It's always going to be good, Faith. Whenever you're ready."

"I'm ready," Faith said, and meant it, and felt, for the first time in six weeks, like maybe alone had never actually been the safest place to be. It had just been the only door she'd known how to open by herself.

---



Chapter 95: Home By Morning



"The mission's not actually over when the objective's secured. It's over when the people who were waiting stop counting."
— Murphy's Laws of Combat

Tuesday, November 4, 1997: Rendezvous Point, Tree Line off Topanga Street,
Sunnydale, Early Morning 5:19am —

Merrill had been staring at the tree line for eleven straight minutes without blinking more than the job strictly required, and Moseby, beside him in the parked van, had noticed, and had decided, with her usual pale-eyed patience, not to comment on it, because some things a person needed to do without being teased about doing them.

"Movement," Merrill said, quiet, the second he had it.

Three shapes came out of the dark tree line at a fast, controlled pace — not running, not quite, but moving with the economical urgency of people who'd spent four days learning exactly how much haste a situation actually required and no more.

Cordelia Chase came through first, barefoot on the cold ground, wrapped in improvised layers that had clearly been assembled from whatever four days of patient hoarding could produce, and Merrill was out of the van and moving toward her before he'd fully registered making the decision.

"Miss Chase," he said, and found, to his own mild surprise, that his voice wasn't steady. "Welcome back."

"Thank you," Cordelia said, and there was something in her face — steady, changed, older than four days should have made anyone, but steady — that told Merrill this conversation was going to need considerably more care than a simple welcome, later, when there was time for it.

Creed came through next, unhurried, unbothered by the cold or the hour or anything else, and Barkley behind him, favoring her left side, and the moment Barkley cleared the tree line Moseby was out of the van at a dead run, all pale-eyed professional patience gone in an instant.

"Keisha," Moseby said, and there wasn't anything careful or controlled left in her voice at all.

"Jan." Barkley's own voice cracked, just slightly, four days of controlled soldier's composure finally, briefly, allowed to slip. "Told you I'd—"

She didn't finish the sentence, because Moseby had already closed the distance and had both arms around her, careful of the injured side, and for a moment neither of them said anything else at all, just held on, four days of separation collapsing into a single held breath.

Merrill looked away, giving them the moment, and found Cordelia watching the same scene with an expression that held something complicated — genuine warmth for two people whose reunion she'd apparently helped make possible, tangled up with something else, something Merrill recognized from his own combat tours as the weight of a person who'd just done something that morning she hadn't fully processed yet. He didn't say anything about it directly. Twenty years in this line of work had taught him that the gentlest thing a man built like him could do was not draw attention to a moment by commenting on it.

"C'mon, kid," he said instead, gruff, understated, the words landing with more warmth than the delivery let on. "Doc's got a room ready. And there's a boy been on the phone with the Captain most of the night who isn't gonna believe you're actually standing here till he sees it with his own two eyes." He fell into step beside her, matching his stride to hers without seeming to notice he was doing it, moving with the quiet economy of a very large man who'd spent a career learning not to announce himself.

Something in Cordelia's face shifted at that — the complicated weight easing, just slightly, replaced by something that looked, for the first time all morning, uncomplicatedly like relief.

"Yeah," she said. "Let's go."

---

Tuesday, November 4, 1997: South Marion Drive, Sunnydale Medical Complex, Room 412,
Sunnydale, Early Morning 5:35am —

Xander had not slept, despite Aura's repeated and increasingly creative attempts to convince him otherwise, and he was staring at the water stain with the fixed intensity of a man doing math on a clock that had, twenty-five minutes ago, dropped below thirty minutes remaining and had started, in his head, to feel like the longest thirty minutes of his entire life.

"You should—"

"Don't say sleep, Aura."

"I was going to say breathe," Aura said, mildly, though the quality of her own stillness suggested she was doing exactly as much counting as he was. "You've been holding it for the last four minutes."

The phone on the side table rang.

Xander had it against his ear before the second ring finished.

"Harris."

"She's clear." Michaela's voice, and there was something in it — controlled, professional, but with a warmth underneath the control that Xander had learned, over four days, to recognize as genuine — that told him everything before she'd finished the sentence. "Chase, Carmichael, and Barkley are all out and accounted for. Extraction team confirms no injuries requiring immediate concern beyond what Barkley was already carrying. They're en route to you now."

Xander sat very still for a moment, the water stain blurring slightly at the edges in a way that had nothing to do with the ceiling.

"She's okay," he said, not quite a question, needing to hear it said plainly one more time.

"She's okay, Xander," Michaela said, gentler now. "And from what I'm hearing secondhand, more than okay. She'll tell you the rest herself. ETA fifteen minutes."

He hung up. Sat with it for a moment, the enormous relief of four days finally, actually ending, and found Aura watching him with her notebook forgotten in her lap for the first time since November first.

"She's clear," he said.

"I heard your half of it," Aura said, and something in her carefully composed face cracked, just slightly, into something unguarded and glad. "Fifteen minutes."

"Fifteen minutes," Xander agreed, and looked at the door, and found that for the first time in four days, the water stain had absolutely nothing left to offer him that mattered more than the door actually opening.

---

Tuesday, November 4, 1997: South Marion Drive, Sunnydale Medical Complex, Room 412,
Sunnydale, Early Morning 5:52am —

The door opened at 5:52am, seventeen minutes later than Michaela's estimate, and Cordelia Chase stood in the doorway in borrowed layers and bare feet, hair a mess, four days etched into her face in ways that would take longer than a morning to fully soften — and looked, for one long, suspended moment, exactly like every single thing Xander had spent four days telling himself she'd be, and something more besides, something steadier and harder-won than he'd let himself fully imagine.

"Hi," she said.

"Hi," Xander said, and found he couldn't manage anything more sophisticated than that single word, because everything else he'd been saving up — the seventeen things, the nineteen things, all of it — had evaporated on contact with the simple, overwhelming fact of her actually standing there.

She crossed the room. He was already moving too, despite four days of careful medical instruction about not straining anything, and they met somewhere in the middle, and for a long moment neither of them said anything at all, just held on, the way you hold onto something you'd genuinely, quietly feared you might not get to hold onto again.

"You're home by morning," he said, finally, into her hair.

"Told you I would be," Cordelia said, muffled against his shoulder, and there was something fierce and grateful and entirely herself in it. "You should've believed me faster."

"I believed you the whole time," Xander said. "I just also had contingency plans, because you'd have been furious with me if I hadn't."

"I would have been," Cordelia agreed, and pulled back just far enough to look at him properly, at the careful bandaging, at the exhausted steadiness in his face that matched, precisely, the exhausted steadiness she suspected was on her own. "You look terrible."

"You look amazing, considering."

"I look like I've been living in a paper gown for four days."

"You look like someone who got herself and two other people out of a federal black site," Xander said, quiet, serious now. "That's not amazing, Cordelia. That's extraordinary. I need you to actually hear that, not just deflect it."

Cordelia looked at him for a long moment, something working behind her eyes — the weight of what the last four days had actually cost her, of what she'd done in that office at five in the morning, of things she wasn't ready to say out loud yet and might not be for a while.

"I will," she said, quiet. "Just — not this second. This second I just want to sit down somewhere that isn't a cell, and eat something that isn't hospital food, and hear about this corkboard everyone apparently decided to name a whole organization after while I was gone."

"The Board," Xander said, and something warm and genuine broke through the exhaustion in his voice. "You're going to have opinions."

"I already have opinions," Cordelia said, settling carefully onto the edge of his bed, close, steady, four days finally, completely behind her. "I've had four days to develop them. Buckle up."

Aura, quietly, in the corner, opened her notebook and wrote down the time — 5:52am, the exact moment the log finally, mercifully, had good news to record — and let the two of them have the rest of the morning without further comment, because some things, even for a woman who'd made logging into a discipline, simply didn't need writing down to matter.

---

Chapter 96: The Full Debrief



"The truth doesn't need dressing up. It just needs someone willing to sit still and actually hear it." — attributed to Michaela Reeves

Tuesday, November 4, 1997: South Marion Drive, Sunnydale Medical Complex, Conference Room B,
Sunnydale, Morning 9:00am —

Michaela had called the debrief for nine, which gave Cordelia three hours to eat something real, sleep for exactly none of them despite everyone's insistence, and change out of the borrowed layers into actual clothes Aura had thoughtfully brought from home — and she sat now at the head of the same conference table where, less than twelve hours ago, an entire town's worth of strange allies had first assembled, and found the room easier to command from this side of it. Somewhere in the way her spine settled into the chair — shoulders back, eyes already mapping the room's exits before anyone had said a word — was a posture she'd learned from a man who wasn't in the room to see her use it properly for the first time, and she noticed the thought arrive, let it sit exactly as long as it needed to, and set it down again without comment, the way she'd gotten good at doing with most things this week.

"Start wherever makes sense," Michaela said, gently. "Take whatever time you need."

Cordelia looked around the table — Giles, Zabuto, Brockhurst, Buffy, Aura with her notebook already open, Xander beside her holding her hand under the table in a way that wasn't remotely subtle and that nobody in the room felt any need to comment on.

"Walsh is dead," she said, plainly, no preamble, because four days had taught her that softening a fact didn't actually change its weight, only delayed people's reckoning with it. "I did it myself. Creed and Barkley were there. I want that on the record clearly, because I'm not interested in anyone constructing a version of this morning where I was protected from the hard part."

The room was quiet for a moment. Giles's expression held something complicated — concern, respect, the careful weight of a man who'd watched enough young people carry impossible burdens to know better than to rush past this part.

"How are you," he said, simply.

"I don't fully know yet," Cordelia said, honestly. "I know it's not going to be a clean answer, and I know I'm not going to have it today. But I did what I decided needed doing, with clear eyes, and I'm not going to pretend otherwise to make anyone else more comfortable." She looked at the table. "The archive drive's in my bag. Full backup of Walsh's DRI program — Nevada facility, subject files, everything. There's a name in there — Rona Williams — that I think matters enough to need its own conversation, but not today. Today I just want the record straight."

"Understood," Michaela said, and there was real respect in it. "Thank you for the clarity. We'll handle the rest."

Buffy reached across and squeezed Cordelia's free hand, briefly, and Cordelia let her, which was, in itself, a small quiet acknowledgment of exactly how much the last four days had changed what she was willing to accept from people.

"Welcome to the Board," Buffy said, quiet.

"I heard about the name," Cordelia said, and something almost like her old self, sharp and dry, surfaced through the exhaustion. "I have opinions about it."

"Everyone has opinions about it," Giles said. "It's proven remarkably durable regardless."

Cordelia looked around the room one more time — at the people who'd spent four days building something around the hole she'd left, and were now, quietly, making room for her to fill it back in on her own terms.

"Good," she said. "Then let's get to work."

Chapter 97: First Meeting



"First impressions are just data collected before you had enough information to be fair." — attributed to Zabuto

Monday, November 10, 1997: 1630 Revello Drive, Sunnydale,
Evening 6:55pm —

Kendra Young stood on the Summers' front porch in the gathering dark, hands folded neatly in front of her, and found herself doing the thing Zabuto had gently teased her about twice already this week, which was rehearsing, silently, exactly how she intended to introduce herself, as though a Slayer walking into a room for the first time required a formal briefing rather than simply a knock.

Zabuto, beside her, seemed to sense the hesitation. "You may simply say hello, Kendra. It is generally sufficient."

"I am aware of the appropriate social protocol, Mr. Zabuto."

"I did not doubt it," he said, mild. "I merely observed you had been standing on this porch for ninety seconds without knocking."

Kendra knocked.

Dawn answered, notebook already in hand, and broke into a genuine, unguarded smile the moment she registered who was standing there. "You came! Buffy said you might, but she wasn't sure, and I told her you seemed like the kind of person who follows through on things once you decide them, and I was right, wasn't I—"

"Dawn," Buffy said, from somewhere behind her, amused. "Let her actually get inside first."

The dining room held more people than Kendra had expected — the actual corkboard on the wall, exactly as described, covered in patrol logs and a section labeled ACTION ITEMS in careful handwriting; Faith, arrived only slightly before her, looking steadier than the rumors Kendra had heard suggested; Jonathan and Tamara, deep in quiet conversation over a legal pad; Warren and Seven, sharing a single chair with the comfortable economy of two people who'd stopped needing personal space from each other some time ago.

"Kendra," Buffy said, warm, crossing to actually greet her properly. "I'm really glad you came."

"I did not wish to be the only Slayer in this town who remained apart from what you have built," Kendra said, formal, and then, catching herself, added, more simply, "Also, I wished to see the board. Mr. Zabuto has spoken of it with some skepticism."

"It's just a corkboard," Faith said, from across the room, dry. "Don't let 'em oversell it."

"It is more than a corkboard," Zabuto said, mild, following Kendra in and surveying the room with the formal thoroughness he brought to everything. "I have observed this town's operations for two weeks now, and I confess I find the informality somewhat unnerving."

"Give it a meeting," Giles said, arriving with a stack of files under one arm. "It generally wins people over by the second hour."

Kendra found a seat, carefully, at the edge of the table, and watched the room organize itself into something that wasn't quite a briefing and wasn't quite a family dinner and seemed to be, somehow, both at once — Joyce moving through with a tray of something that smelled extraordinary, Dawn updating the board with careful new entries, Faith and Buffy trading easy, careful banter that suggested six weeks of guardedness were slowly, patiently thawing.

"You are welcome to add an item," Dawn said, appearing at her elbow with a marker held out. "To the board. Everyone gets to. That's sort of the whole point."

Kendra looked at the marker. At the board. At sixteen years of training that had never once suggested a Slayer might be handed a marker and invited to simply contribute.

She took it.

"What ought I write," she said.

"Whatever's actually true," Dawn said, simply. "That's usually the right answer."

Kendra considered this, and then wrote, in careful, deliberate letters, beneath the existing entries: *Kendra Young — available for patrol, training, and consultation on Watcher Council protocols. Present and glad to be here.*

She stepped back, looked at it, and found, for the first time in sixteen years of training to be a weapon, that being simply present and glad to be somewhere felt like an legitimate entry to have made.

"Good addition," Buffy said, warm, reading over her shoulder.

"Yes," Kendra said, and found she meant it completely. "I believe it is."

Chapter 98: What Landlorn Actually Wanted



"An old admiral's curiosity is more dangerous than his anger." — attributed to Admiral Landlorn

Somewhere in the Never-After, aboard the Retribution,
Same non-time —

Marcus Okafor had been talking for what felt like hours, though the Never-After's indifference to actual time made that estimate essentially meaningless, and Admiral Landlorn had listened to all of it — names, structure, the Board's rough shape as Marcus understood it from overheard fragments and careful deduction — with the patient stillness of something that had learned, centuries ago, that the best information came from letting a person talk themselves all the way to the bottom of what they knew.

"That's everything," Marcus said, finally. "Everything I've got, at least. I don't know their full org chart. I know there's a formation — sorry, apparently they renamed it, something with a board — building real capability in that town, and I know it's bigger and stranger than a Hellmouth usually produces."

Landlorn was quiet for a long moment, turning the information over with the unhurried consideration of a creature who genuinely had all the time this place would ever grant him.

"You've been remarkably forthcoming," Landlorn said. "For a boy negotiating his own freedom."

"I figured honesty was the actual leverage," Marcus said. "You've got centuries of experience reading people who lie to you for advantage. I didn't think I'd out-bluff that. So I didn't try."

Something that might, on a less ancient face, have been genuine respect moved behind Landlorn's eyes. "A wise calculation." He stood, slowly, the unhurried motion of something that had stopped needing to rush toward any decision centuries ago. "I find myself curious what you intend to do with this information, once you're home."

"Warn them," Marcus said, simply. "That the walls are thinner than they think. That something's bleeding through where it shouldn't be, and whatever's causing it needs looking at before worse things than me start falling through by accident."

"A civic-minded answer."

"A practical one," Marcus said. "I don't especially want to end up back in your hold because nobody in Sunnydale thought to check whether the town's gotten leaky."

Landlorn considered this for a long moment, and then, with the ceremonial formality of a creature that took its own word seriously precisely because the Never-After required it, extended one hand.

"I will open you a door," he said. "As promised. And I will ensure nothing on this ship considers you leverage on the way through it." A pause. "I find myself, additionally, curious about your Board's activities. Should you find reason, at some point, to establish a channel of communication between this vessel and that town — I would not consider the prospect uninteresting."

Marcus looked at him for a long moment. "You want an open line to Sunnydale."

"I want to know what's causing my walls to thin," Landlorn said, simply. "That's not a threat, boy. That's self-interest, plainly stated. Whatever's happening in your town affects my domain as much as yours. I would rather know about it early than discover it the hard way, the way ancient things generally prefer to discover unpleasant surprises."

Marcus considered this, weighing it the same careful way he'd weighed every decision since falling through that door three days ago.

"I'll pass the message along," he said, finally. "No promises what they do with it. But I'll pass it along honestly, same as I told you everything else."

"That," Landlorn said, "is all I would ever reasonably ask of anyone."

He gestured, and somewhere behind Marcus, the air folded in on itself, a door opening where no door had been a moment before, cold Sunnydale night air spilling through the gap.

"Go home, Marcus Okafor," Landlorn said. "And should the day come that your Board wishes to speak with something considerably older than themselves — you may tell them the door remains open, on my end, whenever they're ready to use it."

Marcus stepped through, into familiar cold November air, and turned back just once before the door closed behind him, to find Landlorn watching him go with an expression that might, on anything else, have been called almost fond.

Then the door was gone, and Marcus Okafor stood alone in a dark, ordinary Sunnydale street, three days older and more informed than the boy who'd fallen through a hole in reality, and started, immediately, walking toward the only address in town he was fairly sure would actually believe his story.

Chapter 99: Old Money, New Paperwork



"New paperwork is simply old power wearing a disguise it hasn't fully learned to wear yet." — attributed to Veronique de Alençon

Wednesday, November 5, 1997: Squisher's Basement, Sunnydale,
Night 11:15pm —

Squisher's basement smelled the way it had smelled for as long as anyone currently drawing breath in Sunnydale could remember, which was to say it smelled like old stone, older secrets, and whatever the current management was using to mask both, and Veronique de Alençon found, three days into actually doing business here, that she'd developed a genuine fondness for the consistency of the smell, the way one grows fond of any environment that reliably tells the truth about itself.

"The terms," said the creature across the table — old, formless in the way that made assigning it a gender or even a species feel presumptuous, known to the room only as the Ledger, because it kept Squisher's actual books and had done so, by all accounts, since before the building had walls — "are seven percent, compounded at the old rate, against the manor's eventual revenue. Standard terms, for old blood re-establishing a house."

"Seven percent at the old rate is closer to eleven by modern compounding conventions," Veronique said, without heat, simply stating the correction the way one corrects a minor arithmetic error. "I am aware what the old rate actually compounds to, Ledger. I have paid old rates before. Several times, in several centuries."

Something that might have been amusement rippled through the Ledger's formless bulk. "You are better informed than most who walk in seeking credit."

"I have eight hundred years of informing myself," Veronique said. "I intend to continue the habit."

Blahd, beside her, said nothing, content to let her run this negotiation, because they'd agreed early — this was her specific expertise, financial architecture across centuries, in a way his own considerable strengths didn't especially cover.

"Six percent," Veronique said. "At the old rate. Compounded annually, not monthly, given the manor's renovation timeline requires patience before it produces revenue at all."

The Ledger considered this with the unhurried patience of something for whom negotiation was simply another form of conversation, unbounded by mortal urgency.

"Six and a half," it said. "And information sharing, as previously discussed. Trask's movements. The formation's — apologies, the Board's — activities, as you observe them."

"We will share what serves mutual interest," Veronique said. "Not everything, unconditionally. I did not survive eight centuries by handing over unlimited intelligence to every creditor who asked for it."

"Fair," the Ledger allowed. "Six and a half, then. Information as it serves us both."

Veronique extended a hand — an old gesture, from a older century, that the Ledger apparently still recognized, because it extended something that might have been a hand in return, and the deal was sealed with the ceremonial weight that old things brought to agreements they intended to honor.

"The manor renovation begins in earnest tomorrow," Blahd said, once they'd stepped back out into the cool November night. "The plasterers arrive at eight."

"Good," Veronique said, and something in her voice held real satisfaction, the pleasure of a problem solved cleanly rather than merely survived. "I confess I had not expected to feel quite this invested in a nightclub renovation, given everything else currently occupying this town's attention."

"You've built six residences across four centuries," Blahd said. "I imagine the habit simply persists, regardless of context."

"Perhaps." Veronique looked up at the stars, at the ordinary November sky over a town that had, this week alone, hosted a Terminator's redemption, a federal black site's collapse, and a pixie-brokered alliance with the Lost Boys. "Or perhaps I simply find myself, for the first time in some centuries, curious what happens next. That's not nothing, Savros. That's rather the opposite of the exhaustion I've been carrying since roughly the Restoration."

Blahd looked at her, something old and considering in his expression.

"No," he agreed, quietly. "It's rather the opposite indeed."

They walked back toward Crawford Street together, nine and eight centuries of accumulated weariness both, unexpectedly, feeling just slightly lighter than they had in longer than either of them cared to calculate.

Chapter 100: Seventy-Eight Percent Becomes One Hundred



"The last twenty-two percent is always the part that costs the most." — attributed to Seven of Nine, private log

Thursday, November 6, 1997: Mears Residence, Guava Drive,
Sunnydale, Evening 8:30pm —

The regeneration module hummed, very quietly, the low frequency hum that Warren had learned, over the past nine days, to recognize as correct rather than concerning, and Seven sat within its housing with her eyes closed, running diagnostic sweeps across each restored implant node in sequence, silent and methodical.

Warren watched the readouts. One hundred percent completion, confirmed across every subsystem, the culmination of nine days of theoretical work drawn from a dead genius's borrowed memory and built, by hand, in a bedroom that had never been designed for this kind of engineering.

"Status," he said, quiet, unable to fully keep the tension out of his voice.

Seven opened her eyes. The optical implant's calibration had shifted, subtly, something in its light catching differently now than it had nine days ago.

"All systems nominal," she said. "Implant degradation has been fully arrested. Projected functional lifespan, with continued module access, is effectively indefinite." A pause, and something in her flat delivery carried, unmistakably now after nine days of Warren learning her registers, genuine relief. "The module functions correctly, Warren."

"You're sure."

"I have run the diagnostic sweep four times," Seven said. "I do not run diagnostic sweeps four times unless the result is important to me." She looked at him steadily. "This result is important to me."

Warren felt something in his own chest unclench that he hadn't fully realized he'd been holding since the project began. "We did it."

"You did a considerable portion of it," Seven said. "Dr. Soong's theoretical architecture provided the foundation. My engineering knowledge provided the practical execution. The collaboration was, empirically, the necessary combination." She stepped out of the housing, steady, sure, moving with none of the careful conservation of function she'd been quietly practicing for the last several weeks. "I feel — I am uncertain of the precise term for what I currently feel."

"Try anyway," Warren said, gently.

Seven considered this seriously, the way she considered everything. "Secure," she said, finally. "Not merely functional. Secure, in a way I do not believe I have experienced since before my initial assimilation." She looked at him. "That is, I believe, primarily attributable to you."

Warren felt his ears do the thing again, the thing that had nothing to do with borrowed genius and everything to do with being seventeen and hearing something真 true and enormous said plainly.

"I'm really glad," he said, simply. "That you're secure. That's — that's the whole thing I wanted, honestly. More than the engineering being cool, which it is, obviously, but. You. Secure. That's the actual goal."

Seven crossed the small distance between them and did something she rarely initiated first — reached out and took his hand, deliberate, precise, the way she did everything, except this time the precision felt, unmistakably, like tenderness rather than calculation.

"Thank you," she said, simply.

"You don't have to thank me for wanting you okay."

"I am aware I do not have to," Seven said. "I am choosing to regardless, because I find the distinction between obligation and choice increasingly important to maintain, the longer I spend being a person rather than merely a drone." She looked at their joined hands. "This is another thing I am choosing. I want that noted clearly."

"Noted," Warren said, and something in his chest, unrelated to any borrowed genius, simply, quietly, ached with how much he meant it.

Outside, the November evening carried on, ordinary and unaware, while in a bedroom on Guava Drive, a regeneration module hummed at one hundred percent completion, and two people who'd found each other through the strangest possible circumstance sat together in the quiet, certain, for the first time in either of their complicated lives, that this thing was exactly right.
