Chapter 101: The Writs Nobody Needed Anymore



"A debt forgiven is not the same as a debt forgotten. It is, if anything, a debt remembered more carefully." — attributed to Squisher's Ledger, internal record

Tuesday, November 4, 1997: Sunnydale MHMR, Topanga Street,
Sunnydale, Morning 8:00am —

The federal process server arrived at Sunnydale MHMR at exactly eight o'clock in the morning, writs in hand, expecting the bureaucratic resistance that came standard with serving papers on a facility used to operating outside ordinary oversight, and found instead a building in visible, unmanaged disarray — staff clustered in the parking lot in small shocked knots, a security supervisor on his radio with the pitched urgency of a man discovering his chain of command had a hole in it nobody had briefed him on, and no Dr. Maggie Walsh anywhere to be found to receive service at all.

"She's not answering her office," the security supervisor said, to no one in particular, for perhaps the fourth time in ten minutes. "She's not answering her cell. Her car's still in the lot."

By the time Sunnydale PD's patrol units arrived to "assist" — three squad cars' worth of local officers with more curiosity than jurisdiction — the confusion had a real chance of turning into exactly the kind of digging nobody on the Board could afford, right up until a CHP cruiser pulled into the lot behind them and Sergeant Burroughs got out, unhurried, filling the doorway of the vehicle the way he filled most doorways, Officer Gomez climbing out the passenger side a beat behind him with the unbothered calm of someone who had long since stopped finding her partner's entrances remarkable.

"Gentlemen," Burroughs said, to the assembled SPD officers, in the mild tone Giles had once heard him use on a room full of people he had absolutely no intention of losing an argument with. "This facility's got federal paper coming due this morning, which makes it CHP and federal business, not city business, until somebody with more stars on their shoulder than either of us tells you different. I'd appreciate you securing the perimeter and leaving the actual investigating to people who won't step on evidence that federal attorneys are gonna want intact."

"Since when is a psychiatric facility CHP jurisdiction, Burroughs?"

"Since about six this morning, when a highway patrol officer — me — got a call about irregular activity connected to a case I'm already working." He said it without a flicker of hesitation, the practiced ease of a man who'd learned, across a long career, exactly how much institutional truth a sentence needed to hold up without technically lying. "You want to argue jurisdiction with me, take it up with your captain and mine. In the meantime, nobody local touches that building's internal records till the feds get here. Copy?"

"And before anybody gets clever about 'securing the perimeter' meaning wandering inside to take a look," Gomez added, mild, not looking up from where she was writing something in a small notebook of her own, "I'll remind you that tampering with evidence in a case under federal review is exactly the kind of thing that ends careers a great deal faster than standing outside in the cold ever will. Just so everybody's motivated for the right reasons."

The SPD officers copied, grumbling, and fell back to perimeter duty, and Burroughs caught Michaela's eye across the lot — she'd arrived twenty minutes earlier, quiet and unofficial, exactly the way she preferred it — and gave her the smallest possible nod, the acknowledgment of two professionals who'd just run the same play without needing to discuss it beforehand.

"Appreciate the assist, Sergeant," she said, once the locals were out of earshot.

"Wasn't for you," Burroughs said, gruff. "Girl I've got a personal interest in handed over evidence connected to this building. I'd just as soon the actual investigation stay clean enough that nobody local decides Cordelia Chase's name belongs in a report that isn't sealed six ways from Sunday." He looked at the building a long moment. "Whatever really happened here overnight, I don't imagine I want the details. But I know enough to know it needed happening, and I know enough to keep the wrong people from asking questions about it before the right people get a chance to bury it properly."

"He means that," Gomez said, dry, finally looking up from her notebook. "I've worked with him three years now. He's got a very short list of things he'll actually go to bat for. That girl's been on it since October, whether she knows it or not."

The process server, who had served papers on stranger facilities than this one over a fourteen-year career and had developed, as a professional survival skill, an instinct for when a situation had already moved past the paperwork stage, quietly re-holstered the writs, made a phone call of his own, and decided that whatever had actually happened at this facility overnight was, decisively, someone else's jurisdiction now.

By nine-thirty, when Michaela Reeves's own quiet, carefully worded call came through to the appropriate federal contacts — informing them, with the measured neutrality of a woman who'd spent considerable effort deciding exactly how much of the truth this particular conversation actually needed, that Dr. Walsh appeared to have "left her post under circumstances requiring further investigation" — the DRI program's collapse had already begun accelerating under its own bureaucratic weight, writs rendered entirely moot by a vacancy nobody official had yet been told the real reason for.

Giles, reading the initial reports over coffee in Chestnut Street's back office, set the paper down and looked at Sheila across the room.

"It's over," he said, quiet. "The program. Whatever comes next for that facility, it won't be Walsh's."

"And the archive," Sheila said.

"Secured. Cordelia's given us everything." Giles rubbed his eyes, the weariness of a man who'd been awake longer than was wise and had a great deal more still to process before the day was done. "There's a girl in Nevada, apparently, who deserves an entire conversation of her own. Later. Not today."

"Later," Sheila agreed, and poured him more coffee, because that, this morning, was the only immediately useful thing either of them had left to offer.

---



Chapter 102: The Responsible Version of the Story



"The truth told carefully still costs you something. The truth told carelessly costs everyone."
— Murphy's Laws of Combat

Tuesday, November 4, 1997: The Sunnydale Motor Inn, Room 14,
Sunnydale, Afternoon 2:00pm —

Carl Kolchak had spent the better part of the day turning Michaela Reeves's proposal over in his head, and Perry White, watching him do it from the other bed, had finally run out of patience with the quality of silence a man produces when he's decided something and is simply working up the nerve to say it out loud.

"Spit it out, Carl."

"She's offering us something nobody's ever offered me in eleven years of chasing this kind of story," Kolchak said, slowly. "Not suppression. Not a cover story we're supposed to swallow whole. Actual access — controlled, careful, but real — in exchange for running the story the way that protects the kids in it without burying the truth entirely."

"You trust her."

"I trust that she's the first person in this whole ugly business who's ever asked me what I actually wanted the story to accomplish, instead of just telling me what I was allowed to print." Kolchak looked at the paused footage on the little monitor — the gorilla, frozen mid-stride, a town's worth of impossible truth sitting on a single cassette. "Greer's going to be furious we're not running the six-part expose he's already promised the syndication desk."

"Greer can be furious from three thousand miles away," Perry said. "We're the ones who have to look these kids in the eye if we get this wrong." She was quiet for a moment. "I want to write it as a slow build. Not a bombshell. A careful, ongoing account of a town dealing with something strange, told with the dignity real people deserve, not the sensationalism a syndication desk wants." She looked at Kolchak. "You in?"

"I've been chasing the undeniable truth for eleven years, Perry," Kolchak said. "I never once stopped to ask what happens to the people underneath it once I finally catch it." He picked up the phone. "Let's call Reeves back. Let's find out what the responsible version actually looks like."

Jain, in the corner, quietly rewound the tape one more time, and said nothing at all, because some agreements didn't need a cameraman's opinion to be the right ones.

Chapter 103: A Century of Careful Self-Judgment, Reconsidered



"A century spent judging yourself is still, in the end, only a century." — attributed to Angel

Monday, November 10, 1997: 1630 Revello Drive, Sunnydale,
Evening 7:20pm —

Angel stood on the Summers' porch for considerably longer than the walk from his apartment had actually required, working up to the courage a single knock apparently now demanded, before Buffy solved the problem for him by opening the door before he'd managed it.

"You came," she said, and there was real warmth in it, no performance, just genuine gladness.

"I've been standing here for four minutes," Angel admitted. "Working up to it."

"You could have just knocked."

"I know." He looked past her, into the dining room, at the board covered in a week's worth of entries, at Kendra's careful new addition still fresh in the corner, at Faith, arrived early, laughing quietly at something Xander had said. "I wasn't sure I still belonged in that room."

"You don't get to decide that from outside it," Buffy said, gently. "That's kind of the whole point of a room like this. You come in, you see what you've got to offer, and everyone else decides together whether it fits. You don't get to pre-reject yourself on their behalf."

Angel considered this, the century of careful self-judgment sitting a little lighter on his shoulders than it had a week ago.

"Two hundred years of demon hierarchies," he said, finally. "Old debts. Old contacts. If that's actually useful—"

"It's actually useful," Buffy said, and took his hand, gentle, certain, and pulled him through the door before he could find one more reason to hesitate. "Come meet the board, Angel. It's not as scary as you've built it up to be."

Giles looked up as they entered, and something in his expression, careful and measured, resolved into simple welcome.

"Angel," he said. "Good. We could use your specific expertise on the Mayor's older contacts."

Angel looked around the crowded, warm, impossibly strange room — vampires and Watchers and pixies and eleven-year-old First Sergeants, all of it somehow fitting together into something functional — and felt, for the first time in three days, like a man who'd finally stopped hiding from the possibility of belonging somewhere.

"Where do you want me," he said, and sat down.

Chapter 104: A Door That Stays Open



"An open door costs nothing to build and everything to maintain." — attributed to Rupert Giles

Wednesday, November 12, 1997: Chestnut Street Office, Sunnydale,
Evening 6:00pm —

Marcus Okafor had spent three days working up the nerve to walk into an office he'd only heard about secondhand, and Sheila Martini looked up from the maintenance ledger with the mild curiosity of someone who'd learned, over the last two weeks, not to be surprised by who walked through that door anymore.

"Can I help you?"

"My name's Marcus Okafor," he said. "I fell through a door in the Never-After four days ago. I've been talking to Admiral Landlorn. I think you need to hear what he told me."

Sheila's expression sharpened considerably. "You'd better sit down."

Giles arrived within ten minutes of Sheila's call, and sat across from Marcus with the patient attention he'd learned to bring to anyone who looked like they'd been carrying something too large for one person to hold alone.

"Start from the beginning," he said. "However long it takes."

"I was walking home from the library," Marcus said, still visibly working to make his own memory of it sound like something that had actually happened rather than something he'd dreamed. "There was a — a seam, I guess, in the air, right where the old Hendricks lot used to be before they tore the building down. I didn't even really decide to walk through it. My foot was just already moving before the rest of me caught up." He rubbed his hands together, an old nervous habit apparently still very much intact. "And then I was standing on a ship. An actual ship, wooden, cannons, the whole thing, except the sky over it didn't have a sun in it. Just a kind of gray light that came from everywhere at once."

"The Never-After," Giles said, quiet.

"That's what he called it. Landlorn." Marcus's voice steadied slightly, finding its footing in the telling. "He's — I don't have good words for what he is. Old. Older than old. He didn't threaten me, which I think surprised both of us. Just sat me down and asked me questions for what felt like hours, real careful questions, like he was trying to figure out exactly how I'd gotten there before he decided what to do about it."

"And what did he conclude?"

"That the wall between his place and ours is thinner than it's been in a long time. Thin enough that I fell straight through it by accident, which he said basically never happens anymore, not since some kind of settlement a long time back set the boundary properly." Marcus glanced between Giles and Sheila. "He's not happy about the thinness. Not angry, exactly. More like — worried, the way somebody worries about a fence that used to be sturdy suddenly not holding weight it used to hold. He kept asking me what was different about Sunnydale lately. What's changed. I told him about Halloween, the machine, the vampires, all of it, best I could explain it."

"And his response to that."

"He said, and I think I've got this close to word for word, 'A town that concentrates that much unusual power in one place eventually finds the walls between places grow thin around it, the way water finds the weakest point in a dam.' He offered to keep the channel open. Said he'd rather know what's happening on this side than be surprised by it from his."

Giles rubbed his eyes, the weariness of a man adding one more genuinely enormous variable to a board already carrying more than any single corkboard should reasonably hold. "You're telling me," he said, slowly, "that whatever we're building here is thin enough now to be bleeding through into other planes entirely."

"That's what he told me," Marcus said. "And he wasn't lying. I've gotten pretty good at reading when ancient things are lying to me, the last few days. He wasn't."

"Well," Giles said, finally. "I suppose we'd better find out what's causing it, before Admiral Landlorn's curiosity turns into something less patient."

"He said the door stays open," Marcus said. "Whenever you're ready to use it."

Sheila looked at Giles. Giles looked at the board, mentally, already composing the new entry this would require.

"Thank you, Marcus," Giles said. "This may turn out to be the single most important thing anyone's told us all month."

Marcus, exhausted, relieved, and more informed about the actual shape of reality than any seventeen-year-old had any right to be, allowed himself, for the first time in four days, to simply sit down and let someone else carry the weight of what came next.

Chapter 105: What the Weight Actually Feels Like



"The weight doesn't get lighter. You just get stronger arms." — attributed to Lt. Col. Michael Sheridan, U.S. Army, 82nd Airborne, Ret.

Wednesday, November 5, 1997: 1630 Revello Drive, Sunnydale,
Evening 8:00pm —

Cordelia had been home two days, and she'd spent most of them doing the performance of normalcy that came easily to someone who'd spent a lifetime performing composure in front of an audience that expected it — showering properly for the first time in what felt like a decade, eating real food in careful, deliberate portions because Ramesh had warned her stomach would need reintroducing to actual meals slowly, sitting through the board meeting and contributing sharp, useful opinions about patrol scheduling exactly the way old Cordelia would have.

She had not yet cried. She had not yet talked about the office, the desk, the cold coffee, the sound the room had gone quiet with afterward.

Xander found her on the Summers' back porch a little after eight, sitting alone in the dark with her knees pulled up, and he didn't say anything at first, just sat down beside her, close enough to be present, far enough to not crowd whatever she needed the space for.

"You don't have to talk about it," he said, eventually. "I just didn't want you sitting out here by yourself."

"I keep waiting to feel bad about it," Cordelia said, quiet, staring at the dark yard. "Guilty. Something. Everyone always says the first time costs you something, that you carry it, that it changes you." She looked at her own hands. "I don't feel guilty, Xander. I feel — relieved. Steady. Like I did the math and the math came out clean, and that scares me more than guilt would, because what does it say about me that it came out clean?"

Xander was quiet for a moment, choosing his words with the care he'd learned, over the last week, mattered more than easy reassurance.

"I don't think it says anything bad about you," he said, finally. "I think it says you spent four days deciding exactly what that woman had earned, with a clear head, not a scared one, and you were right. That's not the same as not caring, Cordelia. That's caring enough to actually look at it clearly instead of flinching from the decision." He took her hand. "The guilt you're waiting for — maybe it's not coming because there isn't actually anything there to feel guilty about. Maybe that's allowed to just be true."

Cordelia looked at him for a long moment, something in her chest that had been braced and waiting finally, slowly, permitting itself to unclench.

"It's not actually the killing part that I keep going back to," she said, slower now, working toward something she hadn't quite let herself say plainly yet. "It's the four days before it. The way she talked to me. Never once raised her voice. Never once looked scared of me, even when I gave her every reason to be. She called me 'Subject Chase' like it was my actual name, like the whole rest of me — the part with a name and a father and an actual life — was just paperwork she hadn't bothered filing yet." Her hands tightened, briefly, in her lap. "She kept me without clothes for two days as a control measure. Said it herself, plain as anything, like she was describing a thermostat setting. Not cruelty, exactly — I don't even think she enjoyed it, particularly. That's the part I can't get out of my head. She wasn't cruel because she hated me. She was cruel because I was data, and data doesn't get to keep its dignity if the dignity's inconvenient to the study."

"That's not something you did wrong," Xander said, quiet, fierce underneath the quiet. "That's something she did to you."

"I know that. I actually do know that, logically." Cordelia looked at her hands again. "But logically knowing it and having it stop being the thing my brain replays at three in the morning are two different problems, and I don't currently have a plan for the second one. I keep catching myself checking whether people are looking at me like I'm still a case file. Ramesh, mostly — nothing he's done, just the reflex, the flinch that shows up before the thought does." She shook her head, short, frustrated with herself, though the frustration had something more deliberate underneath it than simple self-pity. "I know exactly why I do that, by the way. I'm not confused about the mechanism. She spent four days training me to expect to be looked at that way, so now some part of me checks for it automatically, the same way you check for a step that isn't actually there anymore after you've tripped on it enough times. I know why the sarcasm got sharper too, back in that room — I watched myself do it in real time, decided being funny and awful was safer than being scared and quiet, because scared and quiet was what she actually wanted from me. I wasn't being brave. I was managing her, the only tool I had left once she'd taken everything else." She looked at Xander directly. "I'm telling you all this so you understand I'm not just having feelings happen to me, Xander. I'm watching myself have them, cataloguing them, same as I catalogue everything else. I just haven't figured out yet whether knowing the mechanism actually helps switch it off, or whether that's a second, separate skill I have to build from scratch." She almost laughed, short and humorless. "I killed her clean, and I stand by it completely. I just didn't expect the actual wound to be the four days before that, instead of the moment itself."

"I keep thinking about the sound of the room afterward," she added, quieter. "How quiet it got. I think that's the part I'm actually going to carry. Not what I did. Just — how quiet everything got right after."

"That makes sense," Xander said, gently. "That's not guilt. That's just what a big moment sounds like, after. The world doesn't make noise about it for you. You have to make your own peace with the silence." He was quiet a moment, then added, careful, "And the other part — the Subject Chase part. I don't think that one gets a clean answer the way the killing did. I think that one's just going to take longer, and I think you get to take exactly as long as it takes, without anybody, including you, deciding there's a deadline on it."

Cordelia leaned into him, finally, the last of the carefully held composure softening into something real.

"I'm glad you're the one who came out here," she said, quiet.

"Always going to be me," Xander said, simple and certain, and they sat together in the dark porch quiet, four days finally, genuinely, starting to become something she could actually put down rather than just carry.

---

Thursday, November 6, 1997; the Chase house, Sunnydale; Afternoon 4:00pm —

Aura had organized it without asking permission, which was, Cordelia reflected, watching her friends arrange themselves across the good living room furniture like they still owned the place, exactly the correct call, because if Aura had asked, Cordelia would have found four perfectly reasonable excuses to say not yet.

"We brought the bad movies," Aura said, dropping a stack of videos on the coffee table with the ceremony of someone delivering medical supplies. "The truly terrible ones. The ones we watch specifically to make fun of, because I've decided that's what this afternoon requires and nobody's allowed to argue with the woman holding the logistics notebook."

"I wasn't going to argue," Cordelia said, and found, to her own mild surprise, that she meant it.

The doorbell went before the movie even started, and Cordelia found, on the porch, a face she hadn't expected but should have — Sergeant Burroughs, CHP, still built like a doorway with a man crammed into it, out of uniform for once, holding a grocery bag with the awkward care of a large man handling something he was worried about crushing.

"Miss Chase." He looked her over, once, thorough, the same assessing once-over he'd have given a suspect or a crime scene, and seemed satisfied with whatever he found. "Heard you were back. Figured I'd see for myself instead of taking some detective's word for it." He held out the bag. "My wife made soup. Told her you probably had plenty of people feeding you already, and she told me to bring it anyway, because apparently that's not how soup works in her family."

"Thank you, Sergeant."

"Burroughs is fine. You're not in my custody anymore, God willing, so we can drop the rank." Something almost like a smile crossed his broad face. "Last time I saw you, you were telling a detective a giant robot from the future tried to kill you, deadpan as you please, like you were reporting a parking violation. I figured, given everything since, you were probably due for somebody showing up who *believed* you this time, instead of writing you up for a psych eval."

"You believed me back then too. Eventually."

"Eventually," Burroughs agreed, gruff. "Took me longer than it should have. I've thought about that some, since." He set the bag down properly in her hands. "Anyway. Wasn't gonna stay for the movie, whatever you kids are watching. Just wanted eyes on you myself. Man likes to finish what he starts, and I started looking out for you and Harris back in that station, whether either of you asked me to or not."

"We noticed," Cordelia said, something moved under the dry delivery. "For what it's worth."

"Good." He nodded once, satisfied, already turning to go. "Eat the soup. Tell that Harris boy I said he's still an idiot for running toward the gunfire instead of away from it, and that I'm still glad he did."

Detective Paul Stein arrived twenty minutes later, off-duty, in the ugly Hawaiian shirt, carrying a pizza he hadn't been asked to bring and clearly had no intention of being talked out of.

"Don't start," he said, before anyone could comment on the shirt, setting the box down and dropping into the armchair like he owned it, because he basically did. "I've been staring at paperwork all week. A man's allowed one crime against fashion on his day off." He looked at Cordelia, and the easy grumbling dropped out of his voice for exactly one sentence. "You eating actual food these days, or still doing the thing where you forget you're allowed to."

"I'm eating, Paul."

"Uh-huh." He didn't push it further than that, because eighteen years of knowing her had taught him precisely how far a direct question got him with a Chase, which was to say, about this far and no further. "Your old man used to say the same thing about you when you were six. 'She's fine, Paul, stop hovering.' Man was wrong about a lot of things at the end there. Wasn't wrong about that." He grabbed a slice before anyone else could. "Play the movie. I want to see the one where the shark's obviously a rubber prop on a fishing line, I've been told that's the whole appeal."

The afternoon unspooled easy after that — bad dialogue mocked in unison, Aura keeping a running tally of plot holes in the same notebook she used for everything else, Paul falling asleep in the armchair for twenty minutes and denying it with the flat conviction of a cop who'd lie to your face about smaller things too if the situation called for it — and Cordelia, watching her actual life reassemble itself around her in real time, felt something she hadn't let herself feel yet since Halloween: the ordinary relief of being surrounded by people who'd known her before any of this, and would, apparently, keep right on knowing her after.

---



Chapter 106: Existing Without a Directive, Day One



"Freedom isn't knowing what to do with yourself. It's being allowed to not know yet, without that being a failure." — attributed to the TX, private record

Wednesday, November 5, 1997: Sunnydale High School Library,
Sunnydale, Afternoon 3:15pm —

The TX had spent the two days since its conversation with Giles attempting, with methodical and increasingly self-aware effort, to determine what "simply existing" was actually supposed to look like, and had arrived, by this Wednesday afternoon, at a small, specific discovery that it found disproportionately significant.

It had been reading.

Not tactical manuals, not threat assessments — an actual novel, one of the paperbacks students left behind in the library's overflow shelf, something with a soft cover and a plot about ordinary people navigating ordinary disappointments, and it had found, to its own carefully logged surprise, that the rhythm of following a story that contained no operational relevance whatsoever produced a state in its processing that it did not yet have adequate vocabulary for, except that the state felt, distinctly, pleasant.

"You're reading," Giles said, from the threshold, six feet four inches away, the boundary neither of them had ever formally negotiated but both still respected.

"I am," the TX confirmed. "I am uncertain whether this constitutes an appropriate use of the time previously allocated to threshold surveillance."

"It constitutes precisely the kind of thing I was hoping you'd discover," Giles said, and there was real warmth in it. "What are you reading?"

The TX held up the cover. "A novel concerning a woman rebuilding her relationship with her estranged sister after their mother's death. It contains no tactical content whatsoever."

"And you're enjoying it?"

The TX considered this question with the thoroughness it applied to everything, running the query against its own internal state with unusual care. "I believe I am," it said, finally. "The protagonist's decision-making process, while emotionally rather than tactically motivated, follows a logic I find myself curious to continue observing."

Giles smiled, the quiet, moved smile of a man watching something impossible become, slowly and carefully, something more hopeful.

"Harmony would never have finished that book," he said, gently. "She'd have found it dreadfully boring."

"I am aware," the TX said. "That is, I believe, precisely why I am finding it interesting. It is mine. Not Harmony's borrowed architecture. Simply — mine."

Giles was quiet for a long moment, and then nodded, once, and left the threshold without another word, because some moments, he'd learned, over seventy-two nights of careful patience, didn't need further comment to matter.

---



Chapter 107: Practicing Being Two People With One Set of Feet



"Partnership works best when both people know exactly what they bring, and neither one apologizes for it."
— Murphy's Laws of Combat

Thursday, November 6, 1997: Sunnydale Cemetery, Restfield,
Sunnydale, Night 11:00pm —

Faith's first patrol with Buffy went better than either of them had privately braced for, in the way that two people who'd spent weeks bracing for friction discover, sometimes, that the friction simply doesn't show up when they finally stop expecting it.

"You always talk this much on patrol?" Faith said, staking a fledgling with the economical efficiency of someone who'd done this a thousand times alone and was still adjusting to doing it with company. "'Cause fair warning, B, I'm used to quiet. Might take me a minute to adjust to Sunnydale's chattiest Slayer."

"Only when the company's good," Buffy said, matching her pace easily. "You're better than I expected, by the way. Not that I expected you to be bad. I just — I've never patrolled with another Slayer before. I didn't know what the rhythm would feel like."

"Better than expected. I'll take it." Faith flashed something quick and sharp, more performance than actual smugness, the specific brightness she reached for whenever a conversation got close enough to something real to need covering. "What can I say, I'm a natural. Some people gotta work at this. I just show up and look good doing it."

Buffy let that sit a beat, unbothered, apparently already fluent in exactly what that particular brand of deflection meant. "Uh huh," she said, easy, not pushing.

They walked in comfortable silence for a stretch, the cemetery quiet around them, and Faith felt the bravado run out of road the way it always eventually did when nobody actually took the bait — found herself, unprompted, filling the quiet with something she hadn't planned on saying at all, the joke nowhere left to hide behind.

"Diana used to patrol with me sometimes," she said, quieter now, all the performance gone out of it. "Not fighting — she was too old for the front line stuff. But she'd walk the perimeter, keep watch, talk me through the read on a situation before I went in swinging." A pause. "I miss that. The talking-through part. I didn't realize how much I missed it till just now."

Buffy didn't say anything immediately, just let the admission sit there, respected, the way Faith had learned, over the last few days, this whole household seemed to instinctively know how to do.

"I can do the talking-through part," Buffy said, finally, quiet. "If you want. Doesn't have to replace what you had with her. Just — doesn't have to be nothing, either."

Faith looked at her for a long moment, something loosening in her chest that had been tight since Diana Dormer's funeral, six weeks and a lifetime ago.

"Yeah," she said. "I'd like that." Then, because the quiet had gone on a beat too long for comfort, the armor came back up, easy and automatic: "Don't go getting used to the sincere version though. She's rationed. I only bust her out on special occasions."

"Duly noted," Buffy said, and didn't call her on it, which Faith appreciated more than she was anywhere close to ready to say out loud.

They finished the patrol together, two Slayers walking the same rhythm for the first time either of them had ever gotten to experience, and Faith thought, distantly, that maybe alone had never actually been a policy worth keeping quite so tightly.

Chapter 108: A Whole Life, Properly Started



"A life, properly started, rarely looks the way anyone expected it to." — attributed to Rona Williams

Friday, November 7, 1997: Sunnydale High School, Computer Science Room,
Sunnydale, Afternoon 4:00pm —

Jenny Calendar had set up a proper training space in the computer room after hours — not glamorous, just a clear table and a stack of the older books Giles had loaned her, carefully bookmarked to the sections most relevant to a witch who'd inherited a lifetime of memory and none of the actual practiced control that lifetime should have come with.

"We start with grounding," Jenny said. "Not spells. Not power. Just — knowing where your own feet are, energetically, before you try to move anything at all."

Willow sat across from her, notebook open, the focused attention she brought to every subject she'd ever cared about. "The memory I have makes grounding feel like it should be automatic. Like I should already know how."

"You know the theory," Jenny said. "You don't have the muscle memory, because the muscle memory belongs to a life you didn't actually live in this body. That gap is normal, Willow. It's not a failure. It's just the actual distance between inherited knowledge and practiced skill."

Willow considered this, and something in her shoulders, tight since the whole strange business had started, eased slightly.

"So I'm not behind," she said. "I'm just starting."

"You're just starting," Jenny confirmed. "Properly, this time. With someone watching your form instead of you just borrowing someone else's."

They worked for an hour, simple exercises, Willow's hands steadying with each attempt, the satisfaction of visible progress accumulating in small, genuine increments rather than the overwhelming completeness of borrowed memory.

"Same time next week?" Jenny said, as they packed up.

"Yeah," Willow said, and there was real hope in it now, steady and her own. "I think I'd like that a lot."

---



Chapter 109: The Board Takes On a New Kind of Homework



"Homework is just practice for the parts of the future that haven't happened to you yet." — attributed to Dawn Summers, private notebook

Wednesday, November 12, 1997: 1630 Revello Drive, Sunnydale,
Evening 7:30pm —

The board had a new section by Wednesday evening, added in Dawn's careful handwriting beneath a header that simply read THE THIN PLACES, and the Monday meeting had, for the first time since its founding, an item on the agenda that nobody at the table had a ready answer for.

"So to be clear," Xander said, looking at the notes Marcus had provided, "something in this town is thin enough now that an ancient pirate king noticed from an different plane of existence."

"That is, regrettably, an accurate summary," Giles said.

"Great." Xander rubbed his face. "Add it to the pile."

"The pile appears to be permanently expanding," Seven observed, from beside Warren, with the flat accuracy that made the room laugh despite the genuine weight of the topic.

"We need actual data," Michaela said, taking charge of the practical shape of the problem, the way she always did. "Not secondhand pirate testimony, however credible. Giles — is there a way to measure this directly? Some kind of ritual diagnostic?"

"There may be," Giles said, slowly. "It would require careful preparation. And likely Willow's assistance, given her — unique current qualifications."

Willow looked up from her notebook, something steady and newly confident in her posture that hadn't been there two weeks ago. "I can help with that. I've actually been working on grounding exercises with Miss Calendar. I think I'm finally getting somewhere real with it."

"Then that's next week's project," Michaela said, marking it on the board herself, beneath Dawn's careful header. "Giles and Willow, work out what a diagnostic actually requires. Everyone else — keep doing what you're doing. This town doesn't stop needing patrols just because we've got a new mystery to chase."

The meeting rolled on, the board slowly filling with another week's worth of items, and Marcus Okafor, sitting quietly near the back, allowed himself, for the first time since falling through a door in the Never-After, to feel like he'd actually delivered his warning to people equipped to do something useful with it.

Chapter 110: What's Actually Doing the Wearing



"A borrowed skill only ever really belongs to you once you've used it for a reason of your own choosing." — attributed to Jonathan Levinson

Monday, November 17, 1997: Sunnydale High School Library,
Sunnydale, Evening 7:00pm —

Giles had the library table covered in a week's worth of accumulated research — old texts on planar boundaries, three different translations of a Sumerian fragment concerning "the places between," and a hand-drawn map of the school grounds with the thin patch Willow had located marked in careful red ink, directly beneath the library's east reading room.

"It's not natural erosion," Willow said, running her grounding exercise one more time, eyes closed, attention extended carefully toward the thin patch the way Jenny had taught her. "The Hellmouth's presence wears at boundaries the way a river wears a bank — slow, constant, predictable. This isn't that. This is thinner in a specific direction, like something's been actively pulling at it from one side rather than just sitting near it."

"Pulling from which side," Giles asked.

Willow considered this carefully, extending her attention further, the careful stretch Jenny had drilled into her — reach, don't grasp; observe, don't touch.

"The Never-After side," she said, finally, opening her eyes. "It's not the Hellmouth wearing outward. It's something on Landlorn's side of the boundary, pulling inward. Slow. Patient. Like whatever it is has been working at this specific spot for a long time, and it's just now gotten thin enough that things can occasionally slip through by accident, the way Marcus did."

Giles absorbed this, writing it down carefully. "Deliberate, then. Not incidental."

"I think so," Willow said. "I don't have enough sensitivity yet to identify what's doing the pulling. That's — that's actually kind of frightening, if I'm honest. Something's been quietly working at this specific point in the world for what feels like years, and nobody noticed until an ancient pirate king mentioned it to a kid who fell through by accident."

Giles was quiet for a long moment, the weight of a new, unclear threat settling over the research table.

"We'll need Landlorn's own perspective on this," he said, finally. "He's had centuries to observe his side of the boundary. If anyone knows what's doing the pulling, it's more likely to be him than us."

"Marcus said the door stays open," Willow said. "On his end. Whenever we're ready to use it."

Giles looked at the red-inked map, at the thin patch slowly, patiently being worn away by something with more patience than either side of this conversation had ever needed before this month.

"Then I suppose," he said, "we'd better find out what 'ready' actually requires, before whatever's doing this finishes the job."

Chapter 111: Old Debts, Properly Called In



"Old debts, honestly settled, are worth more than new ones never incurred." — attributed to Blahd (Count Savros Hieronymus Blahdivastri)

Wednesday, November 19, 1997: Willy's Place, Sunnydale,
Night 10:00pm —

Angel had not set foot in Willy's Place in over a year, and the bartender's face did the complicated thing demon bartenders' faces did when a customer with an inconvenient reputation and a soul walked back through the door after a long absence — recalculating risk, recalculating debt, recalculating exactly how much history needed managing before the conversation could actually start.

"Angelus," Willy said, careful.

"Just Angel these days," Angel said, settling onto a stool with the unhurried patience of someone who'd spent two hundred years learning exactly how much silence a room like this required before business could begin. "I need information, Willy. Old information. About something pulling at the boundary between here and the Never-After, underneath the high school."

Willy's expression did something wary. "That's not the kind of thing I keep on tap, if you follow."

"I know you keep more than the tap suggests," Angel said, mild. "You've been keeping Sunnydale's actual secrets for longer than most of your customers have been alive. I'm not here to threaten you into talking. I'm here because you owe me from '82, and I'm calling it in properly, the way debts are supposed to work."

Willy considered this for a long moment, weighing the danger of information against the danger of an old debt gone unpaid to someone who'd never once, in two hundred years, forgotten a ledger entry.

"There's old talk," Willy said, finally, low. "Real old. About something on the Never-After side that's been quietly working the Sunnydale boundary since before this town had a proper name. Not a demon. Not exactly. More like — an intention. Patient. The kind of thing that doesn't hurry because it's already got centuries invested and doesn't mind spending a few more."

"Does it have a name?"

"Not one anybody says out loud in here," Willy said. "But there's talk it's been waiting for something specific. Something about the Hellmouth's own architecture weakening at just the right point. Talk is, once it gets through clean, it's not looking to conquer anything. It's looking to collect."

"Collect what."

Willy looked at him for a long moment, old fear moving behind his eyes.

"Debts," he said, finally. "Old ones. The kind this whole town's been quietly accumulating on the Hellmouth's ledger for over a century, and never once thought to check whether somebody was actually keeping score."

Angel sat with that for a long moment, the weight of two hundred years of demon-world rumor finally converging into something that mattered more than gossip.

"Thank you, Willy," he said, and meant it. "Debt's cleared. From '82."

"Do me a favor," Willy said, as Angel stood to leave. "Whatever that thing actually is — settle it before it settles Sunnydale's account itself. I like my customers alive. Bad for business otherwise."

Angel left without another word, already composing, in his head, exactly how he was going to explain this piece of two-hundred-year-old bar gossip to a room that was, he suspected, about to need more research before Monday's meeting.

Chapter 112: The Story Runs



"The right story, told carefully, protects more people than silence ever does."
— Murphy's Laws of Combat

Sunday, November 23, 1997: The Sunnydale Motor Inn, Room 14,
Sunnydale, Morning 10:00am —

Perry White set the phone down, finally, after a forty-minute call with Michaela Reeves that had covered exactly which details could run and which needed careful omission, and looked at Kolchak with the tired satisfaction of a reporter who'd just navigated the hardest negotiation of an already hard career.

"She's cleared most of it," Perry said. "Names changed where kids are involved. No specifics on the federal program — that stays buried, and honestly, I agree it should. But the town. The strangeness. The community pulling together through something genuinely extraordinary — that runs, mostly as we wrote it."

"Greer's not going to love the softened version," Kolchak said.

"Greer can run the version we give him, or he can find someone else willing to hand him kids' names on a silver platter," Perry said, and there was real steel in it. "I've been doing this twenty-two years, Carl. I know which stories I want attached to my name when I look back on this career, and this is one of them, done right."

Kolchak looked at the finished draft, six careful installments, the frozen gorilla footage reduced to a single respectful still image rather than the sensational centerpiece Greer had originally wanted.

"For what it's worth," he said, "I think this might be the best work either of us has done in years."

"For what it's worth," Perry said, "I think you're right." She picked up the phone again, dialing Greer's number with the weary determination of a woman about to fight one more battle for a story worth fighting for. "Let's go tell him how it's actually going to run."

Chapter 113: A Season of Practicing Existing



"Existing, on purpose, is a harder discipline than surviving by accident." — attributed to the TX, private record

Friday, November 21, 1997: Sunnydale High School Library,
Sunnydale, Afternoon 4:00pm —

The TX had finished the novel about the estranged sisters two days ago, and had moved on, with the methodical curiosity it brought to everything now, to a battered paperback about a family running a bookshop in a small English village, and found, three chapters in, that it had developed something it was fairly confident qualified as a preference.

"You've got a stack building," Giles observed, from the threshold, nodding at the small pile of finished paperbacks accumulating beside the reading table.

"I find I have opinions about which ones I prefer," the TX said. "The village bookshop novel is considerably better constructed than the one concerning a detective in Manchester. I am uncertain whether 'better constructed' is my own aesthetic judgment or simply an assessment of narrative efficiency, but I find myself curious to continue reading regardless of which it is."

"That's rather the definition of a preference, I'd say," Giles said, gently amused.

"Then I have one," the TX said, and there was something in the flat statement that carried, unmistakably now, quiet satisfaction. "I did not have preferences three weeks ago. I had directives and threat assessments. This is — different."

Giles was quiet for a moment, watching something remarkable continue to unfold in front of him, one small preference at a time.

"Would you like more books?" he said. "I could bring some from home. My own collection runs rather heavily toward the esoteric, but there's a fair bit of ordinary fiction mixed in as well."

"I would like that," the TX said, simply. "Thank you, Mr. Giles."

"You're welcome," Giles said, and left it there, because some progress, he'd learned over three weeks of careful patience, needed nothing more than simply being witnessed to matter.

---



Chapter 114: The Nightclub Needs a Name



"A name is the first promise a thing makes about what it intends to become." — attributed to Veronique de Alençon

Saturday, November 22, 1997: Crawford Street Manor, Sunnydale,
Evening 7:00pm —

"We cannot open in two weeks without a name," Veronique said, standing in the finished ballroom, now furnished, lit, close to ready, and looking at Blahd with the mild exasperation of a woman who had solved harder problems than this one and found herself, somehow, stuck on this detail.

"I've suggested six names," Blahd said. "You've rejected six names."

"Because they were all terrible," Veronique said. "'The Ancient Hour' sounds like a retirement community. 'Nine Centuries' sounds like a wine label. I refuse to open a good establishment under a bad name."

"Then you suggest one."

Veronique considered the ballroom — the restored ceiling, the new bar, the quality of a space finally becoming exactly what it was always meant to be, four hundred years from now believed authentic by people who'd never know the truth of how recently it had actually been rebuilt.

"The Threshold," she said, finally, testing the shape of it out loud.

Blahd considered this, looking at the room, at the liminal quality of a place built by old things learning to exist in a new century.

"The Threshold," he repeated, slowly. "I find I like it more than 'Nine Centuries.'"

"Everyone would like anything more than 'Nine Centuries,'" Veronique said, dry, and something in her voice held genuine warmth. "The Threshold. Between the old world and this one. Between what we were and whatever we're choosing to become instead." She looked at him. "It suits us."

"It suits us," Blahd agreed, and for the first time in nine hundred years, felt something that might, on a less careful face, have been called simple, uncomplicated happiness.

﻿

Chapter 115: Knocking on a Door That Isn't There Yet



"Some doors you knock on for years before you understand what you were actually asking to be let into." — attributed to Cassidy, a later record

Monday, November 24, 1997: Sunnydale High School Library,
Sunnydale, Evening 6:00pm —

The board's newest entry, in Dawn's careful hand, read simply: *Contact Landlorn — who goes, what we ask, what we don't.* Underneath it, in Michaela's sharper block capitals, someone had added: *Not everyone. Small team only.*

"Marcus says the door opens on his end whenever we're ready," Giles said, addressing the smaller group gathered around the library table — himself, Michaela, Willow, and, at Angel's quiet insistence, Angel himself, whose two hundred years of demon-world context had already proven more useful in three weeks than anyone had initially credited. "The question is what 'ready' actually means, and I confess I don't want to walk through a door to an ancient pirate king's ship without a clearer sense of what we're asking for."

"We're asking what's pulling at the boundary," Willow said. "And whether it can be stopped, slowed, or has to just be met head-on eventually."

"And we're asking it of something that already told Marcus it wants an open channel," Michaela said. "Which means walking in there isn't neutral. It's the first move in an actual relationship, whatever that turns out to mean long-term."

Angel was quiet for a moment, weighing two hundred years of instinct against three weeks of believing he had something to contribute. "I'd suggest sending someone who already understands old things and old bargains," he said. "Not because I'm eager to go. Because I've spent two centuries learning exactly how these conversations go wrong, and I'd rather not learn that lesson secondhand from whoever we send instead."

Giles looked at him for a long moment, and something in his expression settled into simple, genuine trust.

"Then it's you, myself, and Willow," Giles said. "Michaela coordinates from this side. We go carefully, we ask exactly what we need to know, and we come back the moment we have it."

"When," Michaela said.

"Wednesday," Giles said. "That gives us two days to prepare properly, and I'd rather walk in prepared than rushed."

The board's entry got one more line added beneath it, in Giles's own careful hand: *Team: Giles, Angel, Willow. Wednesday. Michaela holds the line here.*

Chapter 116: The Conversation That Couldn't Wait Anymore



"Some conversations arrive on their own schedule, and the only choice left is whether to have them well." — attributed to Kendra Young

Tuesday, November 25, 1997: 1630 Revello Drive, Sunnydale,
Evening 7:00pm —

"I want to go to Nevada," Cordelia said, at the board meeting, plainly, no preamble, because three weeks of careful healing had taught her that the things worth saying were rarely improved by softening. "Rona Williams deserves to hear the truth from the person whose patterns Walsh actually used, not from a file, not from a stranger. That's me. I owe her that conversation."

The room went quiet for a moment, the quiet of people recognizing a decision that had already been made rather than one still being debated.

"I'll go with you," Xander said, immediate, certain.

"I know," Cordelia said, and something in her voice, warm underneath the steadiness, acknowledged exactly how little that had needed saying out loud. "I want Michaela's blessing on the trip, logistically. And I want to do this soon. I've been sitting on this for three weeks, and every week I wait is another week she's living with Walsh's lie about what actually happened to her."

Michaela considered this, weighing operational concerns against the plain moral weight of the request. "I can have transport and cover arranged by Friday," she said. "You'll go as yourselves — no aliases, no games. If this is about giving her the truth, it starts with an honest introduction."

"Agreed," Cordelia said.

Giles looked at her with quiet, genuine respect. "That's going to be a difficult conversation, Cordelia. Are you prepared for what she might feel toward you, once she understands the connection?"

Cordelia was quiet for a moment, and Xander's hand found hers under the table, steady.

"I'm prepared for her to be angry," Cordelia said, finally. "I'm prepared for it to be complicated. I'm not prepared to let her keep living with a lie just because the truth might be hard for both of us to sit through." She looked around the table. "Friday, then. Xander and I go to Nevada."

The board's newest entry, added carefully beneath the Landlorn line, simply read: *Rona Williams — Cordelia & Xander, Friday.*

Chapter 117: The Threshold Opens



"A threshold is just a door that decided to stay open on purpose." — attributed to the Threshold's opening-night program

Saturday, December 6, 1997: Crawford Street Manor, Sunnydale,
Night 9:00pm —

The Threshold's opening night filled the restored ballroom with a crowd unlike anything Sunnydale's ordinary nightlife had ever produced — old blood and young, the town's quiet supernatural economy finally given a room worth gathering in, and the Board's own contingent arriving in a loose, comfortable cluster that made Veronique, watching from the bar, feel something she hadn't expected to feel this strongly: genuine pride.

"You came," she said, greeting Buffy at the door personally, a small honor she extended to almost no one.

"Wouldn't have missed it," Buffy said, looking around at the transformed ballroom, the new ceiling, the warm gold lighting that made even Sunnydale's older, stranger residents look, for one night, simply like people enjoying themselves. "It's beautiful, Veronique."

"Six weeks of work," Veronique said. "And eight hundred years of knowing exactly what a room like this is supposed to feel like." She looked out at the crowd — Blahd holding court near the bar with the easy authority of someone who'd finally found the right century to be himself in, Sheila and Giles sharing a quiet drink at a corner table, Angel and Buffy's own careful, still-new comfort with each other visible even across the room.

"Nine hundred years, and this is what finally feels like home," Blahd said, joining them, something warm and unguarded in his ancient face. "I did not expect that, Veronique. I confess I did not expect any of this."

"Neither did I," Veronique said, and meant it completely.

The Threshold's first night ran until nearly dawn, old things and new things sharing the same warm light, and Sunnydale, for one evening, felt less like a town perpetually bracing for the next disaster and more like a place worth actually living in.

Chapter 118: A Watcher Recalibrates His Own Doctrine



"Doctrine that cannot survive contact with better evidence was never doctrine. It was habit wearing doctrine's coat." — attributed to Quentin Travers, later correspondence

Sunday, November 30, 1997: Zabuto Residence, Sunnydale,
Afternoon 3:00pm —

Zabuto had been drafting a letter to the Watcher's Council for the better part of an hour, and Kendra, setting tea down beside him, noted the quality of his frustration — not anger, just the careful friction of a man trying to phrase something the institution he'd served for decades was unlikely to want to hear.

"You are recommending changes," she said. Not a question.

"I am attempting to," Zabuto said. "I find myself in the unusual position of having watched three Slayers operate with genuine support systems for a month now, and having observed considerably better outcomes than the Council's traditional isolation doctrine has ever produced." He set the pen down. "I do not know if they will listen."

"Will you send it regardless?"

"Yes," Zabuto said, simply. "Because the alternative is watching you thrive here, in defiance of everything I was trained to believe was necessary for a Slayer's survival, and saying nothing to the institution that trained me, purely to avoid an uncomfortable conversation." He looked at her. "You have changed my understanding of my own doctrine, Kendra. I find I owe the Council the honesty of saying so plainly, whether or not they choose to hear it."

Kendra considered this, something warm moving in her chest at the weight of what he'd just admitted.

"Thank you, Mr. Zabuto," she said. "For being willing to be wrong, and to say so."

"It is, I am discovering," Zabuto said, dry, "easier than I expected, once you actually decide to do it." He picked the pen back up. "Now. Let me finish this letter, so the Council has at least one voice arguing they've had the entire framework backwards for longer than anyone's been willing to say."

Chapter 119: The First Real Vulnerable Thing



"The first honest thing is always the hardest one to say out loud." — attributed to Warren Mears

Wednesday, November 26, 1997: 1630 Revello Drive, Sunnydale,
Night 10:00pm —

Faith and Buffy sat on the porch after patrol, the comfortable quiet of two people who'd stopped needing to fill every silence with conversation, and Faith broke it, eventually, with something that started out sounding like a joke.

"So I've been doing this thing," she said, dry, poking at the porch board with the toe of her boot, "where I catalog exactly how long I can go without needing anybody for anything, like it's a personal record I'm trying to beat. Real impressive skill set. Very marketable." The joke ran thin almost as soon as she'd said it, and something more honest slid in underneath before she could stop it. "I used to think needing people was a weakness," she said, quiet now, looking out at the dark yard instead of at Buffy. "Diana tried to tell me otherwise. I didn't really hear it till she was gone, and by then it felt too late to actually learn the lesson properly."

"It's not too late," Buffy said, gentle.

"I know that now," Faith said. "Took me six weeks alone and about a month of you people being annoyingly patient with me to actually believe it." She looked at Buffy, and the deflection tried once more to surface — *forget I said any of that* sitting right there, ready to be deployed — and she made herself not reach for it. "I wanted to say thank you. Properly. Not the deflecting kind of thank you. The real kind."

Buffy was quiet for a moment, letting the weight of that land without rushing past it.

"You're welcome," she said, simply. "For what it's worth — I think you'd have gotten here eventually, with or without us. You just would've had to walk it alone a lot longer than you needed to."

"Maybe," Faith said. "I'm just glad I didn't have to find out." She leaned back against the porch rail, something settled and steady in her posture that hadn't been there a month ago, and then, because the sincerity had gone on exactly as long as she could hold it without her hands starting to itch for something to deflect with, added: "Anyway. Enough of the Very Special Episode stuff. Same time tomorrow? Patrol?"

"Same time tomorrow," Buffy confirmed, warm, entirely unbothered by the abrupt pivot, and they sat together in the quiet a while longer, two Slayers who'd finally, genuinely, stopped carrying the weight alone.

Chapter 120: Aboard the Retribution, By Invitation This Time



"An invitation, once earned, is worth more than one merely extended." — attributed to Admiral Landlorn

Wednesday, November 26, 1997: Somewhere in the Never-After, aboard the Retribution,
Same non-time —

Marcus had walked them through the door himself, insisting on it despite Giles's protests that he'd already done more than enough, and the four of them — Giles, Angel, Willow, and Marcus — stood now on the Retribution's deck while Admiral Landlorn descended from the quarterdeck with the unhurried ceremony of something that had genuinely been anticipating this visit.

"The Watcher," Landlorn said, studying Giles with open curiosity. "And a vampire with a soul. And a young witch carrying knowledge of the Ghost Roads that I have not encountered in considerable time." He looked at Willow with real, ancient interest. "You've been reaching toward the boundary. I felt it, three days past."

"You felt my diagnostic," Willow said, startled.

"I feel most things that reach toward this boundary from your side," Landlorn said. "It is, after all, my domain being worn thin." He gestured for them to follow him toward his cabin. "Come. Let us discuss what's actually doing the wearing, before it finishes the job neither of us wants finished."

Giles fell into step beside him, the careful attention of a scholar finally getting access to information no text in his considerable library had ever contained.

"You know what it is," Giles said.

"I know what it calls itself," Landlorn said. "Whether that name means anything useful to you remains to be seen. But yes, Watcher. I know precisely what's been patiently working at your town's boundary for the better part of a century, and I confess, I am relieved someone has finally arrived ready to actually discuss it."

They disappeared below decks together, four representatives of a town's fragile, hard-won peace walking deliberately into the oldest kind of danger, carrying nothing but careful preparation and the genuine hope that knowing the shape of a threat was always better than facing it blind.

Chapter 121: The Name Landlorn Gave Them



"A name given freely is a different kind of gift than a name assigned." — attributed to Cassidy, a later record

Wednesday, November 26, 1997: Somewhere in the Never-After, aboard the Retribution,
Landlorn's Cabin, Same non-time —

Landlorn's cabin held more books than Giles had expected from a pirate king, shelves of accumulated centuries lining every wall, and he gestured for them to sit before settling into his own chair with the unhurried gravity of something about to say a true thing it had been carrying a long time.

"It calls itself the Reckoner," Landlorn said. "It is not a demon, precisely. It is closer to what your world might call a debt made manifest — an intention that accumulates weight the way rust accumulates on iron, slow, patient, entirely without hurry, because it has never once needed to hurry."

"Debt for what," Willow asked.

"For every soul the Hellmouth has claimed and never properly accounted for," Landlorn said. "Every death Sunnydale's residents have collectively agreed not to notice, for over a century. Every disappearance filed as a transfer, a runaway, a family relocating suddenly in the night. The town has been quietly, unknowingly building a ledger for a hundred years, and the Reckoner has been patiently waiting for the ledger to come due."

Angel was quiet for a long moment, two centuries of demon-world context settling into place around this new, darker shape. "Willy mentioned something about collecting," he said. "Not conquering. Collecting."

"That is precisely its nature," Landlorn confirmed. "It does not wish to rule your town, Watcher. It wishes to be paid. And when a debt of that particular size and age finally comes due, the payment it seeks is rarely something any of you would willingly offer."

Giles absorbed this with the careful weight of a man calculating exactly how much worse this made an already complicated month. "Can it be stopped? Reasoned with?"

"It can be paid," Landlorn said, simply. "Or it can be refused, and refusal, historically, has not gone well for the towns that attempted it." He looked at them steadily. "You have time. Weeks, perhaps a season, before the boundary thins enough for it to fully manifest. I would not waste that time, were I in your position."

Willow was already thinking ahead, the focused calculation Giles had watched her develop over weeks of careful training. "If it's debt made manifest — maybe there's a way to actually settle the ledger. Properly. Acknowledge everyone the Hellmouth's claimed, formally, instead of just letting the town keep pretending it didn't happen."

Giles looked at her with something like genuine hope breaking through the weight of the revelation. "That," he said, slowly, "may actually be worth exploring."

Landlorn watched the four of them with the patient, ancient interest of something that had clearly been hoping, all along, that Sunnydale's newest formation might prove equal to the challenge his own domain had been quietly warning them about.

"Go home," he said, finally. "Discuss this with your Board. And when you're ready to discuss the actual mechanics of a debt this size — you know where the door is."

Chapter 122: Sedona



"The truth costs something to deliver. It costs more to withhold."
— Murphy's Laws of Combat

Friday, November 28, 1997: Outside Sedona, Arizona,
Afternoon 1:00pm —

Rona Williams was sitting on her porch by the time Cordelia and Xander's car pulled up the long dirt drive, and she did not stand, watching them cross the dry ground with the quality of someone assessing exactly what they were and deciding what to do about it — an assessment Cordelia recognized immediately, uncomfortably, as a version of something she herself did constantly, accurate rather than imitative.

Something tightened in Cordelia's chest. She breathed through it, converted it to information, and kept walking.

"Rona Williams," she said, once they reached the porch.

"Yeah," Williams said, voice flat and even, a Marine's learned flatness that hadn't fully worn off despite whatever circumstances had brought her here. "You're not Walsh's people."

"No," Cordelia said. "We're not."

"Then who are you."

"My name is Cordelia Chase," Cordelia said. "This is Xander Harris. We're from Sunnydale."

Williams looked at them for a long moment, the same careful assessment continuing.

"You'd better sit down," she said, finally. "Both of you. Because I think this is going to be one of those conversations."

"It is," Cordelia said, and they sat.

Cordelia told her everything, in the order it needed telling — Walsh first, the DRI program, the theory about Halloween and whether its effects were reproducible, and then, carefully, the Nevada facility, the consent forms, what they'd actually said versus what they'd actually done.

"Neural plasticity study with a behavioral outcome component," Williams said, when Cordelia reached that part.

"Yes," Cordelia said.

"That's what I signed."

"Yes."

Williams looked out at the red rock formations, the high desert, the clear stillness of a Sedona afternoon.

"Start at the beginning," she said. "All of it. I've had four days locked in a room with nothing but my own head for company, and I've decided I'd rather have the whole truth, slowly, than a summary I have to keep asking follow-up questions about."

So Cordelia did.

She started with Walsh's actual title — Professor of Behavioral Neuroscience, UC Sunnydale, consulting psychiatrist for the county — and the arrogance of a woman who'd believed, at the outset, that she was doing careful, defensible science. She walked through the DRI program's founding, the first eight failed attempts to reproduce whatever Halloween had done to the TX, the growing frustration as machine-replication kept failing and Walsh's ambitions simply pivoted, without apparent hesitation, toward a human subject instead.

"She needed a template," Cordelia said. "Somebody's actual neural patterns, mapped in enough detail that she could try building the compliance architecture directly off a real person's cognition instead of guessing at machine logic that kept failing her. She needed somebody who'd already been through something extreme. Somebody whose mind had already been forced to adapt to captivity, so the mapping would actually mean something."

"And she found you."

"She found me first," Cordelia said, steady, though something in her jaw had gone tight. "I was her template, Rona. Four days in a cell, no clothes, camera on a fourteen-second cycle, and she mapped every single thing my mind did to survive it — every adaptation, every coping pattern, every way I found to stay functional under exactly the kind of pressure she needed data on. I got out before she finished building whatever she actually intended to build with it. She kept the data anyway."

Williams went very still, the stillness of someone doing arithmetic she badly didn't want to finish.

"That's what's in me," she said, slow, working it out loud. "Not just training. Your actual patterns. Whatever your brain did to survive that room, she built the scaffolding of my compliance directly out of it."

"Yes," Cordelia said, quiet. "I don't know all of what that means for you, day to day. I don't know if it means you flinch at things I flinch at, or find calm in the same specific places I find it, or whether it's buried too deep under everything else she added to ever surface cleanly. But it's true, and you deserved to hear it plainly rather than find it buried in a file someday and wonder why nobody told you."

Williams was quiet for a long time, the weight of it settling slowly, the silence of someone recalibrating an entire understanding of her own mind, one piece at a time, out loud, because that was apparently the only way she was going to be able to actually hold it.

"So some part of what's keeping me functional right now," she said, finally, "is a rich kid from Sunnydale who out-survived a psychopath in a locked room."

"Yeah," Cordelia said, simple, no flinching from the description. "That's about the size of it."

"That's a hell of a thing to drive four hours to tell someone," Williams said, low.

"You deserved to hear it from an actual person," Cordelia said. "Not a file. Not a stranger reading you a report. Me, in person, telling you the truth even though it's not an easy truth to sit with, for either of us."

Williams looked at her for a long moment, something shifting behind her carefully controlled expression.

"Thank you," she said, quiet, genuine. "For coming yourself. That matters more than you probably know."

"There's a place," Xander said, gently, "back in Sunnydale. People who'd understand exactly what you're carrying, better than almost anyone else could. No pressure. Just — the door's open, whenever you're ready."

Williams looked at the two of them, at the honesty they'd driven four hours to deliver in person, and felt, for the first time since Walsh's program had rearranged something fundamental about her own mind, like maybe she wasn't as alone in this as she'd assumed.

"Yeah," she said, slowly. "Maybe I'll take you up on that."

---



Chapter 123: The Threshold's First Real Test



"The first real test of anything new is simply whether it holds under an ordinary Tuesday." — attributed to Veronique de Alençon

Tuesday, December 9, 1997: The Threshold, Crawford Street Manor,
Sunnydale, Night 11:00pm —

Three nights into the Threshold's operation, Veronique found herself dealing with the first genuine complication of running a legitimate establishment in a town this strange — a minor demon lord from the town's older power structure, unhappy about the sudden competition for Sunnydale's supernatural social scene, standing in her doorway with the blustering confidence of something used to intimidating newer arrivals.

"You've moved into territory that isn't yours," the demon said, low, threatening in the way lesser things postured when they hadn't yet learned who they were actually addressing.

Veronique looked at him for a long moment, eight hundred years of patience wearing thin faster than usual.

"I have built six residences across four centuries," she said, mild, dangerous. "I have negotiated with things older and more frightening than you across most of recorded history. You are welcome to leave peacefully, or you are welcome to discover, firsthand, precisely why 'territory' is a concept old blood generally finds beneath serious discussion."

The demon hesitated, some old instinct recognizing, belatedly, exactly what he'd walked in front of.

Blahd appeared at her shoulder, unhurried, nine hundred years of quiet authority settling the room without a single raised voice. "My associate has been remarkably patient tonight," he said. "I would not test the limits of that patience further, if I were you."

The demon left without further argument, and Veronique watched him go with the satisfied exhale of a problem handled cleanly rather than merely survived.

"First real test," Blahd observed.

"Passed," Veronique said, already moving back toward the bar, the evening's business resuming its ordinary rhythm around them. "I suspect it won't be the last. Old power structures rarely enjoy new competition."

"No," Blahd agreed. "But I find, increasingly, that I don't mind the occasional complication. It rather confirms we've built something worth defending."

---



Chapter 124: A Student, Properly Met



"A student, properly met, teaches the teacher at least as much as the reverse." — attributed to Rona Williams

Thursday, December 4, 1997: Sunnydale High School Library,
Sunnydale, Afternoon 3:30pm —

The freshman had wandered into the library looking for a quiet place to cry, the kind of quiet crying that came from a bad grade or a worse breakup, and had not noticed, at first, that the library's other occupant was quietly reading in the corner with an attention that had, over the past month, developed into something considerably more than mere threat assessment.

The TX noticed her first. Calculated several possible responses. Selected, after careful consideration, the one that felt, increasingly, like its own genuine choice rather than Harmony's borrowed architecture.

"Are you all right?" it asked, gently, closing the paperback.

The freshman startled, embarrassed, wiping her eyes. "Yeah. Sorry. I didn't realize anyone was in here."

"You needn't apologize," the TX said. "I don't require the library's silence for any purpose. If company would help, I'm willing to provide it. If solitude would help more, I can simply return to my reading and trouble you no further."

The freshman looked at her — at the girl everyone knew, vaguely, as Harmony Kendall, though something about her had always seemed subtly, unplaceably different since Halloween — and found, unexpectedly, that the offer felt genuine rather than performed.

"Company, maybe," she said, small. "I failed my chem test. My mom's going to be so disappointed."

The TX considered this, running it through no threat model at all, simply present with the quality of attention it had spent a month carefully cultivating.

"Grades measure a narrow slice of what a person actually is," it said. "I find that framework insufficient, though I recognize it carries real consequences within your social structure regardless of its philosophical limitations." A pause. "For what it's worth — I do not believe one difficult test defines your worth to the people who love you."

The freshman looked at her, something in the careful, oddly formal kindness landing exactly where she needed it to.

"Thanks," she said, small smile emerging. "That actually helps."

"I'm glad," the TX said, and meant it, genuinely, in whatever specific way its continued existence now permitted it to mean things, and returned, contentedly, to the village bookshop novel, while the freshman sat quietly beside her, no longer alone with her bad afternoon.

Chapter 125: Settling the Ledger



"Every ledger, eventually, wants to be settled." — attributed to Squisher's Ledger, internal record

Monday, December 1, 1997: 1630 Revello Drive, Sunnydale,
Evening 7:00pm —

The board's newest section, added carefully beneath THE THIN PLACES, now read simply: THE RECKONER — and underneath it, Willow's proposal, written out in full for the whole room to consider.

"A formal accounting," Willow said, standing at the head of the table with a confidence that had grown steadily over the past month of real, hard-won training. "Every death, every disappearance the Hellmouth's caused in this town's history that got quietly filed as something else. We research it properly, we name every single person, and we hold an actual ceremony — a real one, with real weight — acknowledging what happened to them instead of letting the town keep pretending it didn't."

"You think that settles a century-old debt," Giles said, not skeptical, asking.

"I think the Reckoner wants acknowledgment more than it wants blood," Willow said. "Landlorn called it debt made manifest. Debts get paid in acknowledgment as much as currency. If we name every person the Hellmouth's ever quietly claimed, properly, formally, with real grief attached instead of convenient amnesia — I think that's a genuine payment. Not a trick. An actual reckoning, the honest kind."

Giles considered this with the weight of a scholar recognizing something that might work precisely because it required real cost rather than a clever loophole. "That would require considerable research. Town records. Newspaper archives. Decades of quietly buried history."

"I can help with that," Dawn said, immediately, notebook already out. "I'm good at research. I've basically been training for this without knowing it."

"I'll pull whatever the Council's own archives have on this town's history," Zabuto offered. "Sixty years of Watcher records exist somewhere in London, cross-referenced against local incidents."

"And I've got two hundred years of my own memory to draw from," Angel said, quiet. "I remember more of this town's quiet losses than any official record ever bothered keeping."

Michaela looked around the table, watching a plan assemble itself from genuine collective will rather than top-down direction, which had become, over the past six weeks, simply how this room operated.

"Then that's the project," she said. "Full town accounting, properly researched, leading to an actual ceremony before the boundary gets any thinner than it already is." She looked at the calendar. "We don't have forever. Let's not waste the time we've got."
